    Spectral Micro Manual

    Spectral Micro Manual¶

    Document number: SR-5002

    Firmware version: 110

    Revision: 2026.C

    Published: 2026-11-13

    Applies to: Spectral Micro driver PCB v2.0, firmware 110

    Original instructions

    Quick start About this manual

    Source Robotics d.o.o. publishes this document for the Spectral Micro BLDC driver. It is revised as the product and its firmware change; the document number, firmware version, revision and date above identify the version you are reading, and the document history on the About page says what changed. Take care that the firmware version named under Applies to matches what #Info reports on your driver: the version selector in the header switches between firmware releases. The text and the images are licensed CC BY-SA 4.0, the code examples MIT.

    Drag to turn the board, scroll to zoom. The model is the board's STEP file from the repository, the PCB and everything mounted on it.

    • Quick start

      Four short parts from the box to a calibrated motor that follows commands, through Motor GUI or the terminal, with a link into the manual at every step.

    • The manual

      Safety, Technical data, Mounting and wiring, First setup, Operation, Firmware, Troubleshooting.

    • Motor GUI

      The usual way to set up and test: the tool and working in it — connect, calibrate, tune with live plots, record telemetry, update the firmware.

    • API reference

      Every CAN message and UART command, the defaults, the errors and the Python library, read from the firmware.

    • Learn

      Guides with code, from a mobile robot to a quadruped leg, the bootloader, and the theory corner on FOC, the cascade and impedance control.

    Before you start

    Quick start

    Quick start¶

    From the box to a calibrated motor that follows commands, in four parts. The quickest way through is Motor GUI in the browser; every step also shows the terminal commands.

    Read first

    Reversed supply polarity, a daisy-chain cable the wrong way round, or 5 V on the UART port destroys the driver. The motor turns during calibration. The Safety chapter is two minutes long.

    Before you start¶

    You start from the box: a driver, a motor and the parts below. The wiring needs a multimeter and a soldering iron.

    Nothing else is assumed: no experience with motor drivers, a terminal or a program.

    The four parts¶

    Part What you do What you end with
    1. Fit the motor Fit the magnet, mount the board behind the motor, solder the three phases A driver on its motor, wired
    2. Connect Connect the supply and the PC, open Motor GUI or a terminal A driver that talks to your PC
    3. Calibrate Restore the defaults, calibrate, set the current limit, save A calibrated driver that keeps its settings
    4. First moves Turn the motor at a speed, move it to a position, stop it A motor that follows commands

    Each step links the page that explains it. Follow the link when a step is not clear or something fails.

    What you need¶

    Where to get it
    Spectral Micro driver single driver or the starter kit, which has everything below except the motor, the bracket and the supply
    A brushless motor with a phase resistance of 2 to 24 Ω and 2.8 A or less: a gimbal motor, from the 2804 class to the 8108 class the tested motors are safe choices
    The encoder magnet, diametrically magnetised; the stock one is 6 × 2.5 mm, another thickness is fine if it gives the 1 mm gap magnet
    A bracket that holds the board behind the motor, and M3 screws the example bracket to adapt and print; a NEMA 17-format motor needs only a spacer
    USB-to-serial adapter, 3.3 V, and its cable serial adapter, UART cable
    Power cable power cable
    A 12 to 29 V supply, 24 V recommended a bench supply with a current limit is the safest start
    For CAN later: CANvas USB-to-CAN adapter and a CAN cable CAN adapter, CAN cable
    Optional: 100 kΩ NTC thermistor for motor temperature protection thermistor
    Software: Motor GUI in Chrome or Edge, or a serial terminal motorgui.com; PuTTY, the PlatformIO monitor or the Arduino serial monitor

    More: What you need.

    1. Fit the motor¶

    The driver goes behind the motor, over a magnet on the end of the motor shaft.

    1. Fit the magnet¶

    1. Press the magnet onto the end of the motor shaft, centred.
    2. Glue it, so that it cannot turn on the shaft.

    The magnet must end up about 1 mm above the encoder chip in the middle of the board.

    A magnet that slips is the usual cause of a calibration that "succeeds" and a motor that then runs badly.

    Check

    The magnet sits centred on the shaft, and once the glue has set it does not move when you push it with a finger. If it does: The encoder magnet.

    More: The encoder magnet.

    2. Mount the board¶

    1. Bolt the bracket to the bottom of the motor.

    2. Bolt the board to the bracket, with the encoder chip (the bottom of the board) towards the magnet.

    3. Check the gap between the magnet and the encoder chip: 1 mm, not touching.

    A NEMA 17-format motor takes the board on a spacer with its own rear screws.

    Check

    The board sits parallel to the motor, with about 1 mm between the magnet and the encoder chip, and the rotor turns by hand without the magnet touching the chip. If not: Mounting on the motor.

    More: Mounting on the motor.

    3. Wire the motor¶

    Solder the motor's three wires to U, V and W. The order does not matter: calibration detects it.

    Check

    With the driver unpowered, a multimeter shows about the same resistance between U and V, V and W, and W and U, and no strand bridges two groups. If not: Wiring the motor.

    More: Wiring the motor.

    2. Connect¶

    The supply powers the motor, the serial adapter connects the PC.

    1. Connect power and the PC¶

    1. Connect the supply to a power connector: DC+ to positive, DC− to negative. Check twice.

    2. Connect the serial adapter to the UART connector with the UART cable: TX to RX, RX to TX. 3.3 V only.

    3. Power on.

    The LED blinks for about two seconds while the bootloader waits for a host. Then the firmware starts, and the LED stays on steadily: the driver is not calibrated yet, which it reports as an error state.

    The serial adapter alone

    The serial adapter alone powers the driver's logic: it answers over UART without the supply, but reports a Vbus error and will not calibrate or move.

    More: Power and the CAN daisy chain · Connectors and pinout.

    2. Open Motor GUI or a terminal¶

    1. Open motorgui.com in Chrome or Edge.
    2. Choose the serial connection.
    3. Press Connect, and choose the adapter's port.

    The driver's identity and state appear: hardware and firmware version, serial number, Not calibrated, No errors.

    If nothing connects: the port, the cable's TX/RX, the browser (Web Serial needs a Chromium browser).

    More: Motor GUI.

    1. Open a terminal on the adapter's port: 256000 baud, line ending CR+LF.
    2. Type #Info.

    The driver prints its hardware and firmware version, its CAN ID, whether it is calibrated, and the motor's parameters.

    More: The serial terminal.

    Check

    The driver's identity and live state appear before you change settings. A missing connection is covered in Motor GUI or Troubleshooting.

    3. Calibrate¶

    The driver learns your motor. There is nothing to enter: calibration measures the motor's resistance and inductance and finds its pole pairs and phase order by itself.

    1. Restore the defaults¶

    First the factory defaults, so that calibration does not start from another motor's values or from junk in the EEPROM.

    Press Default in the settings.

    #Default
    What else #Default resets

    It also sets the hardware version and batch date to factory values; from firmware 109 it keeps the CAN node ID.

    2. Calibrate¶

    We recommend a Motor GUI preset

    In Motor GUI's Presets tab, apply Generic motor, BLDC motor on Spectral Micro (soft) before you calibrate. It writes a low current limit and soft loops and leaves the resistance, the inductance and the pole pairs to the calibration, which measures them, so the motor calibrates safely. Raise the limits and the gains from there (Current limit and loop tuning). More: Motor GUI.

    1. Fix the motor to the bench, with nothing on the shaft.
    2. Set the supply to 24 V.

    3. Start the calibration:

      Start the calibration. The GUI switches to Calibration mode and shows the driver's log step by step.

      #Cal

    4. Keep the shaft free until the end, about a minute more after the log below.

    The LED flashes, the motor clicks, then turns in both directions, and the driver reports each step: the magnet, the supply, the resistance and inductance, the pole pairs, the phase order, the motor constants, and finally

    Calibration success!
Next, by itself: the encoder table, then anti-cogging (about a minute; keep the shaft free). Everything is saved when they finish (#Encal state, #Cogcal state)
Going to idle!

    The driver then goes on to measure the encoder table and the anti-cogging, and saves everything when they succeed.

    A failed step names itself:

    • Magnet test: Failed is the magnet;
    • Resistance test: Failed means a phase is not connected or the motor is outside the 2 to 24 Ω range;
    • Open loop: Failed means the rotor did not follow the field: check the three wires and that the shaft is free;
    • Commutation/alignment check failed! means the motor did not respond: check the three wires, the supply, and that the magnet does not slip.

    Check

    The calibration log ends with Calibration success!. If it does not, use the failed step in Calibration troubleshooting; do not treat a stopped motor as success.

    More: Calibration, with the whole log and every message.

    3. Set the current limit and save¶

    The current limit is the current the motor may draw, in mA; it is also the grip of the motor against a load.

    From firmware 109 calibration puts back the limit that was in force (107 and older left 1600 mA), so check it now, before you save. 1000 mA suits the gimbal motors in the tested list; a small 2804 is better at 500 mA, a motor rated for more can go up to 2800 mA.

    Set the current limit in the limits, then Save (the GUI warns about unsaved changes until you do).

    #Ilim 1000
#Save

    Without saving, the calibration is gone at the next power cycle. The LED now shows three short flashes and a pause: normal operation.

    Check

    After Save, Motor GUI no longer marks unsaved changes, and after a power cycle #Info prints Calibrated. Saving explains which settings are kept.

    More: Current limit and loop tuning · Saving the configuration.

    4. First moves¶

    The motor is calibrated and saved. Now it moves on command.

    1. Move it¶

    Use the jog controls:

    • Jog velocity turns the motor at a speed;
    • Jog position moves it to a position;
    • Jog current applies a torque;
    • Send setpoint sends a value in the current control mode.

    The plots show position, speed and current while it moves.

    #V 16384        one revolution per second
#V 0
#P 0            back to where it started
#P 16384        one revolution forward
#Idle

    Position is in encoder ticks, 16384 per revolution; speed in ticks per second; current in mA. If the motor hums or whines, lower the current limit or tune the loops.

    Check

    The position and speed feedback follow the request, and Idle stops driving the motor. A motor that does not follow needs Troubleshooting before a load is attached.

    More: Control modes · Current limit and loop tuning.

    2. Where to go next¶

    You want to Read
    get to know Motor GUI: the screen, the plots, recordings, several motors Motor GUI, Working in Motor GUI
    control the driver from a program over CAN Over CAN, then With Python
    put several drivers on one bus give each a node ID and terminate the ends: Power and the CAN daisy chain
    protect the motor from overheating Thermistor
    update the firmware Firmware
    look up a message or a command API reference
    build something: a wheeled robot, a leg, a compliant arm Guides
    understand why it does not work Errors and recovery, Troubleshooting

    About this manual¶

    Scope and audience¶

    The Spectral Micro Manual covers the Spectral Micro BLDC driver from unpacking to a tuned motor on a CAN bus: what the driver is, how to mount it on a motor and wire it, the first setup with Motor GUI or the serial terminal, calibration and tuning, how the driver is controlled over UART and CAN or from Python, and how its firmware is updated. The API reference lists every CAN message and UART command of the firmware release named on the landing page. The Guides are worked examples.

    It is written for someone who builds a robot or a machine with the Spectral Micro: a maker, a student or an engineer who can solder three motor wires and open a serial terminal. It does not assume any knowledge of field-oriented control; the Theory corner in the appendix explains the concepts the control modes rely on.

    Read the Safety chapter before you power anything. The Quick start is the whole first setup, in four short parts; the chapters are in the order you need them and go deeper.

    Finding your way¶

    The header has four tabs. Spectral Micro Manual is the document you read from the start: the quick start, then safety, technical data, mounting, the first setup, operation and firmware updates, in numbered chapters. Motor GUI, the tool in the browser, is the usual way to do what the chapters describe; each procedure shows it first and the terminal command beside it. API reference lists every CAN message, every UART command and the Python library's calls for the firmware release this site describes, with the defaults and the error flags, and is where you look things up. Guides are worked examples with code. Support says where to get help and how to report a problem.

    The whole site describes one firmware release. The dropdown next to the site name switches between releases, and a page from an older release says so in a bar at the top. The repository card on the right of the header opens the firmware repository; every page ends with a link to edit it and one to report a problem with it.

    Warnings and notes¶

    Three kinds of highlighted text appear in this documentation. Read every Danger and Notice before you act on the step it belongs to.

    Note

    Points out something that is easy to miss or that changes what follows: a default, a limit, a side effect.

    Notice

    The robot, the tool, or data on the control box can be damaged or lost if this is ignored. Nobody is at risk.

    Danger

    Someone can be injured if this is ignored. Stop and read the whole box before acting on the step it belongs to.

    Related documents¶

    Document Number Covers

    A document marked with a firmware version describes that firmware release, here 110; the version selector in the header switches between releases. The compatibility table says which firmware, Python library and hardware revisions work together.

    The STEPFOC stepper driver has similar hardware and firmware and is run the same way; its documentation is at docs.source-robotics.com/STEPFOC-docs. The SSG-48 gripper, which is driven by a Spectral Micro, is documented at docs.source-robotics.com/SSG48-gripper-docs.

    Document history¶

    The Spectral Micro Manual describes one firmware release; one revision is kept per release, and the version selector in the header switches between them. The earlier version of this site, for firmware 101, is kept as version 101.

    The text and the images of this documentation are licensed under CC BY-SA 4.0, the code examples under the MIT licence. The Source Robotics name, the product names and the logos are not covered by either.

    Chapters

    1. Overview

    Overview¶

    What the Spectral Micro is and what it is made of, how it relates to the STEPFOC stepper driver that is built on it, and what you need around it to get a motor turning.

    In this chapter¶

    • The driver at a glance
    • Spectral Micro and STEPFOC
    • What you need

    The driver at a glance¶

    The Spectral Micro is a field-oriented controller for small three-phase brushless motors: gimbal motors and other low-current motors with a phase resistance of a few ohms. It mounts behind the motor, reads the rotor position from a magnet on the motor shaft, and drives the three phases with sinusoidal currents placed from that position, the way a servo drive does.

    The motor can be commanded in position, speed, current (torque) or impedance, holds a position at standstill, reports where it is, and runs quietly and efficiently. It is made for robot joints, legs, arms and grippers.

    What is on the board¶

    Part What it does
    STM32F103CB microcontroller runs the control loops at 5 kHz and the CAN and UART interfaces
    14-bit magnetic encoder (MT6816) reads the position of the magnet on the motor shaft: 16384 ticks per revolution
    Three-phase driver stage with inline current sensing drives the phases with up to 2.8 A and measures the phase currents
    16 Kbit EEPROM keeps the configuration and the calibration over a power cycle
    Two CAN connectors and two power connectors daisy-chain several drivers on one bus and one supply
    CAN termination switch the 120 Ω resistor the first and the last node need
    UART connector (3.3 V) the serial terminal: every setting, calibration, tuning
    Thermistor pads an NTC in the motor windings for temperature protection
    PIN header a spare pin for your own use: an analog input or a digital input/output
    HALL header pads for hall sensors; not implemented in the firmware
    JTAG pads (1.27 mm) the programming adapter, for installing the bootloader or for development
    Status LED normal operation, calibration or error, see below

    The connectors and their pinout are in Connectors and pinout.

    What the firmware does¶

    The firmware closes three nested loops on the motor: a current (torque) loop, a velocity loop around it and a position loop around that, plus an impedance (PD) loop that makes the motor behave like a spring and damper.

    A command picks the loop: a current setpoint, a speed, a position or a PD setpoint. An open-loop mode and a voltage-torque mode exist beside them; Control modes describes each.

    Calibration measures the motor: its phase resistance and inductance, its pole pairs and the order of its phases, the angle between the encoder and the rotor field, and its torque and velocity constants. Nothing about the motor has to be typed in; see Calibration.

    The driver protects itself and the motor: it stops on a supply voltage outside the limits, on an encoder fault, on an over-speed, on a motor over-temperature (with the thermistor), on a hardware fault of the driver stage, on an E-stop command and on a watchdog that runs out. The status LED and #Error tell which; see Errors and recovery.

    How it is controlled¶

    • UART, a 3.3 V serial port at 256000 baud: human-readable commands such as #P 16384, and the only way to run the calibration. See Over UART.
    • CAN, 1 Mbit/s, up to 14 drivers on one bus: compact frames for real-time control from a PC with the CANvas adapter, a microcontroller or a robot control box. See Over CAN.
    • Python, the Spectral-BLDC library on top of CAN. See With Python.
    • Motor GUI, in the browser, over serial or CAN: the usual tool for setup, testing and tuning. See Motor GUI.

    The status LED¶

    Pattern Meaning
    three short flashes and a pause normal operation: no error, calibrated
    fast flashing, about 10 times a second the calibration routine is running
    on, not flashing error mode: at least one error is active, or the motor is not calibrated

    The LED can be turned off with #LED 0.

    Normal operation Calibration Error mode

    Spectral Micro and STEPFOC¶

    The Spectral Micro and the STEPFOC stepper driver are sister drivers: similar hardware and similar firmware, each made for its kind of motor. The Spectral Micro drives three-phase brushless motors; STEPFOC drives two-phase stepper motors.

    They are not the same board and do not run the same firmware, but they are controlled and run practically the same way: the same CAN and UART commands, the same Python library, Motor GUI and bootloader.

    What this means in practice:

    • The commands are practically the same. The CAN messages and UART commands of the API reference exist on both drivers, with the same IDs, names and layouts; STEPFOC adds a few that only make sense on a stepper (homing against a sensor, open-loop stepping). A program written for one runs on the other. The idle behaviour is the exception over UART: the Spectral Micro sets it with #Brake 0 or #Brake 1 and coasts by default, STEPFOC has the actions #Brake and #Coast. Send_Settings over CAN is the same on both.
    • The firmware releases go in step, and from 109 share their numbers. From release 109 the two drivers are released together under the same version number; the Spectral Micro went from 107 straight to 109, so there is no Spectral Micro 108. Before, the numbers did not always match (Spectral Micro 107 went with STEPFOC 108); the compatibility table lists the pairs. Some defaults differ, each measured on its own driver: the direct PLL speed estimate at 100 Hz and 234 µs delay compensation here, 60 Hz and 166 µs on STEPFOC.
    • Calibration differs. The Spectral Micro measures the motor's resistance and inductance and finds its pole pairs; STEPFOC cannot, so there the three values are entered from the motor's datasheet first. See Calibration.
    • The motor terminals differ. U, V and W for the three phases, instead of A+, A−, B+, B− for the two coils of a stepper.
    • The grippers differ. The SSG-48 gripper is driven by a Spectral Micro, the MSG gripper by STEPFOC; both use the driver's gripper mode.

    The guides under the Guides tab were written with the Spectral Micro; the code in them runs unchanged on STEPFOC.

    What you need¶

    This page lists what a Spectral Micro setup is made of, what comes with the driver, and what you buy or make yourself.

    The driver¶

    The Spectral Micro is sold as a single driver or as a starter kit in the Source Robotics shop:

    • Spectral Micro BLDC controller
    • Spectral Micro starter kit, which adds the adapters, the magnet, the cables and a thermistor listed below

    Every driver is end-of-line tested and ships with the current firmware release and the bootloader already installed, so a driver works out of the box with a serial terminal, the Python library or Motor GUI. The firmware can be updated later without a programming adapter; see Firmware.

    A brushless motor¶

    The Spectral Micro drives three-phase brushless motors with a phase resistance of about 2 to 24 Ω and a current of 2.8 A or less: gimbal motors from the small 2804 class up to the 8108 class, and other brushless motors of similar resistance, such as the NEMA 17-format 42BLS40. The tested motors are known to work. The motor needs a place for the encoder magnet on the axis of its shaft, on the side where the board is mounted.

    Low-resistance motors

    The calibration routine's default voltages are made for motors of 2 Ω and more. A motor with a much lower resistance, such as a drone motor, draws a destructive current at those voltages. Do not calibrate one without lowering the calibration voltages (#Resv, #Openv, #Vp) first. The current limit does not protect it: calibration sets its own, up to 1600 mA.

    The encoder magnet¶

    A diametrically magnetised 6 × 2.5 mm neodymium magnet on the end of the motor shaft, about 1 mm above the encoder chip in the middle of the board: 6 × 2.5 mm diametrical magnet. An axially magnetised magnet, or one that wobbles, makes calibration fail or report bad positions. The 6 × 2.5 mm size is the stock one, not a requirement: another thickness is a way to set the 1 mm gap when a shaft or a bracket leaves the wrong distance. The encoder magnet says how to fit it.

    A mounting bracket¶

    The board does not bolt to a gimbal motor directly: a bracket holds it behind the motor, with the encoder chip on the shaft's axis and 1 mm from the magnet. The firmware repository has an example bracket to adapt to your motor; see Mounting on the motor. A motor with the NEMA 17 hole pattern, such as the 42BLS40, takes the board on a spacer with its own screws.

    Adapters and cables¶

    Part Needed for Shop
    USB-to-serial adapter (3.3 V) the serial terminal: calibration, tuning, any # command Serial adapter
    CANvas USB-to-CAN adapter CAN control from a PC, the Python library, Motor GUI over CAN CAN adapter
    CAN cable and power cable daisy-chaining several drivers CAN cable, power cable
    100 kΩ NTC thermistor motor temperature protection (optional but recommended) Thermistor
    JTAG programming adapter only for installing the bootloader on an older board, or for firmware development Programming adapter

    Cable orientation

    The daisy-chain cables for CAN and power are keyed by their orientation, not by their connector. A cable plugged in the wrong way round destroys the driver. Use the cables from the shop or follow Power and the CAN daisy chain exactly when you make your own.

    A power supply¶

    12 to 29 V DC, 24 V recommended; see Ratings and limits. Calibrate at 24 V: the resistance, inductance and motor constants come out wrong at a lower voltage. A bench supply with a current limit is the safest way to start.

    Software¶

    • Motor GUI, in the browser, over the serial adapter or the CAN adapter: the usual tool for setup, calibration, tuning, plots and firmware updates; see Motor GUI.
    • Or a serial terminal: PuTTY, the PlatformIO monitor in VS Code, or the Arduino serial monitor; see The serial terminal.
    • The Python library Spectral-BLDC for CAN control from your own programs; see With Python.

    Safety¶

    The Spectral Micro is a bare circuit board that drives a motor from a supply of up to 29 V. Read this chapter before you connect anything; the few rules here are the ones that destroy a driver, a motor or a finger when they are ignored.

    Electrical¶

    Supply polarity

    Reversing the polarity on DC+ and DC− destroys the driver at once. Check the polarity of every power cable before you plug it in, every time.

    Cable orientation on the daisy chain

    The CAN and power cables that link one driver to the next are keyed by their orientation, not by their connector: a cable plugged in the wrong way round puts the supply on the wrong pins and destroys the driver. Use the cables from the Source Robotics shop, or make yours exactly as shown in Power and the CAN daisy chain.

    UART is 3.3 V

    The UART port runs at 3.3 V. A 5 V serial adapter destroys the board. Use a 3.3 V adapter, such as the one from the shop.

    Supply voltage

    12 to 29 V DC. The driver raises a Vbus error below 9 V and above #Vmax, 29 V after #Default. 29 V is also the hardware limit: do not connect a supply that can rise above it, and allow for the voltage a decelerating motor pushes back into the supply. A bench supply with a current limit is the safest way to start.

    Do not disconnect a running motor

    Never unplug a motor, or a power cable in the daisy chain, while the driver is powered. The inductive kick of the windings and the arcing at the connector damage the driver stage.

    Static electricity

    Static electricity damages the microcontroller, the encoder chip and the driver stage. Unplug the supply and touch earthed metal before you handle the board, and hold it by its edges, away from the chips and the pads.

    Mechanical¶

    Moving parts

    A calibrating or running motor turns without warning, in both directions, and a motor in position mode pushes back hard against a hand. Keep fingers, hair and cables away from the shaft and whatever is mounted on it, fix the motor to the bench before the first test, and test without a load on the shaft.

    A powered motor can move with nobody at it

    The driver moves its motor whenever a command arrives, over UART or from any host on the CAN bus, and keeps following the last one. A speed command goes on turning the motor after the program that sent it has crashed or its cable has come out, unless the watchdog is set (Stopping the motor). Before you reach for the motor or the mechanism it drives, put the driver in idle (#Idle, Send_Idle) or switch the supply off.

    The encoder magnet

    The magnet sits about 1 mm above the encoder chip. A magnet that touches the chip, or sits far too close, can damage the microcontroller; a magnet too far away gives a wrong position. Mount it as described in The encoder magnet.

    Hot motor and driver

    A motor running at its rated current gets hot enough to burn, and so do the driver's chips and the board around them. Switch off and let both cool for a few minutes before you touch them. Set the current limit for your motor (Current limit and loop tuning), use the thermistor, and give the driver airflow when it runs near 2.8 A. #Ilim is clamped to 2800 mA, and from firmware 109 so are the current limit of Send_Limits over CAN and the current of a gripper move. The motor's rating is usually lower: a host must keep them within it.

    Stopping the motor¶

    • Send_ESTOP over CAN, or #Idle over UART, stops the motor at once; the E-stop error stays until it is cleared.
    • Cutting the supply stops the motor as well; the motor then holds nothing, so a vertical axis drops.
    • The watchdog (Send_Watchdog) stops the motor by itself when the host stops talking. From firmware 109 its action can instead hold the position or let a joint down at a limited speed, for a set time at most, then brake or coast (The watchdog): while that runs the motor is still driven.

    Nothing on the driver is a certified safety function. A machine that can hurt someone needs an emergency stop that cuts the motor supply, independent of the driver.

    Firmware updates and programming¶

    When you connect a programming adapter to the JTAG connector, disconnect the motor supply and the UART adapter first. Updating over the bootloader needs no adapter; see Firmware.

    Liability¶

    The Spectral Micro is sold as a component for people who build their own machines. Its hardware designs, firmware and this documentation are under development and may contain errors. Whoever builds a machine with it is responsible for its safe design, assembly and operation.

    3. Technical data

    Technical data¶

    The numbers: ratings and limits, the connectors with their pinout, the dimensions, and the motors that have been tested with the driver.

    In this chapter¶

    • Ratings and limits
    • Connectors and pinout
    • Dimensions and CAD
    • Tested motors

    Ratings and limits¶

    Electrical¶

    Value
    Supply voltage 12 to 29 V DC, 24 V typical; 10 V absolute minimum
    Vbus error limits (firmware) below 9 V or above #Vmax, 29 V after #Default
    Phase current, continuous 1.5 A typical
    Phase current, maximum 2.8 A (#Ilim and, from firmware 109, Send_Limits over CAN are clamped to 2800 mA); needs good cooling and short bursts
    Power, maximum 80 W
    Power, idle 0.3 W
    PWM frequency 25 kHz
    Control loop frequency 5 kHz
    Electrical frequency, maximum 500 Hz
    Current sensing inline, on two phases
    Encoder 14-bit magnetic, 16384 ticks per revolution, diametrical magnet 1 mm above the chip
    UART 3.3 V, 256000 baud by default (1 000 000 tested)
    CAN CAN 2.0, 1 Mbit/s, 11-bit identifiers, node IDs 0 to 13 (14 and 15 are reserved by the bootloader), 120 Ω termination by switch
    Thermistor input 100 kΩ NTC, 54.9 kΩ pull-down
    PIN analog input or general-purpose input/output, 3.3 V
    Driver stage protection hardware overcurrent (trips at 3 to 5 A), undervoltage lockout (6.3 to 8 V) and thermal shutdown (150 to 180 °C); shown as a driver error

    Reversing the polarity on DC+ and DC− destroys the board.

    Thermal and environmental¶

    Value
    Operating temperature of the board −20 to 130 °C
    Motor temperature limit (firmware, with the thermistor) 75 °C by default (#Tlim), −20 °C minimum

    Mechanical¶

    Value
    Dimensions 39 × 39 mm
    Mass 8 g
    Mounting four holes on the NEMA 17 pattern, 31 mm apart; the encoder chip in the middle of the board

    Firmware limits¶

    Value
    Position signed 24-bit over CAN (±8.4 million ticks, ±512 revolutions), 32-bit internally
    Speed signed 24-bit over CAN, encoder ticks per second
    Current setpoint signed 16-bit over CAN, mA
    Motors the calibration defaults are made for a phase resistance of 2 to 24 Ω
    Configuration kept in the EEPROM with #Save / Send_Save_config

    The defaults of every setting are listed in the Defaults reference.

    Connectors and pinout¶

    Connector Pins Type Cable
    Motor U, V, W solder pads, three per phase the motor's own leads, see Wiring the motor
    Power, two of them DC+, DC− JST XH, 2 pins Power cable; the second connector feeds the next driver
    CAN, two of them CANL, CANH JST ZH, 2 pins CAN cable; the second connector feeds the next driver
    UART 3V3, GND, TX, RX JST SH, 4 pins UART cable
    Thermistor TERM, 3V3 solder pads the 100 kΩ NTC from the shop or any 100 kΩ NTC, see Thermistor
    JTAG 3V3, GND, SWCLK, SWDIO 1.27 mm pads Programming adapter, pressed onto the pads
    PIN GND, PIN, 3V3 solder pads your own signal: an analog input or a digital input/output, 3.3 V
    HALL 5V, GND, HALL1, HALL2, HALL3 solder pads hall sensors: not implemented in the firmware, see the note below

    Hall sensors are not implemented

    The HALL header and the twelve resistor pads above it are on the board for hall-sensor commutation, but the Spectral firmware does not implement it: the magnetic encoder is the only position source. Leave the header unpopulated.

    Polarity and orientation

    DC+ to the positive supply, DC− to the negative; a cable the wrong way round destroys the driver. The daisy-chain cables have an orientation too: see Power and the CAN daisy chain.

    Note

    • UART is TX to RX and RX to TX between the driver and the adapter.
    • The UART port and the PIN header are 3.3 V. 5 V on either destroys the board.
    • The thermistor pin has a 54.9 kΩ pull-down; the firmware's temperature table is for a 100 kΩ NTC.
    • The CAN termination switch is next to the CAN connectors; ON connects the 120 Ω resistor.
    • The encoder chip sits on the bottom of the board, in the middle: that side faces the magnet.

    Dimensions and CAD¶

    The board is 39 × 39 mm with four mounting holes on the NEMA 17 pattern (31 mm apart). The encoder chip is exactly in the middle of the board, so a bracket that centres the board on the motor shaft puts the chip on the shaft's axis.

    Drag to turn the board, scroll to zoom. The model is the board's STEP file from the repository, the PCB and everything mounted on it.

    The STEP files of the board and of an example mounting bracket are in the firmware repository, in STEP files: Spectral_micro_STEP.STEP is the board, Spectral_micro_bracket.STEP the bracket to adapt to your motor (Mounting on the motor).

    Downloads and files lists everything that can be downloaded.

    Tested motors¶

    The Spectral Micro drives three-phase brushless motors with a phase resistance of about 2 to 24 Ω: the calibration routine's default voltages are made for that range. The motors below have been run on it. Calibration measures each motor's resistance, inductance and pole pairs, so nothing has to be looked up before the first run.

    Motor Kind Where
    BGM8108 gimbal motor, 8108 class AliExpress
    GM8112 gimbal motor, 8112 class AliExpress
    GM7008H gimbal motor, 7008 class AliExpress
    GM6008H gimbal motor, 6008 class AliExpress
    GBM5208 gimbal motor, 5208 class AliExpress
    GM5208 gimbal motor, 5208 class AliExpress
    GBM5010 gimbal motor, 5010 class AliExpress
    GB4114 gimbal motor, 4114 class AliExpress
    2804 gimbal motor, 2804 class AliExpress
    2804 (RCTimer) gimbal motor, 2804 class AliExpress
    2804 100KV gimbal motor, 2804 class, 100 KV AliExpress
    42BLS40-24-01 NEMA 17-format brushless motor, 24 V, 26 W, 1.8 A, 219 g Stepperonline

    Before the first test, set the current limit for the motor with #Ilim (firmware 107 and older leave 1600 mA after a calibration; from 109 calibration puts your limit back). The factory default of 1000 mA suits the gimbal motors above; a motor rated for more can go up to the driver's 2800 mA. See Current limit and loop tuning.

    Choosing a motor

    A lower phase resistance and inductance give a higher speed at a given supply voltage; the velocity constant KV that calibration finds tells how fast the motor can turn: roughly 0.8 × Vbus × KV revolutions per minute. A high pole-pair count (the large gimbal motors have 11 to 14) gives smooth torque at low speed and limits the top speed through the driver's maximum electrical frequency. Motors with visibly uneven windings, a resistance that differs between the phases or an uneven back-EMF calibrate badly and run rough.

    Drone motors

    Motors with a phase resistance well under 1 Ω draw a destructive current at the calibration routine's default voltages. The Spectral Micro is not made for them.

    4. Mounting and wiring

    Mounting and wiring¶

    How the driver goes onto the motor and what gets connected to it: the encoder magnet on the shaft, the board on a bracket behind the motor, the three phases to the motor terminals, the supply and the CAN bus, and the thermistor. Do the pages in order; the magnet first, because it is the one thing you cannot check from the outside later.

    In this chapter¶

    • The encoder magnet
    • Mounting on the motor
    • Wiring the motor
    • Power and the CAN daisy chain
    • Thermistor

    When all of it is done, continue with First setup.

    The encoder magnet¶

    The encoder reads a diametrically magnetised magnet on the end of the motor shaft. The magnet must sit centred on the shaft's axis, parallel to the board, about 1 mm above the encoder chip, and it must not move. Every calibration problem that is not a wiring problem comes from this magnet.

    The magnet¶

    A neodymium disc, diametrically magnetised (north and south across the diameter, not through the thickness). The shop's 6 × 2.5 mm diametrical magnet is the stock size. An axially magnetised magnet looks the same and does not work: the magnet test may even pass, and the position is wrong.

    The size is not fixed

    6 × 2.5 mm is the stock size, not a requirement. Any diametrically magnetised disc of about 6 mm diameter works, and the thickness is a way to set the gap: a thicker magnet brings its face closer to the encoder, a thinner one farther, so with a shaft or a bracket that leaves the wrong distance you can pick the thickness that gives 1 mm instead of changing the bracket. What matters is the 1 mm from the magnet's face to the chip, the magnet centred on the shaft, and the diametrical magnetisation.

    Fitting it¶

    1. The magnet goes on the end of the shaft that faces the board. On most gimbal motors the shaft ends at the bottom of the motor, flush with the housing or a little proud of it, and the board is mounted on that side (Mounting on the motor). On a motor whose shaft ends inside the housing, a short holder pressed into the shaft's end carries the magnet out to where the board can see it.
    2. Press the magnet on, centred on the shaft's axis. A magnet that sits off-centre wobbles its field as the shaft turns and gives a position error that repeats every revolution.
    3. Fix it with a drop of glue. A magnet that can turn on the shaft is the classic cause of a calibration that reports success and a motor that then runs badly: the magnet slipped between calibration and use.
    4. Check the height after the board is mounted: 1 mm, the magnet not touching the chip. A magnet that touches the chip can destroy the microcontroller; a magnet 2 mm away or more gives a weak signal.

    Check

    The magnet sits centred on the shaft, and once the glue has set it does not move when you push it with a finger.

    Checking it¶

    With the driver powered and the serial terminal open, #Magnet answers Magnet Good when the chip sees enough field, Magnet Bad when it does not. The calibration routine runs the same test first.

    What the magnet test checks

    Only that a magnet is close enough to the encoder. It passes with a magnet that is not glued, sits off-centre or can slip on the shaft, and fails only when there is no magnet or it is too far away. The 1 mm gap, the centring and the glue are yours to check.

    Mounting on the motor¶

    The board sits behind the motor, with the encoder chip, in the middle of the board, on the axis of the shaft and 1 mm from the magnet. The board's four holes are on the NEMA 17 pattern, 31 mm apart.

    Gimbal motors¶

    A gimbal motor has no standard mounting for a board; a bracket holds the board at the right distance. The firmware repository has an example to adapt to your motor, Spectral_micro_bracket.STEP in STEP files: change its hole pattern to the motor's bottom holes and its height so that the magnet ends up 1 mm from the chip.

    1. Bolt the bracket to the bottom of the motor. The magnet on the shaft's end sits in the bracket's centre hole.
    2. Bolt the board to the bracket with M3 screws, with the encoder chip (the bottom of the board, see Connectors and pinout) towards the magnet. Tighten the screws evenly; the board must be parallel to the magnet's face.
    3. Check the gap between the magnet and the encoder chip: about 1 mm, the magnet not touching it. If it is wrong, change the bracket's height or the magnet's thickness (The encoder magnet).

    Check

    The board sits parallel to the motor, with about 1 mm between the magnet and the encoder chip, and the rotor turns by hand without the magnet touching the chip. If the gap is wrong: The encoder magnet.

    Motors with the NEMA 17 pattern¶

    A brushless motor in the NEMA 17 format, such as the 42BLS40 in the tested motors, takes the board directly, with the motor's own rear screws: remove the four screws, put on a spacer that sets the magnet height, the board on the spacer with the chip towards the magnet, and longer M3 screws in. Check the gap.

    The board is bare

    Nothing on the board may touch the motor's housing or a metal bracket. Keep the bracket clear of the components on both sides of the board (the connectors on the bottom need room), and keep metal swarf and screws away from the board while you work. Touch earthed metal before you pick the board up and hold it by its edges: static electricity damages its chips.

    Wiring the motor¶

    A three-phase brushless motor has three wires. They go to the terminals U, V and W on the board (see Connectors and pinout), in any order: calibration finds the order of the phases. Firmware 105 and newer compensates it in software; firmware 101 asks you to swap two wires when it is reversed.

    Soldering¶

    1. Cut the leads to a comfortable length: long enough to reach the pads with the board mounted, short enough not to get into the rotor. With firmware older than 105, leave them long enough to swap two of them later.
    2. Tin the leads and solder each to one group of pads, U, V or W. The three pads of a group are one terminal.
    3. Make sure no strand bridges two groups and that nothing touches the rotor or the housing.

    A motor that comes with a connector keeps it: solder a short lead with the mating connector to each pad group.

    Check

    With the driver unpowered, a multimeter shows about the same resistance between U and V, V and W, and W and U, and no strand bridges two groups.

    Warning

    Never connect or disconnect the motor while the driver is powered.

    Phase order and direction¶

    Calibration spins the motor in open loop and watches the encoder, then checks that a current command moves the rotor the expected way.

    With firmware 105 and newer, the order of the phases is detected and compensated, so the motor calibrates identically with any wiring, and positive commands turn the motor the same way whatever the wiring: counter-clockwise seen from the encoder side of the board, see Control modes. To turn the other way, use negative setpoints.

    Firmware older than 105 does not compensate the order: calibration stops with Phase order is wrong! Switch motor phases and try again!. Swap any two of U, V and W and calibrate again.

    If the motor does not respond to the commutation check, calibration says so (Commutation/alignment check failed!). That means a phase is not connected, #Iqoffset is set too low or the magnet slipped, not that the wires are in the wrong order; see Calibration.

    Power and the CAN daisy chain¶

    Each driver has two power connectors (JST XH) and two CAN connectors (JST ZH). The second connector of each pair feeds the next driver, so a chain of drivers hangs on one supply cable and one CAN cable.

    Power¶

    • 12 to 29 V DC, 24 V recommended. DC+ to the positive supply, DC− to the negative; reversed polarity destroys the driver.
    • The power connectors are rated for 4 A. A chain of drivers shares that: with motors at 1.5 A each, plan for at most two or three drivers per chain that draw their full current at the same time (three or four with 10 Ω gimbal motors), and feed a longer chain from both ends or from several supplies.
    • A motor that decelerates pushes energy back into the supply. A lab supply or a battery absorbs it; a supply that cannot (some switch-mode supplies) sees its voltage rise, and the driver raises a Vbus error above #Vmax (29 V after #Default).

    The cable orientation¶

    Danger

    The daisy-chain cables are keyed by their orientation, not by the connector: the same 2-pin cable fits both ways, and the wrong way puts DC+ on DC−. Every cable must run as in the picture below, the same way at both ends. A wrong cable destroys the driver at the moment it is powered.

    The cables from the shop are made this way: CAN cable, power cable, and all of them under connectors and cables.

    CAN¶

    • CAN 2.0, 1 Mbit/s, 11-bit identifiers. Up to 14 drivers on one bus, each with its own node ID, 0 to 13 (0 by default; set it with #CANID or Send_CAN_ID and save). The firmware accepts 14 and 15 too, but the bootloader reserves them: a driver on one of those IDs cannot be updated over CAN.
    • Twisted-pair wire; keep the bus short at 1 Mbit/s (a few metres at most).
    • Termination: the first and the last node on the bus need a 120 Ω resistor between CAN H and CAN L. The switch next to the CAN connectors turns the driver's resistor on (ON). Drivers in the middle stay off. A bus with the right termination measures about 60 Ω between CAN H and CAN L with everything unpowered.
    • A PC joins the bus with the CANvas adapter, which has its own termination.

    Thermistor¶

    A 100 kΩ NTC thermistor in the motor lets the driver stop before the motor overheats. It is optional, and off by default; use it on any motor that runs near its current limit for long, and on a motor that holds a load at standstill, which heats one winding without any movement to show it.

    Without it, nothing protects the motor from overheating

    The driver limits the current, not the temperature. A motor that runs continuously, holds a load for long, or is set up badly (a current limit above its rating, loops that oscillate, a wrong resistance or inductance) can overheat and burn its windings without any warning. With the thermistor fitted and enabled, the driver stops first. The starter kit includes one.

    Fitting it¶

    Solder the thermistor's leads to the two thermistor pads, TERM and 3V3 (Connectors and pinout). Put its bead inside the motor's windings, where the heat is: on a gimbal motor the windings are open at the bottom and can be reached through one of the mounting holes or past the edge of the stator.

    Thermal cement holds the bead in place and conducts the heat; keep the leads clear of the rotor.

    Enabling it¶

    #Term 1
#Tlim 75
#Save

    #Temp then prints the temperature; with the thermistor disabled it prints Temp Off. Above the limit the driver raises a temperature error and stops; it clears with #Clear once the motor has cooled.

    Below −20 °C it raises the same error, so #Term 1 without a thermistor, or with a broken lead, stops the driver too. 75 °C is the default limit and a safe one for the enamel of most small motors; do not go higher unless the motor is rated for it.

    Over CAN, Send_Respond_Temperature returns the temperature, and the temperature error is one of the bits of Send_Respond_State_of_Errors.

    5. First setup

    First setup¶

    From a mounted and wired driver to a calibrated, tuned motor. Motor GUI in the browser is the usual tool for it; every step also gives the terminal command, for a terminal or for the console in Motor GUI.

    1. Motor GUI: the browser, the serial adapter, opening the port, what is on the screen.
    2. The serial terminal: the alternative — PuTTY, the PlatformIO monitor or the Arduino monitor, and the # commands.
    3. Calibration: the factory defaults, then the run that measures the motor and finds its pole pairs, phase order and constants.
    4. Encoder table and anti-cogging: two corrections the driver can measure on itself after a calibration, how to run them and how to check them.
    5. Current limit and loop tuning: the current limit for the motor, the loop gains if the defaults do not suit it, the speed estimate and the delay compensation.
    6. Saving the configuration: nothing survives a power cycle until it is saved; where it is kept.

    A driver that has been through these pages is ready for Operation. The Quick start is the same path in four short parts.

    Motor GUI¶

    Motor GUI is the usual way to set up, test and tune a driver: a tool in the browser that connects to one driver over the serial adapter or to a bus of drivers over the CANvas adapter, shows the state, moves the motor, runs the calibration, tunes the loops with live plots, records telemetry and updates the firmware. Nothing is installed. Everything it does, the driver also does from a terminal or from Python — the GUI reads and writes the same settings — so every procedure in this manual shows the GUI first and the terminal command beside it.

    The browser¶

    Motor GUI reaches the hardware through the browser's serial port access (Web Serial), which Chromium-based browsers have: Chrome, Edge, Brave, Opera. Firefox and Safari cannot open serial ports.

    Two ways to connect¶

    Adapter Use it for
    One driver over serial the 3.3 V USB-to-serial adapter on the driver's UART connector, 256000 baud the first setup and calibration (calibration runs over UART only), tuning on the bench
    A bus over CAN the CANvas adapter; it appears as a serial port and speaks slcan every driver on the bus by its node ID: testing a machine, updating firmware on all joints

    Opening the port¶

    1. Plug in the adapter and the motor supply. The adapter alone powers the driver's logic, enough to open the port and read the settings, but the driver reports a Vbus error and will not calibrate or move until the supply is on.
    2. Open motorgui.com, choose the connection kind, and Connect. The browser asks which port; pick the adapter's.
    3. The driver's identity appears: hardware version, firmware version, serial number, batch date, whether it is calibrated and activated, and the active errors. In a terminal, #Info, #SERNUM and #Error print the same.

    If the port is not offered, the browser is not Chromium-based, or another program holds the port (a terminal left open, PlatformIO's monitor), or the operating system does not give the browser the port:

    The adapter is COM and a number. Device Manager lists it under Ports (COM & LPT): the entry that disappears when you unplug the adapter is yours. An adapter that shows a warning sign there instead needs the driver of its USB chip (on most 3.3 V adapters a CP210x or a CH340) from the chip maker.

    The serial adapter is /dev/ttyUSB0 on most adapters, the CANvas adapter /dev/ttyACM0; ls /dev/ttyUSB* /dev/ttyACM* lists them. To open them without root, join the dialout group once, then log out and in again:

    sudo usermod -aG dialout $USER

    The serial adapter is /dev/cu.usbserial- and a few characters on most adapters, the CANvas adapter /dev/cu.usbmodem and a number; ls /dev/cu.* lists them. Use the cu. name, not the tty. one, in a terminal or a script.

    The browser's port picker lists the same adapters. If the port opens and nothing appears: the driver is unpowered, or TX and RX are crossed on an adapter that is not the Source Robotics one.

    What is on the screen¶

    • State: the identity above, the control mode the driver is in, the errors, and Clear errors.
    • Jog: Jog position, Jog velocity and Jog current move the motor in the matching control mode; Send setpoint sends a value in the current mode. Live stream while dragging follows a slider as you move it.
    • Plots of position, speed and current, panning and zooming, with the value under the pointer.
    • Settings: the motor parameters (pole pairs, resistance, inductance), the limits (current, velocity, voltage), the loop gains with Apply gains and Apply preset, the CAN node ID, the watchdog, the thermistor. The GUI marks Unsaved changes until you Save to the driver's EEPROM; Default restores the factory values.
    • Calibration: starts #Cal and shows the driver's log step by step; afterwards the resistance, inductance and pole pairs it measured, the phase order and direction, Kt, KV and the flux linkage.
    • Telemetry: the stream from the driver, and recordings of it for a test or a bug report.
    • Firmware update: the releases from GitHub, flashed through the bootloader; Scan bootloaders on a bus.
    • Gripper: the gripper controls, for a driver in gripper mode.
    • Console: type any # command by hand; the answer appears below it.
    • Wiring diagrams and Keyboard shortcuts under help.

    Screen guide¶

    Check

    After connecting real hardware, the state and identity belong to your driver. If they do not appear, check the transport, the selected port, the supply and the TX/RX wiring before trying to move.

    Try it without hardware¶

    Open Motor GUI, choose Mock CAN (demo), and press Connect. The motor and gripper panels run in the browser without a port picker. Motor calibration (#Cal) still requires a real UART connection; the CAN demo does not perform it.

    What next¶

    Calibration is the first thing to do with a connected driver; Working in Motor GUI covers everyday use: moving the motor, the plots, recordings, several motors on a bus.

    The serial terminal¶

    Motor GUI is the usual way to work with the driver; a plain serial terminal is the alternative, and it is what the console inside Motor GUI is.

    Everything in this manual that is done in the GUI is also one # command, and the terminal shows the driver's answers exactly as it prints them, which is useful when something does not work.

    The driver talks on its UART connector at 256000 baud, 8N1, 3.3 V. Any terminal that sends a line ending in CR+LF works.

    Connecting¶

    1. Plug the USB-to-serial adapter into the UART connector with the UART cable. With another adapter: 3.3 V only, and cross TX and RX: the driver's TX to the adapter's RX, the driver's RX to the adapter's TX.
    2. Connect the motor supply. The adapter's 3.3 V powers the driver's logic, so the driver answers over UART with only the adapter plugged in, but it reports a Vbus error and refuses calibration and every motion command until the 12 to 29 V supply is on.
    3. Find the adapter's port:

    The adapter is COM and a number. Device Manager lists it under Ports (COM & LPT): the entry that disappears when you unplug the adapter is yours. An adapter that shows a warning sign there instead needs the driver of its USB chip (on most 3.3 V adapters a CP210x or a CH340) from the chip maker.

    The serial adapter is /dev/ttyUSB0 on most adapters, the CANvas adapter /dev/ttyACM0; ls /dev/ttyUSB* /dev/ttyACM* lists them. To open them without root, join the dialout group once, then log out and in again:

    sudo usermod -aG dialout $USER

    The serial adapter is /dev/cu.usbserial- and a few characters on most adapters, the CANvas adapter /dev/cu.usbmodem and a number; ls /dev/cu.* lists them. Use the cu. name, not the tty. one, in a terminal or a script.

    Danger

    5 V on the UART pins destroys the board.

    A terminal¶

    PuTTY needs to send CR+LF when you press Enter, which the stock build cannot. Use the modified PuTTY: under Terminal → Keyboard select the CR LF option, then open the serial port at 256000.

    Keyboard setting Session

    With the firmware project open in VS Code with the PlatformIO extension, the serial monitor (the plug icon in the status bar) uses the speed and the send_on_enter filter from platformio.ini, so a line goes out with CR+LF when you press Enter.

    platformio.ini The monitor

    Select the adapter's port under Tools, any board (the monitor does not care), open the serial monitor, set 256000 baud and Both NL & CR as the line ending.

    The first commands¶

    #Info

    prints the driver's identity: the hardware and firmware version, the CAN ID, the calibration state and the motor parameters. #Help (firmware 106 and newer) prints a short command reference, #Error the active errors, #SERNUM the serial number and #Param the gains.

    Check

    #Info answers with the driver's identity, the hardware and firmware version first. If nothing comes back: the baud rate, the CR+LF line ending, TX and RX, see The driver does not answer.

    Every command starts with #, and a Set/Get command takes one number after a space: #P 16384 moves to position 16384; #P alone prints the position. Names are case-sensitive. The whole set is in the UART commands reference, and Over UART explains the protocol.

    Calibration¶

    Calibration teaches the driver its motor. It checks the magnet and the supply, then measures the motor's phase resistance and inductance and computes the current loop gains from them.

    It spins the motor in open loop to find the pole pairs and the direction, checks the order of the phases and aligns the encoder to the rotor field, and measures the motor constants Kt, KV and the flux linkage. Nothing about the motor has to be entered first.

    It runs once per motor; the result is kept by saving. From firmware 109 every #Cal goes on by itself to two more runs, the encoder table and the anti-cogging, and saves. #Calonly is the calibration alone.

    The motor turns

    Calibration spins the motor in both directions, fast at times. Fix the motor to the bench and take everything off the shaft.

    Supply voltage

    Calibrate at 24 V. At a lower voltage the resistance, the inductance, KV and Kt come out wrong.

    The settings¶

    The routine uses six settings, which it prints when it starts. The defaults suit motors with a phase resistance of 2 to 24 Ω; change them only for a motor outside that range.

    Setting Default Command What it does
    Current loop bandwidth 160 Hz #Cbw the bandwidth the current loop gains are computed for (Kpiq ≈ ω·L, Kiiq = ω·R·T, with ω = 2π × the bandwidth and T the 200 µs control period); too high makes the motor vibrate and whine. 500 Hz up to firmware 107
    Resistance voltage 1000 mV #Resv the voltage the resistance measurement starts at; it rises until the power reaches the limit below
    Max power dissipation 12 W #Calpwr the power the resistance and inductance measurements may put into the motor
    Open loop voltage 5000 mV #Openv the voltage of the open-loop spin that finds the pole pairs
    Open loop speed 20 rad/s #Opens the electrical speed of that spin
    Phase search voltage 5000 mV #Vp the voltage of the spin that checks the phase order

    A seventh setting is not printed: #Iqoffset, 300 mA by default, the current of the angle-offset search and of the commutation check. None of the seven is saved; after a power cycle they are back at these defaults. The current gains the bandwidth gives are saved with the calibration.

    Low-resistance motors

    These voltages are made for motors of 2 Ω and more. On a motor with a much lower resistance, such as a drone motor, they drive a destructive current. The Spectral Micro is not made for such motors; if you must try one, lower the three voltages first. Lowering the current limit does not help: calibration sets its own.

    Calibration sets its own current limit

    The routine does not use the current limit you set. It raises the limit to 1600 mA, and drives the motor with up to 1250 mA while it measures Kt and KV and with #Iqoffset while it aligns the encoder. From firmware 109 it puts the limit that was in force back when it ends; firmware 107 and older left 1600 mA in force (known issue KI-2).

    1. Restore the defaults¶

    The EEPROM of a new driver, or of one that ran another motor, can hold values that do not fit: another motor's parameters, or junk that shows as -1 in #Info. Calibration starts from what is there, so start from the factory values.

    Default in the settings.

    #Default

    This also sets the hardware version and batch date to factory values, and clears an encoder table and an anti-cogging set from another motor; note what #Info printed if you need them. From firmware 109 the CAN node ID is kept. Saving the configuration says what else it does.

    2. Run it¶

    We recommend a Motor GUI preset

    In Motor GUI's Presets tab, apply Generic motor, BLDC motor on Spectral Micro (soft) before you calibrate. It writes a low current limit and soft loops and leaves the resistance, the inductance and the pole pairs to the calibration, which measures them, so the motor calibrates safely. Raise the limits and the gains from there (Current limit and loop tuning). More: Motor GUI.

    Start the calibration. The driver enters Calibration mode and the GUI shows its log step by step; when it finishes, the resistance, inductance and pole pairs it measured, the phase order and direction, Kt, KV and the flux linkage appear in the settings.

    #Cal

    The LED flashes fast, about 10 times a second, while the routine runs, and the driver prints each step as it goes. The numbers below are those of one tested motor; yours will differ:

    ----------------
Calibration mode
-----Settings-----
Current loop bandwidth: 160.00Hz
Resistance voltage: 1000mV
Max Power dissipation: 12.00W
Open loop voltage: 5000mV
Open loop Speed: 20.00Rad/s
Phase search voltage: 5000mV
----------------
Magnet test: Passed
Vbus test: Passed
Resistance = 7.026575Ohm
Inductance = 0.001492H
Kp_iq = ...
Ki_iq = ...
Pole pair = 11
Dir = 1
Calibrating angle offset... stage 1/13, sweep point 0
Calibrating angle offset... stage 2/13, sweep point 0
...
Phase order = 2
Phase order is good!
KV is: ...
Kt is: ...
KV runs: ... ... ... ... (same direction within ... %)
Flux linkage is: ...
----------------
Calibration success!
Next, by itself: the encoder table, then anti-cogging (about a minute; keep the shaft free). Everything is saved when they finish (#Encal state, #Cogcal state)
Going to idle!
----------------

    The Calibrating angle offset... lines come every half second while the encoder is aligned, which can take about 40 seconds; the routine has not stopped.

    #Calonly runs the same calibration and nothing after it; it ends with Save config with #Save instead.

    From firmware 110 #Estop or #Close stops a running calibration; #Idle does not. It ends as a failed calibration (Calibration failed!): the motor is released, and the driver is not calibrated until it calibrates again. After #Estop, send #Clear first.

    On firmware 109 a reset (#Reset) was the only clean way over UART: #Close switched the mode under the running calibration and left it half done.

    What the steps are:

    Step What happens Fails with
    Magnet test the encoder sees enough field: a magnet is there and close enough. The test says nothing about centring, the gap or the glue Magnet test: Failed: magnet missing, too far, or axially magnetised
    Vbus test the supply is within limits Vbus test: Failed
    Resistance a rising voltage is put on the windings and the current measured; the motor clicks. The resistance follows from the two Resistance test: Failed: no current flows, a phase is not connected, or the motor is far outside the 2 to 24 Ω range
    Inductance a voltage step and the rate at which the current rises give the inductance. Kp_iq and Ki_iq are computed from R, L and the bandwidth Inductance test: Failed
    Open loop the stator field is turned at the open-loop speed and the encoder watched: the pole pairs are the ratio of electrical to mechanical turns, and 1 to 49 are accepted. The direction the rotor turned is remembered as the commutation direction (Commutation dir in #Info); Dir is always 1 after a calibration Open loop: Failed, then The rotor did not follow the open-loop field (pole pairs must come out as 1 to 49). and Check the motor's three wires and that the shaft is free.
    Phase order a current command must move the rotor the expected way, and the encoder's zero is aligned to the rotor field (the angle offset, #Offset). A motor that does not respond gets a second try at another angle Commutation/alignment check failed!, Motor did not respond as expected to a current command... and a line Spin-confirm delta: … ticks (need > 8192), Vbus: … mV that says how far the motor turned (half a revolution is needed) and at what voltage
    Kt, KV, flux linkage four spins from standstill, about a second each and alternately forward and back; KV is the mean speed per volt available, and the constants follow from it. KV runs: in the log shows all four and how far they agree nothing. KV runs disagree is a warning, not a failure: two runs in the same direction differ by more than 5 %. The shaft was not free, or something held it; calibrate again

    The measured values are estimates

    The resistance, the inductance and the motor constants differ a little from one run to the next, and KV, and with it Kt and the flux linkage, can be off by a factor of two or three on cheap motors with uneven windings. For the loops this does not matter; for a torque estimate in newton-metres, measure Kt yourself.

    What follows the calibration¶

    Half a second after Calibration success! of a #Cal the driver starts two runs on its own: the encoder table, then the anti-cogging identification. Keep the shaft free for about a minute more.

    When they succeed, everything is saved. A command from a host (a motion command, #Idle) stops them; then nothing is saved, and the calibration is kept only if you #Save it.

    A host that takes the drive before they start has 3 s: after that the runs are dropped and do not start later. #Cal always runs them; #Calonly never does. Encoder table and anti-cogging explains both.

    Set the current limit before you calibrate

    The encoder table turns the field with a current of 80 % of the current limit, for about 20 s. Calibration uses its own 1600 mA for its steps and puts the limit that was in force back before the encoder table runs. So set #Ilim for the motor before #Cal (known issue KI-19).

    3. Set the current limit and save¶

    Calibration puts back the current limit that was in force when it started (firmware 107 and older left 1600 mA). If that is not your motor's yet, set it now (Current limit and loop tuning), then save. After #Cal the driver saves by itself when the runs succeed; save anyway once they are done, or after #Calonly.

    Set the current limit in the limits, then Save. The GUI marks Unsaved changes until you do.

    #Ilim 1000
#Save

    Without it the calibration is gone at the next power cycle. After #Calonly the driver is idle. After #Cal it ends the two runs holding the position the shaft reached: #Idle releases it. The LED shows normal operation; #Info shows Calibrated, the pole pairs, the resistance and inductance, Kt and KV, the phase order and the commutation direction.

    Check

    After a power cycle #Info still prints Calibrated, and Motor GUI shows Calibrated. Not calibrated means the save did not happen: calibrate and save again.

    When to calibrate again¶

    • After changing the motor, the magnet or the wiring.
    • After restoring the defaults.
    • After an update from firmware 107 or older to 109, where the motor can turn freely: the older calibration stored the resistance and the inductance too low. An assembled SSG-48 keeps its calibration and gets its preset (Upgrading from older firmware).
    • After a firmware update that clears the calibration (a release note says so when it does).

    A calibration that reports success but a motor that runs badly afterwards usually means the magnet moved on the shaft between calibration and use: fix the magnet and calibrate again.

    Troubleshooting¶

    Message Cause
    Magnet test: Failed no magnet, magnet too far (more than about 1.5 mm), or an axially magnetised magnet
    Vbus test: Failed supply off, too low, or the cable the wrong way round (if the driver survived it)
    Resistance test: Failed, Inductance test: Failed a phase not connected or shorted; a motor far outside the 2 to 24 Ω range
    the motor wobbles left and right and the routine fails one phase is not connected
    Open loop: Failed the rotor did not follow the field, or the pole pairs came out outside 1 to 49: a phase open, the shaft not free, the open-loop voltage (#Openv) too low for a high-resistance motor, the supply's current limit set too low
    Commutation/alignment check failed! the motor did not respond to the current command: a phase open, #Iqoffset set too low, a magnet that slips. Run #Cal again. Firmware 101 printed Phase order is wrong! Switch motor phases and try again! here; since 105 the wiring is compensated and swapping wires does not help
    Cal: not started, the E-stop is latched (#Clear first), or Stopped by an E-stop: #Clear, then calibrate again. after Calibration failed! an E-stop is latched or ended the calibration: #Clear, then #Cal; the alignment starts again from its first stage
    the routine stops in a step and prints nothing more stop it with #Close (firmware 110) or reset the driver (#Reset), and try again
    success, but the LED stays on and no error is reported #Info shows -1 somewhere: the EEPROM holds a value from an older release (The check at power-up); restore the defaults and calibrate again
    the resistance, KV or Kt look wrong the supply was not 24 V, or the motor is one of the uneven ones; see the note above

    Calibration runs over the serial connection only: a terminal, or Motor GUI over the serial adapter, not over CAN.

    Encoder table and anti-cogging¶

    Firmware 109 measures two corrections on the drive itself, with nothing but the motor on the bench. The encoder table corrects the small error of the encoder's angle reading. The anti-cogging is a current that cancels the motor's own torque ripple. The same routines run on the sister driver STEPFOC.

    After Calibration success! of a #Cal both run by themselves, and everything is saved when they succeed (What follows the calibration). #Calonly is the calibration alone. This page explains what they are, how to run them again, and how to check them.

    On the Spectral Micro bench (gimbal motor, 2026-11-10) a full #Cal measured the encoder table and identified the anti-cogging, and saved both. Check the result (Checking them) before you keep it.

    The motor turns

    Each run turns the motor one revolution forward and one back, slowly. Keep the shaft free: nothing on it and nothing it could drive. A mechanism that cannot turn a whole revolution, a gripper's jaws for example, has to come off first.

    The encoder table¶

    The magnetic encoder reads the shaft angle with a small error that changes slowly around the turn. The magnet is never exactly on the axis, and the chip itself is not perfectly linear. On the bench gimbal motor it was 0.50° peak to peak.

    The STEPFOC bench stepper read 0.61 to 0.65° from run to run. Two of its tables agreed within 0.021°.

    The commutation multiplies it by the pole pairs. On an 11-pole-pair motor 0.50° spans about 5.5 electrical degrees. The electrical offset sits near the middle of that span, so the field is a few electrical degrees off at places. That costs torque and makes the current uneven around the turn.

    To measure it, the driver turns the field open loop, one shaft revolution forward and one back at 2048 ticks/s (about 20 s). A current of 80 % of the current limit holds the rotor to the field.

    At each 1/128 of a turn it records how far the encoder's angle is from the field's. Forward and backward are averaged, so friction, which pulls the rotor behind the field in each direction, cancels. The motor's own pull at higher orders is left out by keeping only the harmonics 1 to 16 per turn.

    The result is a table of 128 corrections. From then on the encoder reading is corrected with it. The electrical offset is moved by the table's mean error, so the commutation stays right.

    The plot shows a measured table from the sister driver STEPFOC, which runs the same routine, on its bench NEMA 17. Its right-hand scale multiplies by the stepper's 50 pole pairs. On an 11-pole-pair motor the same error is about 4.5 times smaller in electrical degrees.

    A check of the routine on a simulated 11-pole-pair motor at the Spectral Micro's loop rate took the commutation error from 30 to 0.4 electrical degrees.

    A table over 4° peak to peak, or one that would move the offset by more than 60 electrical degrees, is rejected as implausible: the magnet is loose or far off its place.

    Anti-cogging¶

    A motor's magnets pull towards the stator's teeth. Errors in the current measurement add a ripple once and twice per electrical turn.

    At low speed both show as a ripple in the speed and the position. Anti-cogging adds a current to the current setpoint that cancels it: a function of the electrical angle, added in velocity and position modes.

    To find it, the driver runs in velocity mode at 1500 ticks/s (parameter 44), one motor revolution forward and one back. It records the current the velocity loop asks for in 256 bins over the electrical angle. Then it averages the two directions, each without its mean, so friction drops out.

    From that it keeps two forms of the same correction:

    • harmonics (parameter 37 = 1): the 8 largest of the harmonics 1 to 32 of the electrical angle, each a·cos(h·θ) + b·sin(h·θ) in mA;
    • table (parameter 37 = 2): 256 values in mA over one electrical turn, interpolated between them.

    The plot shows both forms on the STEPFOC's bench NEMA 17. Its cogging current was about the size of the Spectral Micro's bench gimbal motor's (7.1 and 6.8 mA rms); the shape over the turn depends on the motor.

    The identification switches the harmonics on. On the bench gimbal motor it found 6.8 mA rms. Parameter 38 scales the correction from 0 to 200 % for experiments; 100 % is as identified.

    The angle here comes from the encoder alone, without the calibration's offset. The part of the correction that comes from the current measurement is tied to the motor's electrical angle, though, which the offset defines. That is one reason #Cal identifies the anti-cogging again.

    The identification records the current the loops ask for, so it depends a little on the gains. After a large change of the current or velocity gains, identify it again.

    The current-sense zero trim¶

    The STEPFOC firmware can also correct the current-sense zeros from the anti-cogging's once-per-turn part (the zero trim, 43 = 4 or 5). The Spectral Micro firmware 109 skips that step until the map of its current channels is measured.

    So here #Cogcal trim (43 = 4) and 43 = 5 identify like 1 and 2. The state reads 3, never 11 (done without the trim). The combined runs and the runs after a #Cal ask for the trim too, with the same result.

    Parameters 62 and 63 hold the 1st harmonic before and after a trim, in 0.1 mA. After a run that asked for the trim, both read the 1st harmonic of the set found.

    The trims (parameters 59 and 60, up to ±60 counts) change only when written. They are saved with the encoder table and added to the zero the driver measures at every start-up.

    Parameter 56 has no effect on the Spectral Micro. It keeps its number so that the two drivers' parameters match. #Cal always runs both corrections, #Calonly neither.

    Running them¶

    The driver must be calibrated and without an error. Each run has a UART command and a CAN parameter:

    What UART CAN: Send_Parameter
    the encoder table, used at once #Encal 52 = 1
    the encoder table, used and saved #Encal save 52 = 2
    the encoder table, then the anti-cogging, then save all #Encal all 52 = 3, or 43 = 3
    the anti-cogging, switched on #Cogcal 43 = 1
    the anti-cogging, switched on and saved #Cogcal save 43 = 2
    stop a run #Encal stop, #Cogcal stop 52 = 0, 43 = 0

    #Encal stop does not stop the anti-cogging part of a combined run (#Encal all). #Cogcal stop stops a run in either part, and so do 52 = 0 and 43 = 0 over CAN.

    1. Make sure the shaft is free: nothing on it and nothing it could drive.
    2. Send the run's command from the table, on the UART or over CAN.
    3. Send nothing else while the motor turns, one revolution forward and one back for each run.
    4. Read the result with #Encal state or #Cogcal state, or parameter 52 or 43 over CAN.

    Check

    The state reads 3, done. The motor holds the position the shaft reached. Any other state is in the table "When a run fails" under Checking them.

    Both runs keep the CAN watchdog fed while they go, and end by holding the position the shaft reached. They stop at once when a host sends a command (a motion command, Idle) or an error comes up. Nothing is saved then.

    While a run turns the motor a capture is not armed. During the anti-cogging run parameters 34 and 35 refuse a write: the runs use the capture's memory and the histogram.

    A save from a run is the whole configuration, as #Save writes it. After a change of the motor, the magnet or its position, run the encoder table and the anti-cogging again, in that order, or calibrate again.

    Two guards keep a correction from running on the wrong data:

    • parameter 37 refuses 1 or 2 while no set is stored for that form;
    • an encoder table measured on its own (52 = 1 or 2) switches an anti-cogging set from before off, because that set was found on the uncorrected encoder. The anti-cogging state then reads 10 until it is identified again.

    Checking them¶

    #Info ends with what is in force:

    In force:
  encoder table: on, error 0.61 deg p-p
  anti-cogging: on (harmonics), saved
