  current-sense zero trim: 0 / 0 counts
  delay compensation: 234 us
  speed estimate: direct PLL, 100 Hz
  input filter: off; integral deadband: off

    The example shows the defaults, with an example encoder error. While a run goes, a line running: encoder table or running: anti-cogging identification follows.

    #Encal state and #Cogcal state print one run's state without starting it. After a finished identification (state 3) it also lists the harmonics. Parameter 43 gives the same state over CAN.

    Over CAN the same is in the parameters (the parameter table):

    Parameter What it tells On the bench gimbal motor
    52 the encoder table's state: 3 done, 4 aborted, 5 error or not calibrated, 6 the rotor did not follow, 7 rejected 3
    54 the table's error peak to peak, 0.01° of the shaft 50 (0.50°)
    55 the amplitude of its once-per-turn part, 0.01° of the shaft
    57 how far the last table moved the electrical offset, 0.1° electrical
    43 the anti-cogging state: 3 done, 6 the shaft did not turn 3
    45 the cogging current found, 0.1 mA rms 68 (6.8 mA)
    79 the bits of what is in force, see What is in force

    Send_Buffer_Read reads the corrections themselves: kind 4 the encoder table (128 values in 1/16 count), kind 2 the harmonics, kind 3 the anti-cogging table (Respond_Buffer_Data).

    A capture of the speed while the motor turns slowly, with the anti-cogging off and on (Status and diagnostics), shows whether it helps on your motor.

    On the STEPFOC's bench NEMA 17 the speed ripple that repeats with the angle was 1.2 to 2 times smaller with the anti-cogging on, at 1500 to 6000 ticks/s. The Spectral Micro has not been measured this way yet.

    When a run fails:

    State Cause What to do
    52 = 6, 43 = 6 the rotor did not follow: the shaft is blocked, or the current is too low to turn it free the shaft; for the encoder table raise the current limit (it uses 80 % of it)
    52 = 5, 43 = 5 the driver was in error or is not calibrated #Error, clear it, calibrate
    52 = 4, 43 = 4 a command came first run it again and send nothing meanwhile
    52 = 7 the table is implausible check the magnet: glued, centred, at the right distance (The encoder magnet), then calibrate
    43 = 8 the encoder table of a combined run failed parameter 52 says why

    When a run fails after a #Cal, the calibration itself is still good: save it with #Save.

    Switching them off¶

    UART CAN: Send_Parameter
    the encoder table off / on #Encal off, #Encal on 53 = 0, 53 = 1
    the anti-cogging off, harmonics, table #Par 37,0, #Par 37,1, #Par 37,2 37 = 0, 1, 2
    how much of the anti-cogging #Par 38, and the percentage 38 = 0 to 200 (%)

    #Save keeps the switches with the corrections. #Default clears both corrections and the trims.

    STEPFOC

    The STEPFOC firmware 109 has the same encoder table and anti-cogging, and the current-sense zero trim as well. Its manual says more.

    Current limit and loop tuning¶

    Calibration leaves the driver with current loop gains computed for your motor and with conservative velocity and position gains. Two things remain: the current limit, which you must set for your motor, and the loop gains, which you tune if the defaults do not suit it.

    Motor GUI shows the motor's response on its plots while you change a gain, which is what makes tuning quick.

    The current limit¶

    Set the current limit to the rated phase current of your motor in mA. Calibration uses its own 1600 mA for its steps and, from firmware 109, puts your limit back when it ends (107 and older left 1600 mA: set it again after calibrating there).

    The factory default (#Default) of 1000 mA suits the gimbal motors in Tested motors; a motor rated for more can go up to the driver's 2800 mA.

    #Ilim clamps the limit to 2800 mA; from firmware 109 Send_Limits over CAN does too. The firmware does not trip on phase current by itself; the MOSFET driver's hardware protection only catches a hard fault. A limit above the motor's rating overheats the motor.

    In the limits set the current limit, then Save.

    #Ilim 1500
#Save

    Crunching and whining sounds at speed usually mean the current limit, or the current loop gains, are too high.

    The loops¶

    Tune from the inside out: the current loop first, then velocity, then position. Before you start:

    • The motor must be calibrated and without active errors; the driver refuses the control modes otherwise.
    • Fix the motor to the bench, nothing on the shaft. A loop that oscillates can shake a loose motor off the table.
    • If the defaults oscillate, lower all gains until the motor is quiet, then raise them one at a time in small steps.

    The gains are in the settings: change one, Apply gains, command a move with the jog controls and read the response on the plots. Apply preset loads a starting set for a kind of motor: a product preset writes its own current and velocity gains; the Generic motor presets set soft limits and velocity gains and leave the current gains to #Cbw and the calibration. Apply a preset before you calibrate, so that the calibration computes the current gains. Save when you are done.

    Each gain is a command: #Kpiq 0.5, #Kpv 0.02, …, then #Save. Command a move with #Iq, #V or #P and judge the response by ear and by #Cyc 20, which streams position, speed and current.

    The current loop¶

    The current loop is a PI controller that turns a current request into a voltage.

    Its gains Kpiq and Kiiq follow from the motor's resistance R, its inductance L (both measured by calibration) and one number, the bandwidth: Kpiq ≈ ω·L and Kiiq = ω·R·T, with ω = 2π × the bandwidth and T the 200 µs control period (Kiiq is applied once per control loop).

    The PI then cancels the coil's own lag, and the current follows a request like a first-order filter at that bandwidth. Calibration computes them this way at 160 Hz, the default from firmware 109; so does setting the bandwidth:

    #Cbw 200          new gains at once from R and L; it prints them
#Save             keeps the gains (the bandwidth itself is 160 again after a power cycle)

    Send_Parameter(49, 200), then Send_Save_config. Reading parameter 49 gives the bandwidth the gains in force amount to with the R and L in force.

    The bandwidth can be 10 to 500 Hz (a tenth of the 5000 Hz control loop). A higher one follows a request and rejects a disturbance faster, and is louder. Gains set by hand with #Kpiq and #Kiiq work too; parameter 49 then tells which bandwidth they amount to.

    Drives calibrated with older firmware

    Firmware 107 and older computed the gains for 500 Hz on the 5000 Hz loop, which is fast for that loop rate. Their saved gains stay as they are after an update to 109: parameter 49 reads about 500 Hz until the drive is calibrated again. Calibrate again after the update where the motor can turn freely. A calibration made with 107 or older stored the resistance about 10 % and the inductance about 45 % too low, so #Cbw on the old values gives gains for a lower bandwidth than it names (on an SSG-48 apply its preset instead, which writes the gains). 160 Hz is the default; on the gimbal-motor bench 300 Hz settled a speed step 3.6 times faster at the same holding noise, and 500 Hz added half again the current noise. The SSG-48 preset sets 300 Hz. See Upgrading from older firmware.

    Test with a current setpoint of a few hundred mA (Jog current, or #Iq 300): the motor should push with a steady, quiet torque. Keep the current well under the limit.

    The velocity loop¶

    1. Raise Kpv until the motor oscillates at a steady speed command, then back off 20 %.
    2. Raise Kiv slowly until it oscillates, then back off a little. Kiv is an integrator: too much makes the motor unstable, none leaves a steady-state error.
    3. Test with a speed setpoint (Jog velocity, or #V 20000; 16384 ticks per second is one revolution per second) and with different speeds.

    The position loop¶

    1. Raise Kpp until the motor overshoots or oscillates when it reaches a target, then back off.
    2. Test with a position step (Jog position, or #P 16384 and #P 0): one revolution and back. The response should be quick, without overshoot; the plot shows both.

    Testing¶

    Run the motor over the whole range of speeds and loads it will see, with a load if you can, and adjust. Then save.

    The speed estimate¶

    The velocity loop needs the speed, which the driver estimates from the encoder count. From firmware 109 parameter 50 picks how:

    Parameter 50 Estimate How it behaves
    0 moving average of the last 20 count differences about 2 ms late, in steps of 250 ticks/s: the estimate of every release before
    1 PLL, the speed is its integrator smooth, a little late
    2 (default) direct PLL, integrator plus the proportional term almost no lag, a little more noise

    Both PLLs are critically damped at the natural frequency of parameter 51, 100 Hz by default (10 to 250 Hz). The lag of the moving average is what limits the velocity gain.

    On the gimbal-motor bench the direct PLL at 100 Hz allowed twice the Kpv of the moving average before the motor vibrated (0.11 against 0.056), lowered the speed ripple 1.3 to 3.4 times between 1500 and 48000 ticks/s, and cut the overshoot of a speed step from about 67 % to 12 % at the same gains.

    At 60 Hz it overshot more, at 200 Hz it held with more current noise. Calibration and the gripper's stop detection always use the moving average. Neither parameter is kept by Save. The speed in Respond_data_pack_1 and Send_Respond_Encoder_data, the watchdog descend and the capture is the picked estimate.

    Commutation delay compensation¶

    The voltage a control loop sets reaches the motor later than the angle it was computed from was read: the rest of the loop, the wait for the PWM update and half a control period.

    By then the rotor has turned on, and the voltage lands a little behind the angle it was meant for. Parameter 64 turns the output voltage forward by the angle the rotor covers in that delay, in the closed-loop modes above 100 ticks/s; 1 to 500 µs are accepted.

    The default is 234 µs, the Spectral Micro's own delay measured on the bench: 20 points between 24000 and 120000 ticks/s gave one delay within 2 to 4 % of theory at every speed.

    Without it the field lags about 6° electrical at 120000 ticks/s. The delay belongs to the driver's timing, not to the motor, so it suits any motor; 0 switches it off. It is not kept by Save.

    The finer points of the velocity loop¶

    Two settings for special cases, both off by default and not kept by Save; both were tested on the STEPFOC bench:

    • Position input filter, parameter 74 (Hz, 0 to 200). A host that sends positions at a fixed rate sends a staircase; without the speed the steps are all the loops see. The filter turns the staircase into a smooth path and makes its own speed from it, in position and PD modes. Use it only for a host that sends positions without the speed feedforward, at about the host's rate ÷ 12 (8 Hz for 100 Hz); with a host that sends the speed it makes the tracking worse.
    • Velocity-integral deadband, parameter 75 (ticks, 0 to 2000). In position mode the velocity integral sees the position error less this band, so a holding motor stops pushing against a small error it cannot correct. It trades accuracy for holding current.

    Impedance (PD) mode¶

    In PD mode the motor behaves like a spring and a damper: the torque is KP × (setpoint − position) + KD × (speed setpoint − speed) + feedforward current.

    Set KP and KD (the gains, or #KP, #KD, or Send_PD_Gains), then command with #PD, #PDV and #PDI or Send_data_pack_PD. Start with a small KP and KD of 0, raise KP until the motor holds position firmly, then add KD until it stops ringing. The theory corner explains the loop.

    Limits¶

    • The velocity limit (#Vlimit) clamps the speed the loops may command, in encoder ticks per second. A rough maximum for your motor is 0.8 × Vbus × KV × 16384 / 60 ticks per second, with KV from calibration.
    • The voltage limit (#Vlim) clamps the voltage of the current loops in mV; 0 uses the supply voltage.
    • #Rstint 1 resets the integrators on every new setpoint, which avoids windup when a program sends one setpoint after another with large steps.

    Saving the configuration¶

    Nothing you set survives a power cycle until you save it.

    Save. The GUI marks Unsaved changes until you do.

    #Save

    Saving writes the configuration to the EEPROM: the motor parameters and the calibration, every gain and limit, the CAN node ID, the heartbeat and watchdog settings, the thermistor and LED settings, the temperature and voltage limits and the gripper flag.

    From firmware 109 also the tool id and the watchdog fallback, the anti-cogging set with its mode and scale, and the encoder table with its on/off switch, parameter 56 and the current-sense zero trims. From firmware 110 also the idle behaviour (#Brake, Send_Settings or parameter 18).

    Over CAN, Send_Save_config does the same. Saving also copies the node ID into the bootloader's board ID (The bootloader). Save after calibration, after tuning, and after changing the node ID.

    Set the current limit before you save a calibration

    Firmware 107 and older left the current limit at 1600 mA after a calibration, and a save stored it. From firmware 109 calibration puts the limit that was in force back; check that it suits your motor (#Ilim) before you save.

    What is not saved¶

    These settings are lost at a power cycle, whatever you save. The driver starts with the values in the Defaults reference:

    • the calibration settings #Cbw (160 Hz; the gains it computed are kept), #Resv, #Calpwr, #Openv, #Opens, #Vp and #Iqoffset: set them again before each calibration that needs other values;
    • the parameters of firmware 109 that tune the loops: the speed estimate (the direct PLL at 100 Hz), the commutation delay compensation (234 µs), the sin/cos table (off), the position input filter and the velocity-integral deadband (off), the anti-cogging identification speed, the d-axis current, and the capture and histogram settings. The parameter table says for every parameter whether it is kept;
    • the cyclic output (#Cyc, #Cyca);
    • the serial number. Saving writes it, but the firmware does not read it back at power-up: after a power cycle the driver reports 1, and the next save writes that 1 over the stored number.

    The defaults¶

    Default in the settings.

    #Default

    Restoring the defaults writes the factory values to the EEPROM and loads them. It erases the calibration (the driver reports Not calibrated), clears the gripper flag, the heartbeat rate and the watchdog, sets the hardware version to 1 and the batch date to 24092024, and stores 1111 as the serial number.

    From firmware 109 it also clears the tool id, the watchdog fallback, the anti-cogging set, the encoder table and its zero trims, and sets parameter 56 back to 0. From firmware 110 it also sets the idle behaviour back to coasting.

    The CAN node ID is kept from firmware 109 (it is 0 only when no valid ID is stored); firmware 107 and older set it to 0.

    Note what the driver reports before you use it. The values are listed in the Defaults reference.

    Use it before the first calibration of a motor, after a firmware update when the driver behaves oddly, and when the state shows nonsense values (a -1 where a number should be).

    The check at power-up¶

    The configuration lives in an EEPROM chip on the board (a 24C16, 2 KB), apart from the firmware: flashing a new firmware never touches it, and only the firmware's check at power-up decides to reset a part of it. Since firmware 106 the saved configuration is in blocks, each with its own checksum; firmware 109 adds three:

    Block Bytes Checksum at Holds
    calibration 60-188, reserved 204-228 200 the motor's resistance, inductance, pole pairs, phase order, direction, angle offset, Kt, KV and the current loop gains
    settings 1 44-180 196 the other gains, the limits, the node ID, the watchdog, the heartbeat and the flags
    settings 2 (109) 260-312 256 tool id, watchdog fallback, the product stamp, the calibration ID, from 110 the idle behaviour; reserved slots
    anti-cogging (109) 324-879 320 the harmonics, the table, mode and scale
    encoder table (109) 884-1147 880 the table, its on/off switch, parameter 56, the current-sense zero trims

    At power-up the driver checks each. A block that fails, after a power loss during a save for instance, is replaced by its factory values without a message, and the others are kept.

    A failed settings block gives the default gains and limits (from firmware 109 the node ID stays, if it was valid); a failed calibration block leaves the driver Not calibrated.

    The new blocks need the chip's upper banks; on a smaller chip they keep their defaults in RAM and are not saved, which #Info says (Settings block 2: not available, block 3 not available).

    Going back to an older firmware is safe: the first two blocks are unchanged, and older firmware ignores everything from byte 256 on. Upgrading from older firmware says what changes when you update.

    A board that comes from a release before 106 has no checksums yet: the driver keeps its values once and seals them; from firmware 109 every board that was ever saved is trusted this way.

    A value that was already wrong (a -1 in #Info, for instance) then stays, and #Default with a new calibration is the fix. A blank EEPROM gets the defaults.

    What the driver reports¶

    • #Info prints the identity and the motor: hardware version, batch date, firmware version, CAN ID, whether the motor is calibrated, temperature, supply voltage, the phase order and direction, the pole pairs, resistance, inductance, Kt and KV, and from firmware 109 the build, the board ID and the calibration ID, the tool id, the watchdog, the EEPROM blocks and, at its end, what is in force;
    • #SERNUM prints the serial number;
    • #Param the gains and limits;
    • #Error the active errors.

    They are the first thing to look at when something is off, and the thing to copy into a bug report. Status and diagnostics explains the new lines.

    6. Operation

    Operation¶

    How a calibrated driver is used: the control modes and what each one does, the two ways to talk to the driver and the two tools on top of them, what happens when something goes wrong, and gripper mode.

    In this chapter¶

    • Control modes
    • Over UART
    • Over CAN
    • The watchdog: what the driver does when the host stops talking
    • With Python
    • Working in Motor GUI
    • Errors and recovery
    • Sound: tones, tunes and recorded sound through the motor
    • Status and diagnostics: what is in force, the identity, telemetry, captures
    • Gripper mode

    The exact layout of every message and command is in the API reference.

    Control modes¶

    The driver is always in one control mode. A command that carries a setpoint switches to the mode that uses it; #Idle or Send_Idle switches back to idle. The driver refuses the closed-loop modes while it is not calibrated or while an error is active.

    stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> NotCalibrated: power-up, no saved calibration
    [*] --> Idle: power-up, saved calibration
    NotCalibrated --> Calibration: #Cal
    Calibration --> Idle: success, #Save
    Calibration --> NotCalibrated: a test fails
    Idle --> ControlMode: a setpoint
    ControlMode --> Idle: #Idle, Send_Idle
    ControlMode --> Error: a fault
    Idle --> Error: a fault
    Error --> Idle: #Clear, cause gone

    A setpoint is #P, #V, #Iq, #PD or a CAN motion frame, and a new setpoint switches the mode without passing through idle; a fault is any of the errors on Errors and recovery.

    ControlMode What the motor does Entered by Setpoint unit
    Idle nothing: coasts (phases open), or brakes (phases shorted) after #Brake 1 or Send_Settings; with an error it always coasts, see Braking in idle #Idle, Send_Idle, #Clear, errors —
    Position holds and moves to a position with the cascade of position, velocity and current loops #P, Send_data_pack_1 (8 bytes) encoder ticks
    Velocity turns at a speed with the velocity and current loops #V, Send_data_pack_1 (5 bytes) encoder ticks/s
    Current (torque) applies a current, so a torque, with the current loop alone #Iq, Send_data_pack_1 (2 bytes) mA
    PD (impedance) behaves like a spring and a damper around a position, with KP and KD #PD, Send_data_pack_PD encoder ticks
    Voltage torque applies a voltage on the q axis directly, without the current loop #Uq mV
    Open loop turns the stator field at a set electrical speed without the encoder in the loop; the rotor follows it like a stepper. It applies the voltage #Openv (5000 mV by default), not a current: #Ilim does not apply, and a low-resistance motor draws far more than its limit #Openloop electrical speed
    Gripper runs the gripper state machine: move to a jaw position with a speed and a current limit #Grippos, Send_gripper_data_pack (with #Gripper 1) 0 to 255
    Calibration the calibration routine, or the gripper calibration #Cal, #Gripcal —

    Braking in idle¶

    #Brake 1 (or Send_Settings over CAN) makes the idle motor brake: the driver keeps the three phases shorted, so the motor resists being turned. #Brake 0, the default, lets it coast.

    From firmware 110 #Save keeps the setting; on 109 every power-up started with coasting. Braking works only in idle without an error; an error or an E-stop disables the driver, and the motor coasts.

    The braking current is not limited

    A motor that is turned while it brakes drives its own back-EMF current through the shorted phases, and the current loop does not limit that current. Use braking only on a motor whose resistance keeps the current under the driver's 2.8 A at the speeds it turns at.

    Units¶

    • Position is in encoder ticks: 16384 per revolution of the motor shaft, counting up without limit over several revolutions. Positive is counter-clockwise seen from the encoder side of the board, see below.
    • Speed is in encoder ticks per second: 16384 is one revolution per second, 60 rpm.
    • Current is in mA. Torque is current times the motor's Kt, which calibration finds.
    • Feedforward: the 8-byte motion frame carries a speed and a current feedforward beside the position, the 5-byte one a current feedforward beside the speed. They are added after the loops, so a host that knows the trajectory can command it directly and leave the loops to correct the rest. Send 0 when you do not use them.

    Try it¶

    As a position: 1.000 turns, 360.0° · as a speed: 60 rpm · the practical maximum for 11 pole pairs is about 2727 rpm

    Torque: 0.30 Nm, 30 N·cm

    The units of this page, with your numbers: 16384 encoder ticks per turn, the practical speed limit from the electrical frequency the control loop can follow (Ratings and limits), and the torque as Kt times the current.

    Positive rotation¶

    The direction is tied to the encoder, not to the wiring: since firmware 105 calibration compensates the order of the phases, so the motor turns the same way whichever wire went to which terminal.

    Which mode am I in?¶

    #Mode prints the mode: Idle mode, Position control, Speed control, Current control, PD control, Open loop, Gripper mode or Gripper calib mode. In voltage-torque mode it prints nothing at all; #Uq then prints the voltage setpoint.

    Over UART¶

    The serial port is the interactive way to use the driver: you type a command, the driver answers in text. It is the only way to calibrate, and the quickest way to try a setting. For real-time control of several motors use CAN.

    The protocol¶

    A command is #, a name, optionally a space and one number, and a line ending:

    #P 16384
#P
#Cal
    • The name is case-sensitive and matches the firmware's spelling exactly (#Kpiq, not #kpiq); at most 19 characters, the argument at most 19 characters and without spaces (#WdFb 2000,0,1000,2). Firmware 107 and older allowed 20 characters for both together.
    • The line ends with LF (\n); a CR before it is accepted. Terminals send CR+LF when you press Enter, with the setting described in The serial terminal.
    • A Set/Get command with a number sets the value and prints it; without a number it only prints it. #P 16384 moves to position 16384 and prints the position; #P prints the position. One exception: #Openloop without a number starts the motor at the stored open-loop speed.
    • An action command takes no number: #Cal, #Save, #Idle.
    • A name the driver does not know is answered with Unknown command; firmware 107 and older answered so also the few names its parser accepted but had no handler for.
    • 256000 baud, 8N1, 3.3 V.

    The driver only answers¶

    The driver never sends anything on its own, except with the cyclic commands: #Cyc 100 makes it print position, speed and Iq every 100 ms as $ <position> <speed> <Iq> (for BetterSerialPlotter), #Cyca 100 as Position:x,Speed:y,Iq:z (for the Arduino plotter); #Cyc or #Cyca without a number stops it.

    Typical sessions¶

    A first test after calibration:

    #Ilim 1500        current limit for the motor
#V 16384          one revolution per second
#V 0
#P 0              back to the start
#Idle

    Tuning:

    #Kpv 0.02
#Kiv 0.001
#V 30000
#Save

    Status:

    #Info
#Error
#Temp
#Vbus

    Every command is in the UART reference, grouped by what it does.

    Over CAN¶

    CAN is the interface for running a machine: compact frames, a fixed cost per message, up to 14 drivers on one bus, and a host that can be a PC, a microcontroller or a robot control box.

    The driver answers a motion command with its position, speed and current in the same frame exchange, so a control loop on the host needs one frame out and one frame back per motor and cycle.

    The frame¶

    CAN 2.0 data frames with 11-bit identifiers at 1 Mbit/s. The identifier carries the node, the command and the error state:

    • Node ID, bits 10 to 7: which driver, 0 to 15. A driver acts only on frames with its own node ID and sends with it. Set it with #CANID or Send_CAN_ID and save; every driver ships as node 0. Use 0 to 13: the bootloader reserves 14 and 15, and a driver on one of those cannot be updated over CAN.
    • Command ID, bits 6 to 1: which message, 0 to 63. The CAN reference lists all of them.
    • Error bit, bit 0: in a frame from the driver, 1 while any error is active. In a frame from the host it is ignored.

    So a host sends Send_data_pack_1 (command 2) to node 3 with the identifier (3 << 7) | (2 << 1) = 0x184, and the driver answers with command 3 under (3 << 7) | (3 << 1) | error. A lower node ID wins the bus arbitration.

    Multi-byte values are big-endian: the most significant byte is in data[0] and goes first. Floats are IEEE 754 single precision. Flags inside a byte are numbered from the most significant bit.

    Three kinds of message¶

    • Send_…: the host sends data. Some of these are answered (a motion command with Respond_data_pack_1, the gripper command with Respond_Gripper_data_pack); most are not.
    • Send_Respond_…: the host asks for data with a remote frame (the RTR bit set, no data) carrying the command ID; the driver answers with a data frame with the same command ID.
    • Respond_…: frames only the driver sends: the answers above, and the heartbeat.

    A control loop¶

    Send Send_data_pack_1 at a fixed rate, 100 Hz to 1 kHz, with the setpoint for this cycle; read Respond_data_pack_1 for the position, speed and current of the last cycle.

    Every motion command (and every request the driver answers) resets the watchdog, so with Send_Watchdog set to a few cycle times the motor stops when the host stops; from firmware 109 it can also hold its position, brake, coast or let a joint down slowly (The watchdog).

    Other frames, such as gains, limits, Send_Parameter or Send_Idle, do not reset it, and neither does anything sent over UART. The heartbeat is the other way round: Send_Heartbeat_Setup makes the driver send a frame on its own every N ms when it has nothing else to send, so the host can see it is alive.

    sequenceDiagram
    participant H as Host
    participant D as Driver
    H->>D: Send_Clear_Error
    H->>D: Send_Watchdog: a few cycle times
    H->>D: Send_Heartbeat_Setup: every N ms
    loop every cycle, 100 Hz to 1 kHz
        H->>D: Send_data_pack_1: the setpoint, with the feedforwards
        D-->>H: Respond_data_pack_1: position, speed, current, the error bit
    end
    D-->>H: heartbeat, whenever nothing else was sent for N ms
    Note over D: no motion command within the watchdog time: watchdog error, the motor stops

    Parameters, identity and telemetry¶

    From firmware 109 a host can also:

    • set and read any setting by its number with Send_Parameter and Send_Get_Parameter (IDs 36 and 37, the answer on 38), including the gains and limits in force, which it could not read back before (Parameters and buffers);
    • ask a drive what it is with Send_Respond_Identity (ID 39): product, tool, firmware release, pole pairs, whether it is calibrated;
    • poll temperature, supply voltage, errors and current in one frame with Send_Respond_Telemetry (ID 40);
    • record signals inside the control loop and read them with Send_Buffer_Read (ID 41) (Status and diagnostics).

    From a PC¶

    The CANvas adapter shows up as a serial port and speaks slcan; python-can opens it with bustype='slcan'. The Python library wraps the whole protocol, see With Python. Any CAN adapter that python-can supports works the same way.

    Several drivers¶

    Give each driver its own node ID before you put them on one bus (a bus with two node 0s cannot tell them apart): connect one driver at a time over UART, #CANID 3, #Save. Terminate the first and the last node, and wire the daisy chain as in Power and the CAN daisy chain.

    The watchdog¶

    The watchdog stops the motor when the host stops talking: a host program that crashes, a cable that comes out, a USB adapter that hangs. It is a timer in the driver.

    Every valid motion frame and every request the driver answers starts it again; when it runs out, the driver raises the watchdog error and does what its action says. From firmware 109 the action can hold the position, brake, coast, or let a joint down slowly; before, the driver always disabled the motor, which then coasted.

    Setting it¶

    motor.Send_Watchdog(_rate=100, _action='Hold')   # 100 ms, then hold the position
    The library knows all five actions from 1.21: 'Idle', 'Hold', 'Brake', 'Coast' and 'Descend'. 1.20 and older know only 'Idle', 'Hold' and 'Brake'.

    Send_Watchdog (ID 15): the time in ms (4 bytes) and the action (1 byte). Or the parameters 6 (time) and 7 (action) with Send_Parameter.

    Watchdog ms in the settings.

    0 ms switches it off, the default. Set it to a few of your host's cycle times: with a 100 Hz loop, 30 to 100 ms. Send_Save_config keeps it, and a kept watchdog runs from power-up: a host that does not start sending within the time finds the driver in watchdog error and clears it with Send_Clear_Error.

    What starts the timer again: Send_data_pack_1, Send_data_pack_PD, Send_gripper_data_pack, the status requests (Send_Respond_…), Send_Get_Parameter and Send_Buffer_Read. Setting the watchdog time (Send_Watchdog, parameter 6) starts a fresh window, and so does Send_Save_config, whose save takes time.

    What does not: the other setting frames (gains, limits, Idle, Clear, Settings, Send_Parameter) and every UART command (known issue KI-11). While the encoder table or the anti-cogging is measured, the driver feeds it itself.

    What a trip does¶

    Action Name What the driver does
    0 legacy what firmware 107 and older did: the motor driver is disabled and the motor coasts
    1 hold holds the position the motor had at the trip, for the fallback time; then the after-action
    2 brake shorts the phases at once; the braking current is not limited
    3 coast leaves the phases open at once: the motor turns freely
    4 descend applies the fallback current, but never lets the motor turn faster than the fallback speed, for the fallback time; then the after-action

    Any other action byte acts as 0. 0 is also what an unchanged host sends: the Python library's default, and what the PAR6 runtime sends.

    Three rules keep a gentler action from softening the response to a real fault:

    • Only a trip of the watchdog alone gets the action. If another error is active as well (E-stop, encoder, supply voltage, temperature, not calibrated, a gripper E-stop), the driver gives the legacy response.
    • Hold and descend take over only from a running motor: position, velocity, current, PD or gripper mode. A driver that was idle when the watchdog ran out gives the legacy response: the motor coasts, also when it braked in idle (#Brake 1). From firmware 110 that holds also when a motion frame comes before the error is cleared; on 109 such a frame started the hold or the descend late, for the whole fallback time.
    • Hold and descend always end after the fallback time, at most 60 s, so a lost host never leaves a motor driven for good. The after-action follows: 0 legacy, 2 brake or 3 coast.

    The driver reports the watchdog error the whole time, and Send_Clear_Error (or #Clear) ends the action at once and goes to idle; the next motion frame takes over.

    Hold¶

    Hold keeps the position loop on the position the shaft had at the trip. Coming from position or velocity mode it keeps the velocity integral too, so a joint under load does not sag at the moment of the trip.

    A host that recovers within the fallback time (a USB hiccup) finds the joint where it left it. On the bench a gimbal motor held within 6 ticks; with a load pulling on its shaft the holding current rose to 440 mA while the shaft crept 59 ticks.

    Descend¶

    Descend applies the fallback current and limits the speed in both directions to the fallback speed. Inside the cap the speed limiter's integral relaxes back to zero (within about 0.25 s), so it holds back only what keeps the speed at the cap and never more:

    • a joint under gravity with 0 mA lowers itself at no more than the cap speed and rests on whatever stops it;
    • a gripper with its gripping current keeps its grip, and its jaws cannot slam shut;
    • a free motor with a current turns at about the cap speed: on the bench a gimbal motor with 200 mA and a 1000 ticks/s cap turned at 1145 to 1163 ticks/s, about 15 % over the cap, which is soft because the limiter's integral relaxes inside it.

    It starts with the velocity integral at zero, so gravity can take the joint down.

    The fallback¶

    Four values, kept by Send_Save_config in settings block 2:

    Parameter #WdFb position What Default
    2 1st how long hold or descend runs, ms (0 to 60000) 2000
    3 2nd the current descend applies, mA (signed) 0
    4 3rd the speed cap of descend, ticks/s (0 to 65535) 1000
    5 4th the after-action: 0 legacy, 2 brake, 3 coast 0 

    #WdFb 2000,0,1000,2
#Save
    Four numbers, commas, no spaces. #WdFb alone prints them, and #Info prints the watchdog with them.

    motor.Send_Watchdog_Fallback(2000, current_mA=0, speed=1000, after='Brake')
motor.Send_Save_config()
    Library 1.21 and newer.

    Choosing an action¶

    • A bare motor on the bench, a conveyor, a wheel: brake (2) stops it fastest; coast (3) or legacy (0) lets it run out.
    • A joint that carries a load: hold (1) for a second or two, so a short loss of the host does not move it, then descend (4) with 0 mA for a joint that gravity pulls down, or brake for one it does not. Choose the cap speed for a joint you can still catch by hand.
    • A gripper: descend with the gripping current, its sign the closing direction, keeps an object held.

    These suggestions are untested on an assembled robot arm; try the action on your machine before you rely on it. Brake is not current-limited (known issue KI-12): a load that drives a braking motor fast drives a large current through the phases; try it with the motor still first.

    Seeing a trip¶

    A capture armed with the error trigger (parameter 28 = 2) records the moments before and after a trip, the watchdog's included: Status and diagnostics.

    With Python¶

    The Spectral-BLDC library wraps the CAN protocol: one method per message, and the driver's answers unpacked into attributes. It runs on Windows, Linux and macOS with any adapter python-can supports, such as the CANvas adapter over slcan.

    Installing¶

    In a virtual environment:

    py -m venv .venv
.venv\Scripts\activate
pip install Spectral-BLDC
    The CANvas adapter is COM and a number (Device Manager, Ports (COM & LPT)): channel='COM41'.

    python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
pip install Spectral-BLDC
sudo usermod -aG dialout $USER     # access to the serial port; log out and in again once
    The CANvas adapter is /dev/ttyACM0 (ls /dev/ttyACM* lists it): channel='/dev/ttyACM0'.

    python3 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
pip install Spectral-BLDC
    The CANvas adapter is /dev/cu.usbmodem and a number (ls /dev/cu.usbmodem* lists it): channel='/dev/cu.usbmodem1101', with your number.

    Check

    python -c "import Spectral_BLDC" returns without an error message.

    The source and the examples are in Spectral-BLDC-Python. For joints with a gear ratio and an offset, the Source Robotics Toolbox (pip install SourceRoboticsToolbox) turns encoder ticks into joint angles.

    The first program¶

    import Spectral_BLDC as Spectral
import time

# the adapter: a CANvas on COM41 (Windows) or /dev/ttyACM0 (Linux), 1 Mbit/s
comms = Spectral.CanCommunication(bustype='slcan', channel='COM41', bitrate=1000000)
# the driver with node ID 0
motor = Spectral.SpectralCAN(node_id=0, communication=comms)

motor.Send_Clear_Error()

# A configured, calibrated motor with a clear shaft: command one turn for five seconds.
# Each command requests a fresh position/speed/current reply; heartbeat is not required.
try:
    deadline = time.monotonic() + 5.0
    next_cycle = time.monotonic()
    while time.monotonic() < deadline:
        motor.Send_data_pack_1(Position=16384, Speed=0, Current=0)
        reply_deadline = min(deadline, time.monotonic() + 0.02)
        while time.monotonic() < reply_deadline:
            message, ids = comms.receive_can_messages(
                timeout=max(0.0, reply_deadline - time.monotonic()))
            if message is None:
                print("No motion feedback in this cycle; check the bus and node ID")
                break
            if ids.node_id != 0 or ids.command_id != 3:  # Respond_data_pack_1
                continue
            motor.UnpackData(message, ids)
            print(motor.position, motor.speed, motor.current, ids.error_bit)
            if ids.error_bit:
                raise RuntimeError("Driver fault: inspect State_of_Errors before retrying")
            break
        next_cycle += 0.02
        time.sleep(max(0.0, next_cycle - time.monotonic()))
finally:
    motor.Send_Idle()                    # also on Ctrl-C; the shaft no longer holds torque

    receive_can_messages returns the raw python-can message and a tuple with the node ID, the command ID and the error bit of the identifier; UnpackData decodes the message into the attributes the message carries.

    This example resends the same setpoint at 50 Hz for five seconds, prints only matching motion feedback, and puts the motor in idle when it ends. Idle releases torque: support any attached mechanism before running it. A missing reply is reported without claiming that a move finished. Configure and calibrate the driver first (Quick start).

    Check

    The program prints a line of position, speed, current and error bit for each cycle, the position moves to 16384 and the error bit stays 0. No motion feedback in this cycle means no answer came back: the port, the node ID and the bus, see Over CAN.

    What is where¶

    Library call Message What you get back
    Send_data_pack_1(Position, Speed, Current) Send_data_pack_1 position, speed, current
    Send_data_pack_PD(...) Send_data_pack_PD position, speed, current
    Send_Respond_Encoder_data() Send_Respond_Encoder_data position, speed (32-bit)
    Send_Respond_State_of_Errors() Send_Respond_State_of_Errors the error attributes
    Send_gripper_data_pack(...) Send_gripper_data_pack gripper_position, gripper_current, the gripper state bits
    Send_Limits(...), Send_Velocity_Gains(...), ... the setting messages nothing; Send_Save_config() keeps them

    The complete map of calls to messages is in the Python library reference, and each message's bytes in the CAN reference.

    Several motors¶

    One CanCommunication per bus, one SpectralCAN per driver with its node ID. Send to each, then read the bus: every answer carries the node ID in ids.node_id, so route it to the right object.

    motors = [Spectral.SpectralCAN(node_id=i, communication=comms) for i in range(3)]
for m in motors:
    m.Send_Respond_Encoder_data()
for _ in motors:
    message, ids = comms.receive_can_messages(timeout=0.1)
    if message is not None:
        motors[ids.node_id].UnpackData(message, ids)

    The Guides build on this: a quadruped leg, gravity compensation, bilateral teleoperation.

    Working in Motor GUI¶

    Everyday use of the driver from Motor GUI: moving the motor, watching it, recording what it does, running several motors, and the few things that only work from the console.

    Moving the motor¶

    The jog controls send setpoints in the matching control mode: Jog velocity turns the motor at a speed in encoder ticks per second (16384 is one revolution per second), Jog position moves it to a position in ticks, Jog current applies a torque in mA.

    Send setpoint sends a value in the mode the driver is in. Idle stops the motor; Control modes says what each mode does. With Live stream while dragging the setpoint follows the slider, which is the quickest way to feel a tuning change.

    Watching it¶

    The plots show position, speed and current as the driver reports them. Pan and zoom to a move; hover for the value. A step in the position setpoint shows overshoot and settling at once, a speed step shows the velocity loop's response; Current limit and loop tuning uses them step by step.

    Recording¶

    A recording keeps the telemetry stream to a file: New recording, run the move, Stop recording. Record the response before and after a tuning change to compare them, or a failure to attach to a bug report (Report a problem).

    Clearing errors¶

    The errors the driver reports are listed with the state; Clear errors sends what #Clear does. An error whose cause is still present comes straight back; Errors and recovery says what each one needs.

    Several motors¶

    Over the CANvas adapter the GUI sees every driver on the bus and addresses each by its node ID.

    Give each driver its own ID over the serial adapter first (the CAN node ID in the settings, then Save), terminate the first and the last node, and set a watchdog (Watchdog ms) on a bus that drives something that must not run away: Power and the CAN daisy chain, Over CAN.

    Gripper¶

    A driver in gripper mode shows the gripper controls: calibrate the jaws, a position from open to closed, a speed, a current limit, and the state the gripper reports. No gripper connection means the driver is not in gripper mode or not on the bus. Gripper mode.

    Firmware updates¶

    Firmware update lists the releases from GitHub and flashes one through the bootloader; on a bus, Scan bootloaders finds the boards. Updating through the bootloader.

    The console¶

    Anything the GUI has no control for is one # command away in the console, with the driver's answer below it; the whole set is in the UART commands reference. The cyclic output (#Cyc) is what the GUI's telemetry stream uses over serial, so do not start it by hand while the plots run.

    Reading a fault¶

    Check

    After recovery the fault flag returns to 0 and the state shows No errors. If it returns, stop and follow Errors and recovery.

    Errors and recovery¶

    When the driver detects a fault it disables the motor, goes to idle, turns the status LED on steadily and sets the error bit in every CAN frame it sends. A disabled motor coasts, also when #Brake 1 is set. The driver stays there until the error is cleared.

    Seeing the error¶

    • The LED: on and not flashing means error mode (or not calibrated). Three short flashes and a pause is normal operation; fast flashing, about 10 times a second, is calibration.
    • #Error prints In error state! or No errors!, followed by one line per active error.
    • Send_Respond_State_of_Errors returns every flag as a bit; the error bit of any frame says whether there is one.
    Normal operation Calibration Error mode

    In error mode the LED does not flash: it stays on, as in the photo on the right.

    The errors¶

    Error #Error line Cause What to do
    Vbus Vbus error the supply voltage is below 9 V or above #Vmax (29 V after #Default). From firmware 109 a low voltage is not checked for the first 500 ms after power-up, while the supply ramps up check the supply and the cable; a decelerating motor can push the voltage up
    Encoder Encoder error the magnet is not detected or the reading is invalid magnet distance and type, see The encoder magnet
    Driver Drv error, and FAULT pin is LOW: the gate driver reports a fault while the fault lasts (firmware 107 and older printed FAULT pin is HIGH) the MOSFET driver's own protection tripped: undervoltage, thermal shutdown or overcurrent lower the current limit, cool the driver, check for a short between the motor leads
    Velocity Velocity error the measured speed exceeded 20,000,000 ticks/s (about 1220 revolutions per second), a fixed limit that #Vlimit does not change a runaway or an encoder fault: check the magnet and the encoder, see The encoder magnet
    Temperature Temp error the thermistor reads more than #Tlim (75 °C by default) or less than −20 °C, with #Term 1 let the motor cool; lower the current limit
    E-stop Received ESTOP command Send_ESTOP was received, or from firmware 110 #Estop over UART clear it when it is safe
    Watchdog Watchdog error no motion command or answered request over CAN within the watchdog time (Send_Watchdog); what the motor does then is the watchdog's action, see The watchdog the host stopped sending; fix the host, then clear. See The watchdog
    Wrong product Wrong firmware for this board (product lock) from firmware 109: this board was calibrated by the other product (a STEPFOC firmware on a Spectral Micro, or the other way round), so the firmware drives nothing (The product lock) flash the right firmware; it does not clear otherwise
    Not calibrated Not calibrated the motor has not been calibrated since #Default, or the EEPROM is invalid run #Cal and #Save
    Current Current error reserved; this firmware never raises it —

    Clearing¶

    #Clear or Send_Clear_Error clears the flags and goes to idle. An error whose cause is still present comes straight back: a Vbus error at 2 V stays until the supply is right; once the supply is 24 V, one #Clear returns the driver to normal operation.

    The calibration error clears only by calibrating (or, for a motor you know is calibrated, #Calibrated 1).

    The watchdog¶

    The watchdog is set with Send_Watchdog over CAN, or with parameters 6 (time) and 7 (action): over UART, #Par 6,<ms> and #Par 7,<action>. It counts the motion commands (Send_data_pack_1, Send_data_pack_PD, Send_gripper_data_pack) and the requests the driver answers (the Send_Respond_ remote frames, Send_Get_Parameter, Send_Buffer_Read).

    Other CAN frames and UART commands do not reset it. Send_Save_config keeps it over a power cycle, like the heartbeat interval. What the driver does when it runs out is the watchdog's action (The watchdog).

    A driver with a saved watchdog that is used over UART only, or by a host that sends no motion command, raises a watchdog error each time the watchdog time runs out, also right after #Clear.

    To turn it off, send Send_Watchdog with 0 and then Send_Save_config over CAN. Over UART, #Par 6,0 and then #Save do the same; nothing else is changed.

    When the driver does not answer¶

    • No LED at all: no power. Check DC+ and DC− and the cable orientation.
    • LED on, no answer on UART: the baud rate (256000), TX and RX crossed, the adapter's port.
    • No answer on CAN: the node ID, the termination (about 60 Ω across the bus, unpowered), 1 Mbit/s on the adapter.
    • #Info shows -1 or nonsense: the EEPROM holds values the firmware does not expect, usually from an older release (see Saving the configuration); #Default, then calibrate again.

    Troubleshooting goes through the symptoms one by one.

    Status and diagnostics¶

    What the driver tells about itself, from firmware 109: what is in force, who it is, its state in one frame, how long its control loop takes, and recordings of its own signals made inside the control loop. The error flags and what to do about each are on Errors and recovery.

    What is in force¶

    #Info ends with the section In force:, and parameter 79 holds the same as bits (What is in force: parameter 79):

    Line Says
    encoder table: on, error … deg p-p, measured, off or not measured (Encoder table and anti-cogging)
    anti-cogging: on (harmonics), on (table), identified, off or not identified, and , saved when it is the saved set
    current-sense zero trim: the two trims in counts, and whether the zero measured with the bridge switching is in use
    delay compensation: the commutation delay in µs, or off (Commutation delay compensation)
    speed estimate: moving average, PLL, … Hz or direct PLL, … Hz (The speed estimate)
    input filter:, integral deadband: parameters 74 and 75, or off
    running: only while one runs: calibration, encoder table, anti-cogging identification, or the runs after the calibration

    Before that section #Info prints, beside what it printed before:

    • Build: the git commit the firmware was built from, 7 hex digits, with a + when it was built with uncommitted changes. Board ID: this board, 8 hex digits made from the chip's unique number; it never changes, and it is not the bootloader's board ID (0 to 13). Calibration ID: a new number at every successful calibration, kept by Save; 00000000 before the first. Over CAN they are parameters 84, 86 and 87; parameter 85 is the build time in Unix seconds.
    • CAN RX overruns and CAN TX dropped: frames the driver lost because its receive buffer was full, and answers it gave up after waiting 1 ms for a free transmit mailbox. Both should stay 0; they count from power-up. From firmware 109 the CAN controller passes only the frames addressed to the driver's own node ID, and the driver sends from all three transmit mailboxes, so a busy bus costs it less.
    • Product: this firmware, Spectral Micro.
    • Current sense zero: the zeros in use, in ADC counts from 2048, measured at start-up with the bridge off (one count is about 1.6 mA), and what the second measurement with the bridge switching found. On the bench the zeros were −8 and −7 counts, and the current read 3.9 mA with nothing flowing, where it read −20 to +19 mA before. A zero more than 200 counts from 2048 is not trusted: the driver then uses 2048 and says not measured or out of range. The current sensors need the motor supply. If the driver starts without it (USB plugged in before the supply), it waits up to half a second for the supply, then uses 2048 and says no motor supply at start-up: 2048 used; measured once the supply is there. When the supply comes, the driver measures the zero by itself at rest, coasting for about 100 ms even if it is set to brake, and the line shows the new zeros. The Vbus error the late supply latched stays until #Clear.
    • Tool ID, Watchdog: … ms, action …, fallback …, Settings block 2: ok and Anti-cogging: …, scale … % with whether it is saved.

    Who the drive is: the identity frame¶

    Send_Respond_Identity (ID 39, a remote frame) answers with the product (1 STEPFOC, 2 Spectral Micro), the tool id, the firmware release, the hardware version, the pole pairs, whether it is calibrated, and eight flags: set up as a gripper, brakes in idle, coils reversed, settings block 2 available, wrong product, current-sense zero measured, anti-cogging saved, switching zero in use (Send_Respond_Identity).

    A host can read it before it commands or flashes a drive, to know it talks to the drive it expects.

    The tool id says what the drive is built into:

    • 0: none (a joint or a bare driver);
    • 1: an SSG-48;
    • 11 to 33: an MSG (the tens digit the rail, 1 = 100, 2 = 150, 3 = 200 mm; the units digit the stepper, 1 = 21.5, 2 = 40, 3 = 60 mm);
    • 200 to 254: your own tools.

    Set it with #Tool or parameter 1 and save.

    Everything at once: telemetry¶

    Send_Respond_Telemetry (ID 40, a remote frame) answers with the temperature, the supply voltage, both error bytes and the current, all from the same moment: one request instead of four, for a host that watches many drives (Send_Respond_Telemetry).

    The product lock¶

    STEPFOC and the Spectral Micro use the same bootloader and the same file name for their firmware, and each firmware drives the other board wrongly: the Spectral Micro firmware on a STEPFOC drove a coil at about 1.7 A in every mode on the bench.

    From firmware 109 a board remembers which product calibrated it, a stamp in the EEPROM written at the first start-up of a calibrated board and at every save.

    A firmware that finds the other product's stamp, or no stamp but a calibration its own product cannot make (the Spectral Micro firmware treats 50 pole pairs or more as a STEPFOC calibration), reads and writes nothing more, drives no pin (its gate driver is enabled only after the check) and starts no control loop.

    It stays reachable: it reports an error, Wrong firmware for this board (product lock) in #Error, the wrong_product bit in the error state and the identity, and a warning in #Info. Flash the right firmware and the board runs as before; nothing was written.

    #Product shows the stamp. On a board the rule misjudges, #Product claim stamps it as this product and #Product clear removes the stamp; power-cycle after either. Both are UART commands only.

    How long the control loop takes¶

    The control loop runs every 200 µs (5000 Hz) and has to finish inside that. #Info prints the last loop's time. On the bench an early build of firmware 109 took about 80 µs of the 200; anti-cogging and the commutation delay compensation each add some 10 µs (measured on STEPFOC).

    The section timing and the register fast paths of the STEPFOC firmware (its parameters 66 to 72) are not in the Spectral Micro firmware.

    A capture¶

    The capture records three signals inside the control loop, sample by sample, 1024 samples per channel: what the loop saw and did, which a host polling over the bus cannot see. Parameters 24 to 33 set it up and report on it, Send_Buffer_Read with kind 0 reads it (Reading the capture; the signals are listed under Capture and histogram).

    A step response, 1024 samples of speed, current and position error, one every 2 loops (0.41 s):

    import time
import Spectral_BLDC as Spectral

comms = Spectral.CanCommunication(bustype='slcan', channel='COM41', bitrate=1000000)
motor = Spectral.SpectralCAN(node_id=0, communication=comms)

def read_all(seconds):
    end = time.time() + seconds
    while time.time() < end:
        message, ids = comms.receive_can_messages(timeout=0.01)
        if message is not None:
            motor.UnpackData(message, ids)

for number, value in ((24, 4), (25, 1), (26, 7), (27, 2), (28, 1)):
    motor.Send_Parameter(number, value)     # A speed, B Iq, C position error; every 2nd loop; start now
motor.Send_data_pack_1(1000, 0, 0)          # the step: to position 1000
read_all(0.5)
motor.Send_Get_Parameter(32)                # how many samples
read_all(0.1)
count = motor.parameters.get(32, 0)
motor.Send_Buffer_Read(0, 0, count)         # kind 0, from sample 0
read_all(3.0)
for i in sorted(motor.capture):
    speed, iq, position_error = motor.capture[i]
    print(i, speed, iq, position_error)

    Trigger 2 records up to an error instead: armed with a pre-trigger, it keeps the moments before a trip, the watchdog's included, and the time after it. The capture calls (Send_Parameter, Send_Get_Parameter, Send_Buffer_Read) need the Spectral-BLDC library 1.21 or newer.

    Memory¶

    The drive's microcontroller has 128 KB of flash, of which the bootloader takes 12 KB, and 20 KB of RAM. Firmware 109 uses about 91.5 % of the 116 KB application flash and 65 % of the RAM for fixed data, which leaves about 7 KB for the stack and the heap.

    Parameter 83 (#Par 83, or Send_Get_Parameter(83)) is the margin check: the bytes of that RAM the stack has never reached since start-up. The stack needs about 1.5 to 2 KB; read parameter 83 after the drive has run its hardest work (a calibration, the encoder table, the anti-cogging, sound) and see that plenty is left.

    A histogram¶

    The histogram adds one signal into 256 bins over the electrical angle or over one motor turn, every control loop, until it is stopped or a bin holds 65535 samples (about an hour): the mean per bin shows what repeats with the angle, such as the cogging current at a steady speed.

    Parameter 35 picks the signal, 34 starts it (1 electrical angle, 2 motor turn) and stops it (0), 36 counts the samples, and Send_Buffer_Read with kind 1 reads the count and the sum of each bin. The anti-cogging identification uses it the same way.

    Sound¶

    From firmware 109 the driver plays sound through its motor: tones, chords, tunes from a queue it plays by itself, and recorded sound such as speech. A current at an audio frequency in the windings makes a small force at that frequency, and the motor, and above all whatever it is fixed to, radiate it as sound.

    How it works¶

    • Where: the driver adds the sound to its output voltage, after the current loop. By default it goes on the q axis (parameter 82 = 1), where it makes torque: the rotor rocks at the tone's pitch. On the bench that was much louder than the d axis (82 = 0), which makes no torque at all.
    • When: only while the driver holds or moves: position, velocity, current or PD mode. Idle, or any error, stops all sound. A joint keeps holding its pose while it plays.
    • Tones: up to 4 at once (channels 0 to 3), a chord on one motor, each with its own pitch, loudness and length.
    • Loudness is given as a current, in mA. The coil's impedance rises with the pitch (√(R² + (2πfL)²), from the motor's R and L), so the same voltage would sound much quieter high than low; the driver turns the current into the voltage that pitch needs, and a note is as loud low as high.
    • Pitch: 20 Hz up to just below 2500 Hz, half the 5000 Hz control loop. Tones are clean up to about a third of the loop rate; melodies sit well between C4 and C6 (262 to 1047 Hz).
    • The queue: 31 events, each setting one channel's pitch and loudness and then waiting. The control loop plays them with loop-exact timing, so the host's own timing never reaches the music: it loads the queue and starts it.
    • Samples: recorded sound, 8 bits, played from a 1024-place buffer at a rate the host sets, up to the loop rate. Speech comes through recognisable but muffled: the telephone band reaches 3400 Hz, the motor 2.5 kHz.
    • The cap: all sound together is limited to the voltage of parameter 81, 2000 mV by default; 0 refuses sound. On a gimbal motor's high resistance the cap comes early: 250 mA in a 7 Ω coil already takes 1.75 V. None of the sound settings is saved.

    A tone over UART¶

    #Tone 440,250,1000      440 Hz, 250 mA, 1000 ms
#Tone 880,200,0         until stopped
#Tone off               stops all sound

    From idle, a calibrated driver first holds the position it is at and says so (Holding the position while it plays (#Idle releases it)); #Idle releases it afterwards. Left out, the loudness is 200 mA and the length 500 ms.

    Tone: not played means the driver is not calibrated or in an error, the pitch is outside 20 Hz to half the loop rate, or parameter 81 is 0.

    Over CAN and from Python¶

    Send_Sound (ID 44) carries every operation: a tone now, an event for the queue, play the queue, play the samples, stop. Send_Sound_Samples (ID 45) fills the sample buffer (Sound in the CAN reference). The Python calls below need the Spectral-BLDC library 1.21 or newer, as does its music module (note names, ringtone melodies, MIDI files, WAV files).

    Over CAN the host makes the driver hold first, for example with a position frame to where the motor is:

    import Spectral_BLDC as Spectral
from Spectral_BLDC import music

comms = Spectral.CanCommunication(bustype='slcan', channel='COM41', bitrate=1000000)
motor = Spectral.SpectralCAN(node_id=0, communication=comms)


def read_answers(seconds=0.1):
    message, ids = comms.receive_can_messages(timeout=seconds)
    while message is not None:
        motor.UnpackData(message, ids)
        message, ids = comms.receive_can_messages(timeout=0.005)


motor.Send_Respond_Encoder_data()                # where the motor is
read_answers()
motor.Send_data_pack_1(motor.position, 0, 0)     # hold it there
read_answers()

motor.Send_Tone(440, current_mA=250, duration_ms=1000)        # one tone
for channel, note in enumerate(('C4', 'E4', 'G4')):            # a chord: three channels at once
    motor.Send_Tone(music.note_hz(note), current_mA=200, duration_ms=1500, channel=channel)

    A tune goes into the queue and plays by itself. A melody becomes two events per note (the note, then a short silence, so repeated notes are heard as two), so up to 15 notes fit at once:

    name, melody = music.parse_rtttl('scale:d=8,o=5,b=120:c,d,e,f,g,a,b,c6')
for channel, freq, current, wait in music.melody_events(melody, current_mA=250):
    motor.Send_Sound_Event(freq, current, wait, channel=channel)
motor.Send_Sound_Play()

    For a longer tune, keep loading events while it plays: bits 0 to 7 of parameter 80 say how many are still queued. music.read_midi reads a MIDI file, music.split_voices splits it into lines for the four channels, and music.lines_events makes the queue's events from them.

    Recorded sound: convert a WAV file to samples at the rate the motor plays, fill the buffer, start it, and keep it filled:

    rate = 2500
samples = music.wav_samples('hello.wav', rate, coil_tau_s=0.00149 / 7.0, clip_percentile=0.95)
motor.Send_Samples(samples[:1000])               # fill the buffer first
motor.Send_Samples_Play(rate, full_scale_mV=2000)
sent = 1000
while sent < len(samples):
    motor.Send_Get_Parameter(80)
    read_answers(0.02)
    buffered = (motor.parameters.get(80, 0) >> 8) & 0x7FF
    room = 1000 - buffered
    if room > 0:
        motor.Send_Samples(samples[sent:sent + room])
        sent += room

    coil_tau_s is the coil's L ÷ R (here 1.49 mH and 7.0 Ω, the motor of the calibration example): the function raises the high frequencies the coil would otherwise swallow, and clip_percentile levels speech so that it plays about four times louder.

    At 2500 samples a second a driver takes about 313 frames a second, some 4 % of the bus. When the buffer runs dry the playing stops and bit 27 of parameter 80 says so.

    Send_Sound and Send_Sound_Samples do not reset the watchdog: with a watchdog set, keep sending a status request while the sound plays.

    Limits and care¶

    • Fix the motor to something that rings. A box or a table is far louder than a motor in free air, and a loose motor jumps and distorts the sound. On the STEPFOC bench the motor was taped down.
    • Keep tones short and moderate. #Tone and Send_Tone default to 200 mA; the bench played notes at 250 mA. The sound current comes on top of the holding current and heats the motor like any other current; a motor is made to turn, and long loud tones are hard on it.
    • On the q axis a tone is a small torque ripple: a held joint vibrates slightly at the tone's pitch. Choose the d axis (parameter 82 = 0) when that matters; it is quieter.
    • The cap, parameter 81, limits all sound together; the output also clamps at the supply voltage.

    Sound has not been tested on a Spectral Micro yet; the code is the same as STEPFOC's. On the STEPFOC bench (2026-11-08, a bare NEMA 17 taped down), tones, scales, a tune from the queue, four-note chords and streamed speech all played.

    The q axis was much louder than the d axis, speech was audible but muffled, and there were no errors, no warming and only small vibrations.

    The arm as an instrument¶

    Every joint of a robot can play its own line, and because the sound rides on top of the control, the arm keeps holding its pose while it plays.

    Load each driver's queue first, then start them one after another: the starts are meant to fall within about a millisecond of each other, and from then on each driver keeps its own time (whether they stay together over a whole song is still to be measured).

    Split a MIDI file into as many lines as there are channels, four per driver, and give each driver its four. Through a gearbox the sound changes with the joint; try each joint's range. Playing on an assembled arm has not been tested yet.

    Checking what plays¶

    Parameter Says
    79, bit 24 sound is playing
    80 bits 0-7 events queued, 8-18 samples buffered, 24 a tone sounds, 25 the queue plays, 26 samples play, 27 the samples ran dry
    81 the sound's voltage cap, mV
    82 the axis: 1 q, 0 d

    Gripper mode¶

    A driver can run a gripper instead of a joint: the same board, with a state machine that moves a jaw to a position between open and closed, at a speed, with a current limit that becomes the grip force, and that reports when it holds something. The SSG-48 gripper is built on the Spectral Micro this way.

    Turning it on¶

    #Gripper 1
#Save

    #Default clears the flag again. Over CAN the flag is parameter 92 from firmware 110: write 1 while the driver is idle (in any other mode the write is refused), then Send_Save_config.

    A driver in gripper mode still answers every other command; the gripper commands only work with the flag set, except the gripper calibration, which runs with or without it.

    Calibrating the gripper¶

    After every power-up the gripper has to find its end stops: #Gripcal or Send_gripper_calibrate. The jaws open slowly at 500 mA until they stop. Then the push eases to 100 mA for a quarter of a second, and the driver reads the open end where the jaws rest against the stop. The closed end is found the same way.

    Position 0 (open) lies 32 ticks inside the open end, and 255 (closed) 16 ticks inside the closed end. So a move to 0 or 255 ends in free travel: it does not press on the stop and reports no object.

    Firmware 109 read the ends during the 500 mA push, so 0 and 255 lay inside the stops, and a full open or close kept pressing on them.

    If it has not found both ends within about 30 s it gives up, and the gripper stays uncalibrated. From firmware 110 it also stays uncalibrated when the two ends lie less than 1000 ticks apart: the jaws did not travel.

    Check the result with #Gripinfo or the gripper_calibrated bit of Respond_Gripper_data_pack. From firmware 110 parameter 93 gives the stroke between 0 and 255 in ticks, and 0 while the gripper is not calibrated. The motor itself must be calibrated (#Cal) and saved before that, once.

    While the gripper calibrates, send only empty status polls. A 5-byte Send_gripper_data_pack, Send_Clear_Error or #Clear stops the calibration without a message. From firmware 110 the next calibration starts from its first step, and #Gripcal or Send_gripper_calibrate during a calibration starts it again. Firmware 109 went on where the stopped one left off (known issue KI-7).

    Moving¶

    Over UART:

    #Gripvel 100       speed, 0 to 255
#Gripcur 400       current limit of the move in mA, the grip force
#Grippos 200       go to position 200 (0 open, 255 closed); also activates the gripper

    Over CAN, Send_gripper_data_pack carries position, speed and current in one frame with the activate and action bits; the driver answers with Respond_Gripper_data_pack: the position, the measured current, whether it is moving, and the object detection:

    Object detection Meaning
    0 moving
    1 an object was detected while closing: the jaws stopped on it and hold it with the current limit
    2 an object was detected while opening
    3 the position was reached

    From firmware 110 an object (1 or 2) is reported once the jaws have stood still at nearly the move's current for 50 ms. Firmware 109 reported it at the first slow control cycle, also at the start of a move.

    From firmware 110 a move starts at the slowest speed and picks up to its own speed within 0.1 s. It slows down over the last stretch, so it arrives slowly. A new command in the same direction keeps the speed the jaws have, so a host that streams positions is not slowed.

    At the position the driver holds with a small dead zone, a quarter of a position count and at most 16 ticks, and friction keeps the jaws there: a few mA are left after a move.

    On firmware 109 the current took a second or more to settle after every move. From firmware 110 the position the driver reports is also rounded to the nearest count; 109 cut it off.

    An empty Send_gripper_data_pack (0 bytes) only asks for the state. The answer to a 5-byte command goes out before the driver acts on it, so its object detection and error bits are still those of the moment before (KI-6); the answers to the next frames show the new move.

    The action bit in the answer is a copy of the last command's action bit and stays 1 after the move ends (KI-8): the object detection tells when it has ended.

    Send_gripper_data_pack's E-stop bit raises the gripper E-stop error; #Gripstop 1 does the same over UART. The error stays: while the E-stop bit is 1 the driver raises it again on every control cycle (KI-5). To recover, clear the bit (#Gripstop 0, or a Send_gripper_data_pack with the bit 0), then #Clear or Send_Clear_Error.

    Current

    From firmware 109 the current you give a gripper move stops at the board's 2800 mA; firmware 107 and older did not limit it. The safe range depends on the gripper's mechanics and motor and is lower; the SSG-48 manual gives it for that gripper.

    From firmware 110 the move's current is the gripper's own limit. The driver's current limit (#Ilim, parameter 16) stays as you set it, and a #Save stores that. Firmware 109 wrote the current of each move into the driver's limit, and a #Save then stored the gripper's current.

    Where the details are¶

    The bytes of the two gripper frames are in the CAN reference, the UART commands in the UART reference.

    7. Firmware

    Firmware¶

    The driver's firmware is open source and released as numbered builds. Release 104 is the first release built for the bootloader: a driver that runs 104 or newer carries a bootloader and updates itself over UART or CAN. A driver that runs 101 or older needs the bootloader installed once with a programming adapter. A driver that reports 101 can be either (Drivers that report 101 says how to tell).

    In this chapter¶

    • Releases and versions: how releases are numbered, how to see which one a driver runs, what the release notes contain
    • The bootloader: what it is, how the driver boots, how it is entered, its protocol and tools
    • Updating through the bootloader: the normal update, with Motor GUI, the bootloader's command-line tool, or a PAR6 control box
    • Upgrading from older firmware: what a driver keeps when it is updated to this release, what works at once and what has to be run
    • Installing the bootloader on an older board: for a driver that runs firmware 101 or older
    • Building from source: PlatformIO, the memory layout, uploading with a programming adapter

    The release notes of every release up to the one this site describes are in the appendix, Firmware release notes.

    Releases and versions¶

    A firmware release is a number: 101, 104, 105, … Every release is a tag V<number> in the firmware repository and a GitHub release that carries the image spectral-bldc-firmware-V<number>.bin, a manifest firmware.json with the image's size, SHA-256 and the commit it was built from, and the release notes. The notes are the firmware's changelog; the appendix collects them.

    Which release does my driver run?¶

    #Info

    prints Software version: <number> among the rest; Motor GUI shows the same number in the driver's identity, and over CAN Send_Respond_Device_Info returns it as the third byte. From firmware 105 the number is the build's own version.

    Before that it was read from the EEPROM, where the last #Default wrote it, so it names the firmware that last restored the defaults, not necessarily the one running: 101 and 104 both write 101.

    Drivers that report 101¶

    A driver that reports 101 runs either release 101 from the main branch, without a bootloader, or release 104 (or an earlier build of Bootloader_main), which also writes 101 and runs above the bootloader. Drivers sold before release 105 shipped with firmware 101 or 103.

    To tell whether yours has the bootloader:

    • Watch the LED as you switch the driver on: with the bootloader it blinks for about two seconds before the firmware starts.
    • Or power it up while bl_flash.py list or Motor GUI's Scan bootloaders is polling: a driver with the bootloader answers in its first two seconds.

    With the bootloader, update it to the current release as in Updating through the bootloader; the release it ran before does not matter then. Without one, install it first: Installing the bootloader on an older board.

    Which release does this site describe?¶

    The one in the version box on the landing page and in the version selector in the header. When a newer release changes what a page says, a new version of the site is published and the previous one stays reachable from the selector. The compatibility table says which releases go with which bootloader, Python library and PAR6 runtime.

    The STEPFOC counterpart¶

    Most Spectral Micro releases have a STEPFOC release with the same changes. From release 109 the two drivers share their version numbers and are released together: the Spectral Micro went from 107 straight to 109, so there is no Spectral Micro 108.

    Before that the numbers did not always match (Spectral Micro 107 went with STEPFOC 108); the compatibility table lists the pairs.

    The two branches¶

    Releases from 104 on are built from the Bootloader_main branch of the repository, whose firmware is linked to run above the bootloader; 104 is the first of them.

    The main branch holds the firmware linked to run without a bootloader: release 101 and earlier, and a few later changes that were never tagged as a release (among them the Kt request and the voltage limit message). It is not updated.

    Version 101 of this site documents a board running 101. It also fits 104, which adds the Send_Respond_Kt request, Send_Voltage_Limit (in the release image) and the bootloader.

    The 104 release image was built from commit 20765c9 on Bootloader_main, not from the source at the tag V104, which does not have Send_Voltage_Limit yet. To move a board without a bootloader to a current release, see Installing the bootloader on an older board.

    The bootloader¶

    Every driver that runs firmware 104 or newer (104 is the first release built for the bootloader) carries a small bootloader in the first 12 KB of its flash.

    It starts the firmware on a normal boot, and it loads a new firmware over UART or CAN when a host asks, so a driver is updated without a programming adapter and without opening anything. The bootloader is open source: stm32f103c-bootloader, shared with STEPFOC.

    Memory layout¶

    Address Contents
    0x08000000 – 0x080022FF the bootloader (about 9 KB)
    0x08002C00 – 0x08002FFF the bootloader's configuration page: the board ID, the firmware's page count and CRC, the bootloader's build version
    0x08003000 – 0x0801FFFF the firmware (up to 116 KB)
    RAM 0x20001000 a magic word the bootloader sets to hand over to the firmware

    The firmware is linked to start at 0x08003000 and sets its vector table there (VECT_TAB_OFFSET=0x3000); the first 0x3000 bytes belong to the bootloader and the firmware never touches them.

    What happens at boot¶

    1. On a reset, the bootloader runs first. If the magic word is in RAM and the reset was a software reset, it checks the firmware's stack pointer and reset vector and jumps straight into the firmware: about 10 ms, the normal boot.
    2. Otherwise it initialises the clocks, CAN, UART and its watchdog, and listens for a host for 2 s.
    3. With no host, and a firmware whose stored CRC is valid, it sets the magic word and resets; the next boot takes the fast path.
    4. With a host talking, it stays as long as the host keeps talking: 3 s after the last command, 60 s once a flash is in progress. Any PING resets the timer.
    5. With no valid firmware (never flashed, or a flash that was interrupted), it stays in bootloader mode and waits. The LED is off; the driver answers nothing on #Info.

    Entering the bootloader¶

    • From the firmware: #Reset over UART or Send_Reset (command 14) over CAN. Since firmware 104 both reset the driver into the bootloader's 2 s window; the host tools send them for you.
    • At power-on: the 2 s window after every power-up, if the host is already polling.
    • Always, when there is no valid firmware.

    The board ID¶

    The bootloader has a board ID of its own, 0 to 13, stored in its configuration page. 14 and 15 do not exist for it: its command frame, identifier 0x700, decodes as node 14 in the firmware's identifier scheme, so those two node IDs are reserved.

    Give drivers node IDs 0 to 13. The firmware accepts #CANID 14 and 15, but the bootloader then keeps its old board ID and a firmware update over CAN cannot reach the driver.

    The firmware keeps the board ID in step with its CAN node ID when the configuration is saved: #Save or Send_Save_config writes the node ID into the bootloader's board ID, so a saved driver answers the host tools under its node ID. Setting the node ID alone (#CANID, Send_CAN_ID) does not: until you save, the bootloader keeps the old ID.

    A replacement board ships as ID 0; two boards with the same ID on one bus corrupt each other's flash stream, so give the new board its ID alone on the bus first (set-id in the host tool).

    Transports and protocol¶

    Over CAN (1 Mbit/s, standard 11-bit identifiers) the host commands on identifier 0x700 with 8 data bytes (board_id, cmd, two parameters). The board replies on 0x701 + board_id with 5 bytes (board_id, cmd, error code, a 16-bit parameter).

    The firmware image streams in bulk frames on (board_id << 7) | sequence, 16 frames of 8 bytes per chunk, with no reply per frame.
