    Over UART (256000 baud) the same commands travel in framed packets [0xBB][CMD][LEN][BOARD_ID][DATA…][CRC16] with the same 5-byte reply.

    Commands: WBUF (1), WPAGE (2), WCRC (3), PING (4), SET_ID (5), ERASE_APP (6), STREAM_BEGIN (7), STREAM_STATUS (8). Error codes: 0 OK, 1 invalid offset, 2 invalid CRC, 3 flash write failed, 4 invalid page number.

    A flash is: ping, reset the firmware if it answers, ping until the bootloader answers, ERASE_APP (about 3.5 s), then for each 1 KB page STREAM_BEGIN, 8 chunks of 16 frames, STREAM_STATUS, WPAGE with the page's CRC32; finally WCRC with the page count and the image's CRC32.

    The bootloader stores the CRC and reboots into the new firmware. The full wire protocol is in the bootloader repository's docs/PROTOCOL.md.

    The host side¶

    Tool Transport Use
    Motor GUI serial adapter (Web Serial) or CANvas one driver on the bench, releases fetched from GitHub
    host_tools/bl_flash.py in the bootloader repository slcan or gs_usb CAN adapters, or UART scripts, several boards, list, set-id, flash --erase-first
    the PAR6 control box's flash_motor_firmware.py and the dashboard's Firmware tab the robot's own CAN bus every joint of a robot, from the box

    Updating through the bootloader has the commands.

    Recovery¶

    An interrupted flash leaves an invalid CRC; the bootloader refuses to start the firmware and waits, so the board is not lost: flash again. A board that answers nothing at all: power-cycle it with the host already polling, to catch the 2 s window.

    The one thing the tools refuse, and that would need a programming adapter to undo, is flashing an image linked for 0x08000000 (a firmware from the main branch) over the firmware region.

    Installing it¶

    Boards that run firmware 101 or older have no bootloader. Installing the bootloader on an older board puts it there once, with a programming adapter at 0x08000000; from then on the board updates like any other.

    Updating through the bootloader¶

    A driver with the bootloader (firmware 104 or newer) is updated over the UART connector or the CAN bus: the host tells the driver to reset, the bootloader waits two seconds for a host, and the host streams the new image into it. No programming adapter, and nothing to open.

    Before you update

    • Park the mechanism: the motor loses torque during the update and until the new firmware is calibrated and commanded.
    • An update may clear the calibration (the release notes say so when it does); plan to calibrate and #Save afterwards, and note the gains #Info prints. An update to 109 keeps it, and the driver runs on it; calibrate again where the motor can turn freely, or apply the SSG-48 preset on an assembled gripper (over UART, with Gripper CAN ID set to the gripper's node): Upgrading from older firmware says why, what changes and what to run.
    • On a bus with several drivers, update each node; a bus with mixed releases behaves unpredictably.

    With Motor GUI¶

    Motor GUI in Chrome or Edge, connected over the serial adapter or the CANvas adapter: choose the release, and the GUI fetches the image and its manifest from the GitHub release, verifies the size and the SHA-256, resets the driver into its bootloader and flashes it. This is the way for one driver on the bench.

    With the bootloader's command-line tool¶

    The bootloader repository has host_tools/bl_flash.py, which flashes an image over CAN (slcan or candleLight/gs_usb adapters) or over UART:

    cd host_tools
pip install -r requirements.txt

# over CAN with a CANvas (slcan) adapter on COM6, driver with board ID 0
python bl_flash.py --transport can --can-interface slcan --channel COM6 flash spectral-bldc-firmware-V109.bin --board 0 --erase-first

# over UART on COM7
python bl_flash.py --transport uart --port COM7 --baudrate 256000 flash spectral-bldc-firmware-V109.bin --board 0 --erase-first


    cd host_tools
pip install -r requirements.txt

# over CAN with a CANvas (slcan) adapter, driver with board ID 0
python3 bl_flash.py --transport can --can-interface slcan --channel /dev/ttyACM0 flash spectral-bldc-firmware-V109.bin --board 0 --erase-first

# over UART with the serial adapter
python3 bl_flash.py --transport uart --port /dev/ttyUSB0 --baudrate 256000 flash spectral-bldc-firmware-V109.bin --board 0 --erase-first
    Your user needs the dialout group for both ports (Motor GUI: the port).

    cd host_tools
pip install -r requirements.txt

# over CAN with a CANvas (slcan) adapter, driver with board ID 0; ls /dev/cu.usbmodem* gives the name
python3 bl_flash.py --transport can --can-interface slcan --channel /dev/cu.usbmodem1101 flash spectral-bldc-firmware-V109.bin --board 0 --erase-first

# over UART with the serial adapter; ls /dev/cu.usbserial* gives the name
python3 bl_flash.py --transport uart --port /dev/cu.usbserial-0001 --baudrate 256000 flash spectral-bldc-firmware-V109.bin --board 0 --erase-first

    The tool sends the reset command to the running firmware (#Reset over UART, Send_Reset over CAN), catches the bootloader's two-second window, erases the application pages and streams the image page by page, each page verified by a CRC; list shows the boards in bootloader mode, set-id changes a board's bootloader ID. The image is the spectral-bldc-firmware-V<number>.bin from the GitHub release.

    Board ID and node ID

    The bootloader has its own board ID (0 to 13), kept in its configuration page, separate from the firmware's CAN node ID. The firmware copies the node ID into the board ID when the configuration is saved (#Save, Send_Save_config), so a saved driver's bootloader ID is its node ID. After #CANID or Send_CAN_ID without a save, --board still needs the old ID.

    Check

    After the flash, from Motor GUI or the tool, the driver starts the new firmware: #Info prints the release you flashed as Software version, and Motor GUI shows it in the driver's identity. If the driver does not answer, see When it fails.

    From a PAR6 control box¶

    A robot built on the Robot Control Box updates every joint from the box itself, over its own CAN bus, from the dashboard's Firmware tab or with tools/flash_motor_firmware.py; the PAR6 documentation describes it.

    What happens in the driver¶

    1. The host sends the reset; the firmware reboots into the bootloader.
    2. The bootloader listens for two seconds. With no host it starts the firmware; with a host it stays as long as the host keeps talking (60 s during a flash).
    3. The application pages are erased, then written page by page; each page is checked by a CRC.
    4. The bootloader checks the whole image's CRC, records it, and starts the new firmware.

    When it fails¶

    A flash that fails or is cancelled part way is not a brick: the stored CRC is invalid, so the bootloader refuses to start the firmware and waits for a host.

    Run the flash again; bl_flash.py list (or a passive scan from the control box) finds the board. A board that answers nothing: power-cycle it with the host already polling, to catch the two-second window.

    An image linked for 0x08000000 (a firmware from the main branch, release 101 and earlier) must never be flashed through the bootloader: it would overwrite the bootloader's own region if the tool did not refuse it. The tools validate the image first.

    Upgrading from older firmware¶

    Firmware 109 adds more to the Spectral Micro than any release before it: parameters, the watchdog actions, the identity and telemetry frames, captures, the current loop from one bandwidth, a choice of speed estimates, and the encoder table and anti-cogging.

    This page says what a driver keeps when it is updated to 109, what works at once, what can be run, and how to go back. Updating through the bootloader is how to flash it.

    There is no firmware 108 for the Spectral Micro: from 109 the Spectral Micro and STEPFOC share their version numbers, so a Spectral Micro goes from 107 straight to 109. The step from 109 to 110 is smaller: From 109 to 110.

    From 109 to 110¶

    Firmware 110 changes the gripper, adds #Estop and parameters 90 to 93, and saves brake or coast; nothing has to be measured again. A driver updated from 109 keeps everything it saved: the settings, the motor calibration, the encoder table and the anti-cogging. 110 keeps the idle behaviour in a slot of settings block 2 that was reserved.

    • Brake or coast (parameter 18) is saved from 110 on. A driver that has not saved it yet coasts in idle, as on 109; send #Brake 1 and #Save once if it should brake.
    • The gripper calibration is kept in RAM, as it always was: calibrate the gripper after the update, as after every power-up. Positions 0 and 255 then lie a little inside the two ends (Gripper mode).
    • Motor GUI 0.7.0 is the version made for 110: its STOP sends #Estop over UART, and its presets write parameters 90 to 92 over CAN.

    Going back to 109 is safe: 109 ignores the saved idle behaviour and coasts at every power-up again.

    What is kept¶

    Flashing never touches the EEPROM, where the configuration lives (The check at power-up). A driver updated from 106 or 107 starts with everything it had:

    • the calibration: pole pairs, R, L, the angle offset, Kt, KV, the phase order and direction, the current gains;
    • the CAN ID, the gains, the limits, the watchdog, the heartbeat, the thermistor and the gripper settings.

    Calibrate again after the update, where the motor can turn freely

    In every firmware before 109 the enable input of phase B was fed by a clock signal instead of a steady level, so phase B could drop out, also while the calibration measured the motor. A calibration made with 107 or older stored the resistance about 10 % and the inductance about 45 % too low (reference gimbal motor: about 6.4 Ω and 1.25 mH, where 109 measures about 7.0 to 7.2 Ω and 1.75 to 1.9 mH).

    The driver keeps running on such a calibration: on the bench an SSG-48 updated to 109 without a new calibration reached every target and gripped as before. Only the current gains are computed from the low values. So:

    • A motor that can turn freely: run #Cal once after the update. The current gains then follow the true values, and the encoder table and the anti-cogging are measured too.
    • An assembled SSG-48 gripper, whose motor moves the jaws: do not run #Cal. Apply Motor GUI's SSG-48 preset over UART instead: it writes 300 Hz current gains for the gripper's motor and saves. Set Gripper CAN ID to the gripper's node first: it starts at 6, the PAR6 gripper's; a PAROL6 gripper is node 0. A motor calibration needs the body opened and the linear guide with the jaws off; then use #Calonly, because #Cal also runs the encoder table and the anti-cogging.

    A driver from 101 to 105, saved before the configuration had checksums, is trusted and sealed at its first start of 109 when it was ever saved; a blank EEPROM gets the defaults. Release 101 and older from the main branch have no bootloader: such a board needs the programming adapter once (Installing the bootloader on an older board).

    The new settings start from their defaults: tool id 0, the watchdog fallback 2000 ms / 0 mA / 1000 ticks/s / legacy, parameter 56 = 0.

    What works at once¶

    These need no measurement and are on from the first start of 109:

    • the current-sense zero, measured at every start-up with the driver asleep: on the bench the current read 3.9 mA with nothing flowing, where it read −20 to +19 mA before, depending on the rotor's position;
    • undervoltage is not checked for the first 500 ms after power-up, so a supply that ramps up slowly no longer leaves a Vbus error behind (overvoltage always is);
    • the watchdog actions: the action byte the host already sends is acted on. Byte 0, what the Python library and the PAR6 runtime send, gives the legacy response, so an unchanged host sees no difference;
    • phase B's enable pin is driven properly, and the control loop keeps the gate driver awake in every driving mode: either could leave a motor that took a command and did not move, or that pushed unevenly;
    • the limits refuse a negative value, and Send_Limits and the gripper's current stop at 2800 mA;
    • setting the watchdog time starts a fresh window, so switching it on again no longer trips it at once;
    • #Default and a settings reset keep the CAN ID;
    • the first motion frame after Idle is followed (the driver's wake-up now comes with it), and a position or speed frame after another mode no longer kicks with a stale velocity integral;
    • the product lock, the new CAN commands and parameters, the identity frame, telemetry, the capture, #Info's In force:, and sound through the motor (not yet tested on a Spectral Micro).

    What can be run or switched on¶

    The encoder table and the anti-cogging are off after the update until they are measured (#Cal measures both); the speed estimate and the delay compensation are new defaults, on from the first start:

    • the encoder table and the anti-cogging (Encoder table and anti-cogging); they cannot be switched on before they are measured;
    • the direct PLL speed estimate at 100 Hz (parameters 50 and 51) and the commutation delay compensation of 234 µs (parameter 64), both on by default from 109 (Current limit and loop tuning). They change how the velocity loop behaves with the gains you had: a speed step overshoots less. Check your velocity gains after the update.

    What does not change by itself¶

    • The current gains. The saved Kpiq and Kiiq stay as they are: on a driver calibrated before 109 they were computed for 500 Hz (parameter 49 reads about 500), from a resistance and an inductance that were too low. A calibration with 109 measures both again and computes the gains at 160 Hz. #Cbw 160 on the old calibration does not replace it: it uses the old values and gives gains for a lower bandwidth than it names. 160 Hz is the new default; it was chosen on the STEPFOC bench.
    • The velocity gains stay as they are; their defaults, Kpv 0.03 and Kiv 0.0003, did not change.

    The whole upgrade¶

    1. Flash 109 (Updating through the bootloader). #Info prints the new firmware version and ends with In force:.
    2. Check that the current limit suits your motor (#Ilim): calibration puts back the limit that was in force, and the encoder table in the next step runs at 80 % of it.
    3. Calibrate, with the shaft free: #Cal, so that the resistance, the inductance and the current gains are measured again. It goes on to the encoder table and the anti-cogging, and saves everything. #Calonly is the calibration alone.
    4. After #Calonly: #Save; and if you want them, run the encoder table and the anti-cogging with the shaft free (#Encal all) and check them.

    With Motor GUI's presets: apply the preset first, then calibrate. A product preset writes its own current and velocity gains, so one applied after the calibration puts its current gains back. The Generic motor presets set soft limits and velocity gains and leave the current gains to #Cbw and the calibration.

    Going back¶

    Flashing 107 or older over 109 is safe for the configuration: the calibration and the first settings block are unchanged, and older firmware ignores everything 109 saved from byte 256 on (tool id, fallback, anti-cogging, encoder table). Flash 109 again and they are all still there.

    Not yet tested on the bench

    A downgrade and the sound on a Spectral Micro had not been tested on the bench when this page was written; the rest of this page was.

    Installing the bootloader on an older board¶

    A driver running firmware 101 or older has no bootloader: its firmware starts at the beginning of the flash. A driver that reports 101 may run 104 and have the bootloader all the same; check first, as in Drivers that report 101.

    To run any release from 104 on (104 is the first release built for the bootloader), which is linked to start above the bootloader, the bootloader must be installed once, with a programming adapter on the JTAG connector. After that, every update goes over UART or CAN.

    Warning

    Disconnect the motor supply and the UART adapter before connecting the programming adapter. The adapter powers the board at 3.3 V.

    What you need¶

    • The programming adapter (an ST-LINK with the 1.27 mm pogo-pin adapter for the JTAG pads), or any ST-LINK, J-Link or OpenOCD-compatible probe wired to 3.3 V, GND, SWDIO and SWCLK.
    • STM32CubeProgrammer or the older STM32 ST-LINK Utility on the PC, with the ST-LINK drivers.
    • The bootloader binary from the bootloader repository's bin/ folder; its README.md names the current file.

    STM32CubeProgrammer or the ST-LINK Utility, and the ST-LINK USB driver (STSW-LINK009) for the adapter.

    STM32CubeProgrammer; the ST-LINK Utility runs on Windows only. The adapter needs no driver, but your user needs access to its USB device: install the udev rules that come with STM32CubeProgrammer, or run it with sudo.

    STM32CubeProgrammer; the ST-LINK Utility runs on Windows only. The adapter needs no driver.

    Steps¶

    1. Connect the adapter to the JTAG pads and the PC.
    2. In the programmer, connect to the target (SWD). The STM32F103CB shows up with 128 KB of flash.
    3. Erase the chip, or at least the application region: the old firmware and the bootloader must not overlap.
    4. Program the bootloader binary at address 0x08000000.
    5. Disconnect the adapter, power the driver. The bootloader finds no valid application and waits in bootloader mode (the LED stays off).
    6. Flash the firmware release through the bootloader, over UART or CAN, as in Updating through the bootloader.
    7. #Info shows the new release. #Default, then calibrate (#Cal), set the current limit (#Ilim) and #Save; see Calibration.

    Check

    #Info prints the release you flashed, and a driver powered up while bl_flash.py list or Motor GUI's Scan bootloaders is polling answers in its first two seconds: the bootloader is there. If the firmware does not start, see Updating through the bootloader: when it fails.

    Memory layout

    0x08000000–0x080022FF bootloader, 0x08002C00 its configuration page (board ID, the application's CRC), 0x08003000–0x0801FFFF the application (116 KB). The bootloader keeps a magic value in RAM to hand over to the application in about 10 ms on a normal boot.

    The programmer's settings¶

    In ST-LINK Utility, Target → Settings with SWD and the default frequency works; Target → Program & Verify with the start address 0x08000000. The pictures of the old flashing procedure apply to the connection and the settings dialog:

    Settings Programming

    The same adapter uploads a firmware you build yourself, see Building from source.

    Building from source¶

    The firmware is a PlatformIO project for the Arduino core on the STM32F103CB, in the Spectral BLDC Firmware folder of the firmware repository on the Bootloader_main branch.

    Building¶

    1. Install Visual Studio Code and the PlatformIO extension.
    2. Clone the repository and check out Bootloader_main.
    3. Open the Spectral BLDC Firmware folder in VS Code; PlatformIO fetches the platform (ststm32@15.4.1) and builds with the PlatformIO Build button or pio run. The image is .pio/build/genericSTM32F103CB/firmware.bin.

    The project is linked for the bootloader: VECT_TAB_OFFSET=0x3000, the linker script ldscript_bootloader.ld, the upload offset 0x08003000 and a maximum size of 118784 bytes, in platformio.ini. A build that exceeds the size fails to link.

    Check

    PlatformIO ends the build with SUCCESS, and .pio/build/genericSTM32F103CB/firmware.bin is there. If it fails, the first error above the summary names the cause.

    Uploading¶

    PlatformIO runs on Windows, Linux and macOS alike. On Linux, an upload with the programming adapter also needs PlatformIO's udev rules (99-platformio-udev.rules, in the PlatformIO documentation), and a serial port the dialout group.

    • Through the bootloader: flash the built firmware.bin with bl_flash.py or Motor GUI as in Updating through the bootloader. This is how a release is installed, and works on any driver with the bootloader.
    • With the programming adapter: Upload in PlatformIO uses ST-LINK and writes the image at 0x08003000. The bootloader must already be on the board; the upload does not touch it. The serial monitor (the plug icon) opens the UART at 256000 with send_on_enter, so you can type commands to the driver from the same window.

    Where things are¶
    src/constants.h FIRMWARE_VERSION, the CAN command IDs, the EEPROM map, the loop and PWM frequencies
    src/common.h the controller and PID structures with their defaults
    src/communication.cpp the UART command handlers
    src/communication_CAN.cpp the CAN receive switch and the transmit functions
    src/SerialPacketParser.cpp the UART parser: it splits a line into the command and its argument (up to 107 it also kept the list of names it accepted)
    src/motor_control.cpp the control loops, the calibration routine, the gripper state machine
    src/bootloader_config.cpp the vector table offset and the reset into the bootloader
    release_guide.md how a release is cut: version bump, build, tag, GitHub release with firmware.json

    Making a release¶

    release_guide.md in the repository is the procedure: bump FIRMWARE_VERSION, build, commit the source change, tag V<number>, create the GitHub release with the image, firmware.json and the release notes. This site's tools/sync_firmware.py reads a release by its tag; the version box names the release.

    A firmware for a board without a bootloader¶

    The main branch builds a firmware linked at 0x08000000 for boards that have no bootloader. Its last tagged release is 101; it is not maintained. Install the bootloader instead: Installing the bootloader on an older board.

    8. Troubleshooting

    Troubleshooting¶

    By symptom. The error flags and what clears them are in Errors and recovery; the faults of the firmware that are known and not yet fixed, with what to do until they are, in Known issues.

    The motor does not move¶

    flowchart TD
    A([The motor does not move]) --> B{Does the driver answer?}
    B -- no --> C[The supply and its polarity, the LED;<br>UART: the baud rate, TX and RX;<br>CAN: the node ID, 1 Mbit/s, the termination]
    B -- yes --> D{Calibrated?}
    D -- no --> E[#Cal and #Save]
    D -- yes --> F{An error active? The LED on steadily}
    F -- yes --> G[#Error names it: fix the cause, then #Clear]
    F -- no --> H{A current limit above 0?}
    H -- no --> I[#Ilim to the motor's rated current, #Save]
    H -- yes --> J[Command a setpoint in a closed-loop mode;<br>#Mode and #Info show the state]

    The driver does not answer¶

    Symptom Check
    No LED the supply, DC+ and DC−, the power cable's orientation. If the cable was the wrong way round the driver is destroyed
    LED on, nothing on the terminal 256000 baud; TX to RX and RX to TX; the adapter's port; 3.3 V adapter; the line ending CR+LF
    LED on, nothing on CAN the node ID; 1 Mbit/s on the adapter; termination (about 60 Ω across CAN H and CAN L with everything unpowered); the CAN cable's orientation
    Unknown command the name's spelling and case: #Kpiq, not #kpiq; an argument with a space in it (#WdFb 2000,0,1000,2, not #WdFb 2000, 0, 1000, 2)
    Wrong firmware for this board (product lock), the LED on steadily the other product's firmware was flashed: a STEPFOC image on a Spectral Micro. Flash the Spectral Micro firmware; the configuration was not touched (The product lock)
    the LED is off and nothing works after a firmware update the bootloader has no valid firmware: flash again, see Updating through the bootloader

    Motor GUI¶

    Symptom Check
    the browser offers no port a Chromium-based browser (Chrome, Edge, Brave, Opera); Firefox and Safari have no Web Serial
    the port is listed but will not open another program holds it: a terminal, PlatformIO's monitor, the Arduino serial monitor
    the port opens, nothing appears the driver's power; TX and RX crossed on a non-Source adapter; the baud rate on a non-Source adapter (256000)
    No connection on CAN the CANvas adapter's port; the bus termination; the node ID of the driver; 1 Mbit/s
    the calibration does not start calibration runs over the serial connection only, not over CAN
    Unsaved changes after a power cycle the settings were not saved to the driver; Save writes them
    the firmware update finds no bootloader power-cycle the driver while the GUI scans, to catch the bootloader's 2 s window. A driver with firmware 101 or older has no bootloader (one that reports 101 may have it: Drivers that report 101): Installing the bootloader on an older board

    Calibration¶

    The messages and their causes are in Calibration. The ones that come back most:

    Symptom Cause
    Magnet test: Failed magnet too far, axially magnetised, or missing
    the motor wobbles left and right during calibration one phase is not connected
    Open loop: Failed the rotor did not follow the open-loop field, or the pole pairs came out outside 1 to 49: a phase open, the shaft not free, the open-loop voltage (#Openv) too low for the motor
    Commutation/alignment check failed! the motor did not respond to the commutation check: a phase open, #Iqoffset too low, a magnet that slips
    calibration succeeds, the motor then runs badly or in the wrong direction the magnet slipped on the shaft; glue it and calibrate again
    after Calibration success! the motor turns slowly by itself for about a minute the encoder table and the anti-cogging that follow a #Cal; let them finish, they save everything (What follows the calibration)
    the anti-cogging does not switch on (parameter 37 stays 0) no set is identified for that form; run #Cogcal save. After an encoder table measured on its own the set is switched off until it is identified again (Encoder table and anti-cogging)
    calibration succeeds but the LED stays on and #Error shows nothing #Info shows -1, a value from an older release (The check at power-up): #Default, calibrate again

    The motor¶

    Symptom Cause
    crunching or whining at speed the current limit or the current loop gains too high; lower #Ilim, lower #Cbw and calibrate again, or lower #Kpiq
    humming, oscillating at rest #Kpv or #Kpp too high; see Current limit and loop tuning
    weak torque #Ilim low; the supply's current limit; a calibration run at a low supply voltage (the constants are off)
    the motor is hot lower #Ilim, enable the thermistor; a gimbal motor held against a load at its current limit heats quickly
    it stops with a Vbus error when decelerating the supply cannot absorb the returned energy; a different supply, or a brake resistor
    it stops with a velocity error the measured speed passed the fixed limit of 20,000,000 ticks/s: an encoder fault or a slipping magnet, rarely a real runaway; #Vlimit does not change it
    it falls behind under a load the torque limit: raise #Ilim within the motor's rating, or a bigger motor
    it holds position with a sawtooth noise the position loop gain; the magnet distance

    Low-quality motors¶

    Motors with visibly bad windings, an uneven back-EMF or a resistance that differs between the phases calibrate poorly and run rough. The tested motors are known good.

    The magnet and the encoder¶

    • The distance is 1 mm. "Magnet Good" from the magnet test does not mean the distance is right, only that the field is strong enough.
    • A magnet touching the chip can destroy the microcontroller; a magnet too far gives a noisy, wrong position.
    • The magnet must be diametrically magnetised and centred on the shaft.

    The driver gets hot or shuts down¶

    The MOSFET driver stage shuts down on its own overtemperature or overcurrent and reports a driver error. Lower the current limit, give the board airflow, check the motor leads for a short. Near 2.8 A the driver needs cooling.

    After a firmware update¶

    #Info shows the new release. If the values look wrong or the LED stays on: #Default, #Cal, #Save. A release note says when an update clears the calibration. After an update to 109, calibrate again (#Cal) where the motor can turn freely; on an assembled SSG-48 apply its preset instead. Upgrading from older firmware says why and what else changes.

    A Vbus error right after power-up, cleared by #Clear, was the supply ramping up; from firmware 109 a low supply is not checked in the first 500 ms.

    Getting help¶

    Support says where to ask and what to include; #Info's output is the first thing.

    Known issues¶

    The faults in the Spectral Micro firmware that are known and not yet fixed, and what to do until they are. The pages that describe each part say the same where it matters; this page collects them in one place. Which releases work together is in Versions and compatibility.

    Spectral Micro firmware¶

    Seen in: 110.

    What happens What to do More
    KI-3 The serial number is not read back from the EEPROM at power-up, so #SERNUM, Motor GUI and Send_Respond_Device_Info report 1, and every #Save writes that 1 over the stored number. Tell drivers apart by their CAN node ID. To keep a serial number stored, set it with #SERNUM before every #Save. Saving: what is not saved
    KI-4 For anyone reading or changing the source, the start values in common.h are not the factory defaults (the gripper flag starts at 1, the pole pairs at 11, Max_Vbus at 29500 mV). The EEPROM replaces them at power-up, so a running driver does not show them. Take the defaults from the Defaults page, which lists what #Default writes, or from Set_Default_config() in EEPROM.cpp. Defaults
    KI-5 While the E-stop bit of the last Send_gripper_data_pack (or #Gripstop 1) is 1, the driver raises the gripper E-stop error again on every control cycle, so Send_Clear_Error or #Clear alone does not clear it. Send a gripper frame with the E-stop bit 0 (or #Gripstop 0) first, then clear the error. Gripper mode: moving
    KI-6 The answer to a 5-byte Send_gripper_data_pack goes out before the driver has acted on the command, so its object detection and error bits are those of the moment before, for example 3 (position reached) from the previous move. Judge the new move by the answers to later frames, or poll with an empty Send_gripper_data_pack. Gripper mode: moving
    KI-8 The action bit in Respond_Gripper_data_pack is a copy of the last command's action bit; the driver never clears it, so it stays 1 after the move has ended. Tell the end of a move by the object detection, 3 position reached, 1 or 2 stopped on an object. Respond_Gripper_data_pack
    KI-9 Open loop (#Openloop) puts the voltage #Openv (5000 mV by default) on the phases without a current limit; #Ilim does not apply, so a low-resistance motor draws far more than its limit. Lower #Openv before you run a low-resistance motor in open loop, and keep the test short. Control modes
    KI-10 The velocity error trips only above a fixed 20,000,000 ticks/s, about 1220 revolutions per second and far above the speeds the driver can follow; #Vlimit does not change it, so it does not catch a motor that runs away. Watch the speed in your host and send Send_Idle or Send_ESTOP when it is wrong. Errors and recovery
    KI-11 Only motion frames and answered status requests reset the watchdog. Setting frames (gains, limits, Idle, Clear) and UART commands do not, so a host that only configures the driver, or a terminal session with a saved watchdog, ends in a watchdog error. While the watchdog runs, send a status request such as Send_Respond_Encoder_data within every watchdog period. Before you work over UART alone, set the watchdog to 0 and save (#Par 6,0, then #Save). The watchdog
    KI-12 The current a turned motor drives through its shorted phases while the driver brakes in idle (#Brake 1) is not limited. Brake only a motor whose resistance keeps that current under 2.8 A at the speeds it is turned at, and do not let a load drive a braking motor fast. Braking in idle
    KI-14 Send_Respond_Device_Info carries the batch date in one byte, its lowest 8 bits, so a host reading it over CAN gets 120 for the 24092024 that #Default stores. Read the batch date with #Info over UART, which prints the whole number. Send_Respond_Device_Info
    KI-19 The encoder table holds a field current of 80 % of the current limit for about 20 s, also in the run that follows every #Cal. With a limit above the motor's rating (1000 mA after #Default), the run is above the rating too. Set #Ilim for the motor before #Encal and before every #Cal (calibration puts the limit that was in force back before the runs that follow). Encoder table and anti-cogging: running them

    Release 104¶

    Seen in: the image of the GitHub release V104.

    What happens What to do More

The image comes from commit 20765c9 on Bootloader_main, which adds Send_Voltage_Limit; the tag lacks it. Check out 20765c9 to see or rebuild the 104 image's source; better, update the board to the current release. Releases: the two branches

    Fixed¶

    What happened Fixed in More
    KI-1 The current limit of Send_Limits over CAN is used as sent, also above the driver's 2800 mA; only #Ilim over UART clamps it. 109: Send_Limits stops the current limit at 2800 mA and ignores a negative value Send_Limits
    KI-2 Calibration sets the current limit to 1600 mA and leaves it there, whatever it was before; a #Save right after calibration stores the 1600 mA. 109: calibration puts back the current limit that was in force when it started Calibration: current limit and save
    KI-7 A 5-byte Send_gripper_data_pack, Send_Clear_Error or #Clear during the gripper calibration stops it without a message and leaves its counters as they were, so the next calibration starts part way through; it may skip the opening or give up early. 110: a new gripper calibration starts from its first step, also after one that was stopped (a command during the calibration still stops it) Gripper mode: calibrating
    KI-13 #Save does not keep the idle behaviour: after every power-up the driver coasts in idle again, whatever #Brake or Send_Settings set before. 110: #Save keeps the idle behaviour (#Brake, Send_Settings, parameter 18) Braking in idle
    KI-15 #Error prints FAULT pin is HIGH while the driver stage reports a fault, which is when that pin is low: the text names the wrong level. 109: the line reads FAULT pin is LOW: the gate driver reports a fault Errors and recovery

    Something that is not here¶

    Check the symptom tables first. If it is still not explained, Report a problem says what to include so it can be reproduced. A fixed issue stays on this page, under "Fixed", with the release that fixes it.

    Appendix

    Firmware release notes¶

    What changed in each firmware release, from the release notes in the firmware repository, up to the release this site describes.

    There is no release 108 for the Spectral Micro: from 109 it shares its version numbers with STEPFOC, and goes from 107 straight to 109. Upgrading from older firmware says what a driver keeps and what to run when it moves to 109.

    V110 (2026-11-13)¶

    Spectral BLDC firmware V110 — application image for flashing via the Spectral bootloader. Mostly gripper work: an SSG-48 gripper (a Spectral Micro in gripper mode) now ends its moves cleanly and closes fully at position 255. Everything below is described in the manual: Spectral Micro docs; what a driver keeps and what to run after the update: Upgrading from older firmware.

    Grippers¶

    • A move ends cleanly. On 109 the current took over two seconds to settle after a move: the speed loop's integral was carried into the hold and unwound slowly through friction. Now the integral is dropped on arrival, the last stretch of a move slows down, and the hold has a small dead zone (a quarter of a position count). A few mA are left after a move.
    • A soft start. A move picks up speed over 0.1 s instead of starting at full speed.
    • Positions 0 and 255 clear the stops, and 255 is fully closed. On 109 the calibration read the ends while pushing at 500 mA, so 0 and 255 lay inside the stops: a full open or close pressed on the stop, drew the grip current for good and often reported an object. Each end is now read under a light 100 mA push (released to no current, the SSG-48's free-running jaws spring back about 1 mm off the stop); 0 sits 32 ticks inside the open stop and 255 16 ticks from the closed one. On an SSG-48 the jaws stop 0.12 mm short of fully closed at 255, and the calibration repeats within 4 ticks.
    • Object detection reports an object after the jaws have stood still against it for 50 ms.
    • The gripper pushes with its own current limit. On 109 every gripper command overwrote the driver's current limit (parameter 16) with its grip current, and a Save config then stored it.
    • The gripper calibration can be started again while it runs, ends on a timeout or a stroke under 1000 ticks, and puts the current limit back. The position is rounded to the nearest count (it was cut off).

    CAN bus¶

    • Parameters 90-93: 90 #Rstint, 91 the thermistor (#Term), 92 gripper mode (#Gripper, written only while idle), each 0 or 1 and saved by Save config, so a preset over CAN can set everything (issue #1); 93, read only, the gripper's stroke between positions 0 and 255 in ticks (0 until calibrated).

    UART¶

    • #Estop: the E-stop, as CAN has it. The motor is released, a running calibration ends, and the E-stop latches until #Clear.
    • #Close during a motor calibration stops the calibration (Close: the calibration is stopped). On 109 it switched the mode under the running calibration.

    Settings and safety¶

    • Brake or coast when idle (parameter 18) is saved by Save config. On 109 it was back to its default at every power-up. A driver that never saved it coasts, as before.
    • Watchdog: a trip while the driver is idle no longer starts the hold or the descend (on 109 it did, for the whole fallback time). A trip while a loop is closed holds as before.

    Compatibility¶

    • The settings and the motor calibration of 109 are kept. The gripper calibration is kept in RAM, as always: calibrate the gripper after the update.
    • Python library 1.22 (pip install --upgrade Spectral-BLDC) names parameters 90-93; 1.21 reads them by number. Motor GUI 0.7.0 uses what 110 adds: STOP over UART sends #Estop, brake or coast counts as saved, a preset over CAN writes 90-92.
    • Bench-tested for this release on an SSG-48 gripper: the moves and the hold, moves of one count, the ends and the closed gap, the soft start, gripping objects, the calibration's repeatability, parameters 90-93, brake or coast over a save and a restart, the driver's current limit through gripper moves, the watchdog while idle and in a closed loop, an aborted gripper calibration. #Estop and #Close were tried over UART on the STEPFOC, which has the same code. Not tested on the bench: an upgrade from V103 with the ST-Link, and a downgrade.

    Assets¶

    • spectral-bldc-firmware-V110.bin — application firmware image (flash at 0x08003000, target STM32F103CB).
    • firmware.json — manifest with version, size, and SHA-256 for download verification by the web flasher.

    Integrity¶

    • SHA-256: eae54f56b4d0eb3677155447a5ce68156bfbda16e81534513ad71cb0dbbbfb21
    • Size: 112984 bytes

    Built from commit 38d210d on Bootloader_main (#Info shows it as Build: 38D210D). Includes bootloader entry support (CAN/UART reset-into-bootloader).

    V109 (2026-11-10)¶

    Spectral Micro BLDC firmware V109 — application image for flashing via the Spectral bootloader. From 109 the Spectral Micro and the STEPFOC share their version numbers, so there is no 108: 109 follows 107. Everything below is described in the manual: Spectral Micro docs; what a driver keeps and what to run after the update: Upgrading from older firmware.

    Phase B (the main fix)¶

    • In every release up to 107 the gate driver's phase-B enable input was fed by the microcontroller's 8 MHz clock output instead of a steady level, so phase B could drop out: weaker or uneven torque, a calibration that failed with a wrong pole-pair count, or a motor that took a current command and did not move, with no error. 109 drives the pin properly. On the bench: 5 of 5 calibrations and full torque on every test, also over 10 restarts.
    • The calibration now reports the true phase resistance and inductance (on the reference gimbal motor about 7.0-7.2 Ω and 1.75-1.9 mH, where 107 reported about 6.4 Ω and 1.25 mH); the old × 1.25 correction is gone.
    • After the update: a calibration made with 107 keeps working. Calibrate again (#Cal) where the motor can turn freely, so the current gains follow the true values. An assembled SSG-48 gripper keeps its calibration: apply Motor GUI's SSG-48 preset instead, with the gripper's CAN ID set first.

    New features¶

    • Parameters: any setting or read-out by number over CAN (commands 36-38) or #Par (parameters 1-89). Identity (39) and telemetry (40) frames.
    • Capture, histogram and buffers (41-43).
    • Watchdog actions: idle, brake, coast, hold the position, or descend at a capped speed, with a fallback time.
    • Current loop from R, L and one bandwidth (parameter 49, default 160 Hz; the SSG-48 preset uses 300 Hz). Measured on the bench and on by default: the direct PLL speed estimate at 100 Hz and commutation delay compensation of 234 µs (a speed step overshoots about 18 % instead of 63 %).
    • Encoder table and anti-cogging measured on the driver: #Cal runs the calibration, then both, and saves; #Calonly is the calibration alone.
    • Sound through the motor: tones, a queue of notes and samples (CAN 44 and 45, #Tone).
    • Identification: #Info and parameters 84-87 report the build (git commit), the board ID and a calibration ID. Parameters 88 and 89 are an experimental current-sampling diagnostic, off by default.
    • Settings in blocks with their own checksums; a reset keeps the CAN ID; a product lock refuses a STEPFOC image on a Spectral board.

    Calibration¶

    • Both current sensors are averaged; pole pairs 1-49; a rotor that does not follow the open-loop field ends with Open loop: Failed and two hint lines; an inductance that gives no gains fails the calibration instead of hanging.
    • Kt and KV are measured in four spins alternating direction; a warning only when two same-direction spins differ by more than 5 %.
    • The gate driver stays awake for the whole calibration. A failed calibration switches the bridge off at once and enables all three phases again. An E-stop ends a calibration, and #Cal is refused while one is latched. Calibrating many times without a power cycle no longer uses up memory.

    Safety¶

    • The control loop keeps the gate driver awake in every driving mode: a command arriving at the wrong moment could leave it asleep, with no torque and no error, until the next idle.
    • Limits: a negative or not-a-number value is ignored by the legacy CAN frames (limits, gains, Kt, voltage limit 0-60000 mV); #Ilim and #Vlimit take the magnitude; the current of a gripper move stays within 0-2800 mA; #PP and parameter 78 take 1-49 pole pairs, only while idle (50 and more would read as a STEPFOC calibration at the next start). PWM duties stay between 0 and 100 %.
    • The current-sense zero waits for the motor supply: started on USB before the supply, the driver used a zero about 100 mA off. It now says so in #Info and measures the zero by itself once the supply is there.
    • #Error says FAULT pin is LOW: the gate driver reports a fault (the text said HIGH). No undervoltage error while the supply ramps up at power-up.

    CAN bus¶

    • A gripper poll right after a new move command no longer cancels it as new: object detection reports contact again (it read "at position" while pressing on an object). The same in every release before 109.
    • The current and PD frames set their setpoint and mode together. Length codes above 8 are read as 8; extended (29-bit) frames are ignored.
    • Watchdog: setting its time or a save starts a fresh window; a negative time means off; hold and descend no longer use the last moving loop's delay compensation.

    Bug fixes¶

    • After #Cal, a host that takes the driver within 3 s drops the encoder-table and anti-cogging runs. #Encal and #Cogcal start only on their own words. While such a run turns the motor, a capture is not armed and the histogram is not changed.
    • A storage block whose check fails is read up to three times before its defaults are used. #Info says the anti-cogging is saved only while what is in RAM equals what is stored.
    • The sound queue and sample buffer no longer replay old content after a stop; a pitch the driver cannot play answers Tone: not played.

    Compatibility¶

    • Python library 1.21 or newer (pip install --upgrade Spectral-BLDC), Motor GUI 0.5.0.
    • Bench-tested for this release: calibration, current mode, restarts, the E-stop, the encoder table and anti-cogging, and the SSG-48 gripper on its 107 calibration. Not tested on the bench: an upgrade from V103 with the ST-Link, a downgrade, and on the Spectral Micro the watchdog actions, the capture and the sound (the same code passed on the STEPFOC).

    Assets¶

    • spectral-bldc-firmware-V109.bin — application firmware image (flash at 0x08003000, target STM32F103CB).
    • firmware.json — manifest with version, size, and SHA-256 for download verification by the web flasher.

    Integrity¶

    • SHA-256: 96945eec70be12f0e74c6add5fd9fafd4054677e4ebf29d0380062c77a4f6d87
    • Size: 112420 bytes

    Built from commit b5cedd5 on Bootloader_main (#Info shows it as Build: B5CEDD5). Includes bootloader entry support (CAN/UART reset-into-bootloader).

    V107 (2026-08-26)¶

    Spectral BLDC firmware V107 — application image for flashing via the Spectral bootloader.

    CAN bus¶

    • Fixed IN_DATA_PACK_PD writing the commanded speed into Feedforward_speed instead of Velocity_setpoint — PD mode's KD (velocity-damping) term was being evaluated against a stale/zero setpoint, so it silently ignored the commanded speed.
    • Added a generic mask+value settings command (IN_SETTINGS) for CAN-unreachable boolean settings, starting with idle-mode brake/coast selection.

    Usability¶

    • Idle mode can now be set to brake (driver stays enabled, phases held shorted via a zero-voltage PWM vector) or coast (Hi-Z, previous/default behavior), via IN_SETTINGS over CAN or #Brake <0|1> over UART.

    Calibration¶

    • Fixed #Cal spuriously failing with "Commutation/alignment check failed!" on some individual motor units. The pre-check that confirms the motor responds to a current command was driving a fixed voltage at the raw (uncalibrated) encoder angle; whether that produced torque depended on each unit's random magnet-to-encoder mounting angle. It now retries once at a 90-degree angle bias before failing.

    Assets¶

    • spectral-bldc-firmware-V107.bin — application firmware image (flash at 0x08003000, target STM32F103CB).
    • firmware.json — manifest with version, size, and SHA-256 for download verification by the web flasher.

    Integrity¶

    • SHA-256: 2ca1f2e3ad3ee7848cf47d8733b084be2daa87fc2e49f954dd41c76405d48ada
    • Size: 95404 bytes

    Built from commit 2e7febc on Bootloader_main.

    V106 (2026-07-06)¶

    Spectral BLDC firmware V106 — application image for flashing via the Spectral bootloader.

    CAN bus - The controller now drains up to 8 CAN messages per pass instead of just 1, so short bursts (multiple commands sent back-to-back, or a busy multi-node bus) no longer silently drop messages once the 3-deep hardware FIFO fills. - A CAN peripheral that fails to initialize no longer bricks the board — UART stays fully usable, only the CAN bus is skipped.

    Safety - Added a hardware watchdog (100ms). If the control loop ever stops running, the board resets instead of leaving the motor driven at a stale duty cycle. - Fixed a bug where Torque_mode could silently overwrite your commanded Iq setpoint when clamping to the current limit — reading it back after clamping now shows what you actually asked for, and raising the current limit afterward immediately restores the original commanded torque instead of requiring the command to be resent. - Fixed undefined behavior in a serial-protocol integer conversion (fourBytesToInt). - Synced default configuration values that had drifted between two locations in the code, so a fresh #Default and a freshly-flashed board's built-in defaults now agree.

    EEPROM / configuration integrity - Added CRC validation over the saved configuration, split into two independent halves: calibration data (resistance, inductance, Kt/KV, angle offset, current-loop gains, etc.) and settings (PID tuning, limits, watchdog config, CAN ID, etc.). - This means a corrupted save (e.g. from a power loss mid-write) is detected and safely defaulted instead of silently running with garbage values — and critically, a future firmware update that only adds a new setting can no longer wipe your calibration, since the two are validated independently. - Boards upgrading from older, pre-CRC firmware keep their existing calibration automatically — no need to recalibrate after this update. - A few unused slots were reserved in the calibration block for upcoming features (e.g. magnet alignment), so those can be added later without another migration.

    Performance - PWM output no longer re-resolves which timer/channel it's writing to on every single call (it was doing this 4x per control tick) — resolved once and cached, freeing up ISR headroom. - Velocity calculation switched from a floating-point divide to an integer multiply.

    Usability - New #Help command — prints a summary of the available commands. - Serial now responds with "Unknown command" for unrecognized input instead of silently ignoring it. - Calibration now prints progress during the angle-offset search (previously up to ~40 seconds of silence that could look like the board had hung). - #Info now always reports live ISR execution time, useful for checking control-loop headroom on the bench. - Fixed a leftover calibration-failure message that incorrectly suggested swapping motor phases — that failure mode no longer exists (reversed wiring has been auto-detected/compensated in software since V105); the message now accurately describes what actually happened (a commutation/current-response check, not a wiring-direction problem).

    Assets - spectral-bldc-firmware-V106.bin — application firmware image (flash at 0x08003000, target STM32F103CB). - firmware.json — manifest with version, size, and SHA-256 for download verification by the web flasher.

    Integrity - SHA-256: edb06ca112a7d24b0581e0b157a4524928067a46f8dc850ce22df8128092a758 - Size: 94908 bytes

    Built from commit e1f8611 on Bootloader_main.

    V105 (2026-07-05)¶

    Spectral BLDC firmware V105 — reversed-wiring auto-calibration

    #Cal now auto-detects reversed motor phase wiring during the open-loop spin and compensates it in software as a phase B<->C reflection, instead of failing and telling you to physically swap phases. Angle-offset calibration is now found automatically as part of #Cal itself (deterministic rotor-lock + verify + symmetry-sweep), so the standalone #Calangle command has been removed. #Info now reports the true firmware version instead of a stale EEPROM value. Existing calibrated units are unaffected and do not need #Default.

    Assets - spectral-bldc-firmware-V105.bin — application firmware image (flash at 0x08003000, target STM32F103CB). - firmware.json — manifest with version, size, and SHA-256 for download verification by the web flasher.

    Integrity - SHA-256: 0bd80ea038b8497fca8ab6a613b083d28e3473b360fa152b512b8ab0304dedb5 - Size: 91732 bytes

    Built from commit b7dded3 on Bootloader_main. Includes reversed-wiring auto-calibration (commutation_dir detection + phase B<->C reflection) and deterministic rotor-lock angle-offset calibration.

    Versions and compatibility¶

    Which firmware release works with which bootloader, Python library, Motor GUI, PAR6 runtime and board revision. One row per firmware release, newest first.

    Firmware Released Bootloader STEPFOC equivalent Python library (Spectral-BLDC) Motor GUI PAR6 runtime Spectral Micro PCB Docs
    110 2026-11-13 required 110 1.22 or newer (names for parameters 90 to 93; 1.21 reads them by their numbers) 0.7.0 or newer (STOP sends #Estop over UART, presets write parameters 90 to 92 over CAN) 0.9 or newer (it sends watchdog action 0, the legacy response) v2.0 110
    109 2026-11-10 required 109 (from 109 the two share their version numbers; there is no Spectral Micro 108) 1.21 or newer (the calls for the commands added in 109: parameters, identity, telemetry, buffers, the watchdog's coast and descend) 0.5.0 or newer 0.9 or newer (it sends watchdog action 0, the legacy response) v2.0 109
    107 2026-08-26 required 108 (STEPFOC 107 has the Send_Settings and PD changes of this release, without its calibration fix) 1.20 or newer 0.9 or newer v2.0 107
    106 2026-07-06 required 106 1.19 or newer 0.9 or newer v2.0 not published
    105 2026-07-05 required 105 1.19 or newer 0.9 or newer v2.0 not published
    104 2026-06-19 required (the first release built for the bootloader, from Bootloader_main) none (STEPFOC has no 104 release) 1.19 or newer (1.20 for Send_Voltage_Limit) 0.9 or newer v2.0 101 applies; 104 adds Send_Respond_Kt, Send_Voltage_Limit (in the release image) and the bootloader
    101 2025-09-29 none (flashed with a programming adapter) 103 1.17 or newer 0.9 or newer v2.0 101

    #Info prints the firmware release a driver runs; Send_Respond_Device_Info returns it over CAN.

    Hardware revisions¶

    What changed in each revision of the Spectral Micro board, and which firmware and which revision of this manual go with it. The board reports its hardware revision in #Info (Hardware version) and in Send_Respond_Device_Info; the silkscreen on the bottom of the board carries the board's name and date.

    Revision Marked Changes Firmware Manual
    v2.0 Spectral motor controller micro v2.0, 10.1.2024 the production board 101 and newer; 104 and newer run on the bootloader this manual

    A board that runs firmware 101 or older has no bootloader; Installing the bootloader on an older board brings it to the current firmware.

    Theory corner¶

    The control concepts behind the driver's modes, for readers who want to know why a setting does what it does. Nothing here is needed to use the driver; the chapters say what to type.

    Field-oriented control of a brushless motor¶

    A brushless motor has three stator windings and a rotor of permanent magnets with \(p\) pole pairs: one mechanical revolution is \(p\) electrical cycles, 11 for a typical 5208 gimbal motor.

    The torque the rotor receives depends on the angle between the stator field and the rotor field, the load angle; it is largest when the stator field stands a quarter of an electrical cycle ahead of the rotor and zero when the two are aligned.

    A hobby ESC does not measure the rotor angle: it steps the field around in six fixed states and infers the rotor's position from the back-EMF of the winding it is not driving, which works at speed and fails at standstill. That is why an ESC cannot hold a position or produce a controlled torque at low speed.

    Field-oriented control (FOC) measures the rotor angle instead. The encoder on the motor shaft gives the mechanical angle \(\theta\); with the pole pairs \(p\) and the angle offset \(\theta_0\) found by calibration, the electrical angle is \(\theta_e = p\,\theta + \theta_0\).

    The three phase currents, which sum to zero, are reduced to two (the Clarke transform) and then expressed in the rotor's frame of reference (the Park transform):

    \[ I_\alpha = I_U,\qquad I_\beta = \frac{I_U + 2\,I_V}{\sqrt{3}} \]
    \[ I_d = I_\alpha \cos\theta_e + I_\beta \sin\theta_e,\qquad I_q = -I_\alpha \sin\theta_e + I_\beta \cos\theta_e \]

    \(I_q\), the quadrature current, is the part of the current that stands at right angles to the rotor field and produces torque: \(\tau = K_t\, I_q\). \(I_d\), the direct current, is aligned with the field and produces none. Two PI controllers regulate \(I_q\) to the torque demand and \(I_d\) to zero by choosing the voltages \(U_q\) and \(U_d\).

    The inverse transforms turn those into three phase voltages, applied by PWM at 25 kHz with space-vector modulation, which gets about 15 % more phase voltage out of the supply than a plain sine would. The loop runs at 5 kHz.

    The consequences are what makes a FOC drive behave like a servo:

    • the current is only what the torque needs, so the motor runs cool and quiet;
    • the field is always placed where it produces the most torque per ampere;
    • the motor produces its full torque at standstill and holds a position;
    • the torque is known from the current, which is what makes impedance control and force-limited motion possible.

    Calibration supplies the constants this needs. The resistance \(R\) and inductance \(L\) of a winding are measured. The current loop gains follow from them and the chosen bandwidth \(\omega_c = 2\pi f_c\) (#Cbw): \(K_p = L\,\omega_c\) and \(K_i = R\,\omega_c\) cancel the winding's electrical time constant and give a first-order response at \(f_c\).

    The driver stores them in the form its 5 kHz loop uses: Kpiq is close to \(L\,\omega_c\), and Kiiq is \(R\,\omega_c T\), with \(T\) the 200 µs control period. The pole pairs come from an open-loop spin: the field is turned a known number of electrical cycles and the encoder says how far the rotor went.

    The angle offset is found by driving a current along the \(d\) axis and reading where the rotor settles. The velocity constant \(K_V\) comes from the back-EMF the spinning motor generates, and the torque constant follows from it: in SI units \(K_t = 1/K_V\). The firmware computes \(K_t = 8.27/K_V\) Nm/A, with \(K_V\) in rpm/V.

    The cascade of loops¶

    Position, velocity and current are controlled by three loops nested inside one another:

    \[ \text{position loop: } \omega_d = K_{pp}\,(\theta_d - \theta) \qquad \text{velocity loop: } I_{q,d} = K_{pv}\,(\omega_d - \omega) + K_{iv}\!\int(\omega_d - \omega)\,dt \qquad \text{current loop: } U_q = \text{PI}(I_{q,d} - I_q) \]

    The position loop is proportional: its output is a speed demand, limited by the velocity limit. The velocity loop is a PI controller: its output is a current demand, limited by the current limit; the integral term removes the steady-state error a load would otherwise leave. The current loop is the innermost and fastest.

    Each loop must be slower than the one inside it, by a factor of five to ten, for the inner loop to look like an ideal actuator to the outer one.

    That is why the loops are tuned from the inside out, and why a current loop bandwidth of 160 Hz (the default from firmware 109) leaves room for a velocity loop of a few tens of hertz and a position loop below that.

    A command picks where the cascade is entered: a current setpoint uses the current loop alone, a speed setpoint the two inner loops, a position setpoint all three.

    The feedforward terms the CAN motion frames carry are added to the demands rather than computed from an error: a speed feedforward is added to the position loop's output, a current feedforward to the velocity loop's output.

    A host that knows the trajectory can command it directly and leave the loops to correct what is left, which gives a faster, smoother response than letting the loops produce the whole demand from the error.

    An integrator accumulates error over time, and keeps accumulating while the output is at its limit; the overshoot this causes when the limit is released is windup. The velocity loop's integrator is clamped to the current limit, and #Rstint resets the integrators on every new setpoint, which suits a host that sends one setpoint after another in large steps.

    Impedance control¶

    Impedance control does not prescribe a motion or a force. It prescribes the relation between the two: how much force the motor produces when it is displaced from its setpoint and how fast. The driver's PD mode implements a spring and a damper around the setpoint:

    \[ \tau = K_P\,(\theta_d - \theta) + K_D\,(\dot\theta_d - \dot\theta) + K_t\, I_{ff} \]

    with the stiffness \(K_P\) in Nm/rad, the damping \(K_D\) in Nm·s/rad, and a current feedforward \(I_{ff}\) for a known load such as gravity. The torque is produced by the current loop (\(I_q = \tau / K_t\)), which is what makes FOC a prerequisite: a drive without the rotor angle cannot produce a commanded torque at low speed.

    The driver computes the same loop in its own units. Its KP is in mA per encoder tick and its KD in mA per tick/s (0.14 and 0.0028 by default), and the result is a current in mA.

    The stiffness in Nm/rad is KP × \(K_t\) × 2.608, and the damping in Nm·s/rad is KD × \(K_t\) × 2.608 (16384 ticks per turn, 1000 mA per A).

    Pushed off its setpoint by an external force \(F\), the motor yields by \(\Delta\theta = \tau_{ext}/K_P\) and pushes back with that force; let go, it returns, with the damper settling the motion. A small \(K_P\) makes a soft joint, a large one a stiff joint; \(K_D\) without \(K_P\) is a pure damper.

    The setpoint can move, in which case the virtual spring drags the rotor along a trajectory with a bounded force — the property legged robots, hand-guided arms and anything that touches people or an uncertain environment need.

    The impedance loop is also what a position loop is not: the position loop's integral action removes any steady-state deflection and so makes the joint as stiff as the current limit allows, which is the right thing for a machine tool and the wrong thing for a leg.

    The full impedance model includes an inertia term, \(M\ddot\theta\); the driver, like most joint controllers, leaves it out, so the apparent inertia is the motor's own.

    Admittance control¶

    Admittance is the inverse relation: the controller measures the force and commands a motion,

    \[ \dot\theta_d = \frac{\tau_{ext} - K\,(\theta - \theta_0)}{B} \]

    on top of a stiff position controller. It is the natural form for a machine that has a force sensor and a position loop that cannot be made soft, such as an industrial arm; impedance control is the natural form for a machine whose actuator is a torque source, such as a FOC motor.

    The driver has no force sensor and implements impedance; a host that wants admittance behaviour can estimate the external torque from \(K_t I_q\) minus the known load and command positions accordingly, with the limits of that estimate (friction, cogging, the current loop's accuracy).

    Compliance and stiffness¶

    Stiffness \(K\) is the force per unit of displacement; compliance is its inverse, \(C = 1/K\), the displacement per unit of force. Passive compliance is in the mechanism — a spring, a flexible finger, a belt; active compliance is in the controller — the impedance loop above.

    The driver offers three points on that scale: the position mode, stiff up to the current limit; the PD mode, as stiff as \(K_P\); and the current mode, which is no stiffness at all, a torque source the host shapes as it likes.

    The current limit #Ilim is a force limit in every mode: the largest torque the motor will produce before it yields, whatever the loops ask. Set it for the motor's rating, and for the mechanism's.

    Which mode¶

    Mode Behaviour under an external force Use
    Position holds the position up to the current limit, then yields positioning, machines, anything that must not deflect
    Velocity holds the speed up to the current limit wheels, conveyors, spindles
    PD (impedance) deflects in proportion to the force, returns when released legs, arms that touch things or people, hand guiding, teleoperation
    Current (torque) produces the commanded torque, moves as the load dictates a host that closes its own loop, gravity compensation, force-limited pressing

    Resources

    Glossary¶

    Activated
    Two flags with the same name. The controller's flag is set by #Activate and reported in Send_Respond_State_of_Errors; nothing else uses it. The gripper's activate bit is set by #Gripact, #Grippos and Send_gripper_data_pack; in gripper mode a move is carried out only while it is set.
    Angle offset (theta offset)
    The angle between the encoder's zero and the motor's electrical zero, found by calibration and stored. The driver needs it to place the current vector relative to the rotor.
    Anti-cogging
    A current added to the current setpoint, a function of the electrical angle, that cancels the motor's own torque ripple (the pull of its magnets towards the stator's teeth and the ripple of current-sensing errors). Identified on the driver (#Cogcal, parameter 43; firmware 109 and newer).
    Back-EMF
    The voltage a turning motor generates in its own windings, proportional to speed. It opposes the supply, limits the top speed at a given voltage, and is what calibration measures to find KV, Kt and the flux linkage.
    Board ID
    The bootloader's own address, 0 to 13, stored in its configuration page. The firmware sets it to the CAN node ID when the configuration is saved. The Board ID line of #Info (parameter 86, firmware 109 and newer) is another thing: 8 hex digits from the chip's unique number, which never change.
    Bootloader
    The program in the first 12 KB of the driver's flash on boards that run firmware 104 or newer. It starts the firmware, and loads a new firmware over UART or CAN. See The bootloader.
    Calibration
    The routine (#Cal) that checks the magnet and the supply, measures the motor's resistance and inductance, computes the current loop gains, spins the motor in open loop to find the pole pairs and the phase order, aligns the encoder to the rotor field and measures Kt, KV and the flux linkage. Its result is kept with #Save.
    CAN ID (node ID)
    The address of the driver on the CAN bus, 0 to 13 (the field holds 0 to 15, but the bootloader reserves 14 and 15). It occupies bits 10 to 7 of the 11-bit CAN frame identifier; the command ID occupies bits 6 to 1 and the error bit is bit 0.
    CANvas
    Source Robotics' USB-to-CAN adapter. It appears as a serial port and speaks slcan, which python-can and Motor GUI use.
    Clarke and Park transforms
    The two steps that turn the three phase currents into the two components FOC regulates: Clarke reduces the three phases to two axes fixed to the stator, Park turns those into the frame that rotates with the rotor (the d-q frame).
    Command ID
    The number, 0 to 63, in bits 6 to 1 of a CAN frame identifier that says which message the frame carries. The API reference lists every command ID.
    Commutation
    Placing the stator current so that it produces torque at the rotor's present angle. FOC does it continuously from the encoder angle and the angle offset; a hobby ESC does it in six steps from the back-EMF, which fails at standstill.
    Commutation delay compensation (parameter 64)
    Turning the output voltage forward by the angle the rotor covers between the angle reading and the moment the voltage reaches the motor. 234 µs on the Spectral Micro by default.
    Control mode
    What the driver is doing with the motor: idle, position, velocity, current, PD (impedance), voltage torque, open loop, gripper, or calibration. A command that carries a setpoint puts the driver in the matching mode.
    Current loop bandwidth (#Cbw, parameter 49)
    How fast the current loop follows a change of its setpoint, in Hz. The current loop gains follow from it and the motor's resistance and inductance: Kpiq ≈ ω·L, Kiiq = ω·R·T (T the 200 µs control period). 160 Hz by default from firmware 109 (500 Hz before).
    Current-sense zero
    The reading of a current sensor with no current flowing. From firmware 109 the driver measures it at every start-up and subtracts it.
    Daisy chain
    Several drivers on one CAN bus and one supply, each driver's second CAN and power connector feeding the next.
    d-q frame
    The rotating frame of reference FOC works in: the q axis is at right angles to the rotor field and the current on it (Iq) produces torque; the d axis is along the field and its current (Id) produces none, so it is held at 0.
    Diametrical magnet
    A disc magnet whose poles are across its diameter, so the field turns with the shaft. The encoder needs this kind; an axially magnetised disc looks the same and does not work.
    EEPROM
    The non-volatile memory on the driver that holds the configuration and the calibration. #Save writes it, #Default restores the factory values.
    Encoder table
    A correction of the encoder's small angle error around the turn, measured on the driver (#Encal, parameter 52) and saved with the configuration (firmware 109 and newer).
    Encoder ticks
    The unit of position: the 14-bit magnetic encoder gives 16384 ticks per revolution of the motor shaft, counted over as many revolutions as the motor makes. Speed is in ticks per second.
    Error bit
    Bit 0 of every CAN frame identifier the driver sends: 1 while any error is active.
    Feedforward
    A term added to a loop's output from what is known in advance rather than from the error: the motion frames carry a speed and a current feedforward so a host that knows the trajectory can command it directly, leaving the loops to correct the rest.
    Field-oriented control (FOC)
    Controlling a motor by regulating the stator current as a vector in the rotor's frame, from a measurement of the rotor angle. The three phase currents are reduced to a torque-producing and a field-producing component, each regulated by its own loop, which gives smooth torque at any speed down to standstill, a known position, and the most torque per ampere.
    Firmware release
    One build of the firmware, numbered 101, 104, 105, … and tagged v101, V104, V105, … in the firmware repository. #Info prints the release a driver runs. This documentation describes one release; the version selector switches between them.
    Flux linkage
    The rotor's magnetic flux as seen by a winding, in weber; found by calibration from the back-EMF, together with KV and Kt.
    Gimbal motor
    A brushless motor wound with many turns of thin wire for low speed and smooth torque at low current, originally for camera gimbals: a phase resistance of a few ohms to a few tens of ohms, and many pole pairs. The kind of motor the Spectral Micro is made for.
    Gripper mode
    The driver running a gripper (#Gripper 1): jaw positions from 0 (open) to 255 (closed), a speed, a current limit that becomes the grip force, and object detection.
    Heartbeat
    A frame (Respond_Heartbeat) the driver sends at a set interval so the host knows it is alive. Off by default.
    Iq, Id
    The two components of the motor current in the d-q frame: Iq produces torque, Id is normally held at 0. The current mode's setpoint and limit are Iq values.
    Impedance (PD) mode
    The control mode in which the driver behaves like a spring and a damper around a position setpoint, with the stiffness KP and the damping KD, plus a current feedforward.
    Kt, KV
    The motor's torque constant (torque per ampere) and velocity constant (speed per volt), found by calibration. Kt turns a current into an estimated torque; KV gives the top speed at a supply voltage.
    Motor GUI
    Source Robotics' browser tool for the driver: setup, calibration, tuning, plots and firmware updates over a serial or CAN adapter.
    Open loop
    Turning the stator field at a set electrical speed without the encoder in the loop, so that the rotor follows it like a stepper: #Openloop, and the open-loop spin of the calibration that finds the pole pairs.
    Parameter
    A setting reached by its number through Send_Parameter and Send_Get_Parameter (CAN 36 to 38) or #Par (firmware 109 and newer). The numbers are permanent.
    Phase order
    The order in which the motor's three phases are connected to U, V and W. Firmware 105 and newer learns it during calibration and compensates it; firmware 101 asks for two wires to be swapped when it is reversed.
    PIN (ADDITIONAL)
    The spare pin of the driver, on the three-pad header next to the UART connector, usable as an analog input or a digital input/output. The firmware's name for it is ADDITIONAL.
    PLL (speed estimate)
    A phase-locked loop that follows the encoder count and gives the speed with less lag than a moving average (parameter 50, firmware 109 and newer; the direct PLL at 100 Hz is the default).
    Pole pairs
    The number of electrical cycles per mechanical revolution, half the number of rotor magnets: 7 for a typical 2804 gimbal motor, 11 for a 5208, 14 for an 8108. Calibration finds it; #PP sets it by hand.
    Product lock
    The stamp in the EEPROM of the product that calibrated a board. A firmware that finds the other product's stamp drives nothing (firmware 109 and newer).
    Remote frame
    A CAN frame with the RTR bit set and no data: a request. The host asks for data with a remote frame under a command ID, and the driver answers with a data frame under the same ID.
    Sound (#Tone, CAN 44 and 45)
    Tones, a queue of notes and recorded samples the driver adds to its output voltage while it holds or moves, so the motor plays them (firmware 109 and newer).
    STEPFOC
    Source Robotics' stepper driver, the Spectral Micro's sister: similar hardware and firmware and practically the same commands, for two-phase stepper motors.
    Termination
    The 120 Ω resistor the first and the last node of a CAN bus need. The switch on the driver turns it on.
    Tool id (#Tool, parameter 1)
    What a drive is built into: 0 none, 1 SSG-48, 11 to 33 an MSG, 200 to 254 a customer's tool.
    Vbus
    The supply voltage at the driver, measured and printed in mV. Outside the limits the driver raises a Vbus error.
    Watchdog
    A timer the driver resets with every motion command and status request it receives over CAN. When it is set (Send_Watchdog) and runs out, the driver reports a watchdog error and stops; from firmware 109 it holds, brakes, coasts or descends as the watchdog's action says.

    API reference

    API reference¶

    Every CAN message and every UART command of the firmware release this site describes, the factory defaults of its settings and which of them are saved, the error flags, and the map from the Python library's calls to the messages they send.

    Where the facts come from¶

    The command IDs, the frame lengths the firmware accepts, the responses it sends, the list of UART commands with their kind (Set/Get, Get or action), their argument type, the mode each enters and what it prints, the parameter numbers, and the defaults are read from the firmware's source at its release tag by tools/sync_firmware.py in the documentation repository, and the pages are built from that at every build. The descriptions, units and byte layouts are written by hand, and the build fails when they name a command the firmware does not have, or miss one it has, or when a byte layout does not fit the frame. The firmware version is in the version box on the landing page.

    Conventions¶

    • Bytes are numbered from 0, the first byte on the bus (data[0]). Multi-byte values are big-endian: the most significant byte first. Floats are IEEE 754 single precision in 4 bytes.
    • Bits inside a byte are numbered from the most significant bit: bit 7 is the first flag the firmware packs into the byte, bit 0 the last.
    • Encoder ticks: 16384 per revolution; speeds in ticks per second; currents in mA; voltages in mV.
    • UART command names are case-sensitive and spelled exactly as the firmware compares them.

    In the API reference¶

    • CAN protocol: the identifier, the three kinds of message, every message by group, and every parameter
    • UART commands: the syntax and every command by group
    • Defaults
    • Errors and LED states
    • Python library: library call → message

    CAN frame tool and downloads¶

    Build or inspect a frame using the command definitions of this manual's firmware release. All calculations run in your browser; the tool does not connect to an adapter or send commands to hardware.

    Downloads¶

    Command definitions (JSON) · Position DBC · Velocity DBC · Current DBC

    The JSON includes the firmware tag, source commit, message IDs, every documented layout and its bit order. The DBC files cover nodes 0 to 13, with both the clean and fault-bit identifiers of driver replies. Reserved commands are omitted. Values use the firmware's native units: ticks, ticks/s, mA and the units in the CAN reference.

    Choose the DBC for the Send_data_pack_1 layout your host sends:

    File Command layout
    Position DBC 8 bytes: position, speed feedforward, current feedforward
    Velocity DBC 5 bytes: speed, current feedforward
    Current DBC 2 bytes: current

    The three layouts share a CAN identifier. DBC cannot select a layout from the frame's length, so load the matching file; the browser decoder below handles all three lengths. Other message layouts are the same in each download.

    An RTR request uses the same identifier as its data reply. DBC describes the data reply; it does not represent the request's RTR flag or the empty gripper status poll. Both are supported by the browser tool.

    The gripper's two object-detection bits have an unusual order: bit 5 is the low bit, bit 4 the high bit. The DBC signal gripper_object_detection_raw retains the wire value and supplies text labels (raw 2 = object while closing, raw 1 = object while opening). The browser decoder converts that pair to the firmware's numeric status (1 = closing, 2 = opening).

    Try a frame¶

    The example 0x07A, data C8 1E 01 2C C0, is a command to gripper node 0: position 200, speed 30, current 300 mA, activate and action set, E-stop and release direction clear. These are an illustration of the encoding; the device's current and motion limits still apply. Select another message above the fields to build one of its documented forms. To inspect a reply, enter its identifier and data bytes below.

    CAN messages
                        
    
                    
    CAN protocol¶
    

    CAN 2.0, 1 Mbit/s, 11-bit identifiers. The identifier carries the node ID (bits 10 to 7, 0 to 15), the command ID
(bits 6 to 1, 0 to 63) and, in frames from the driver, the error bit (bit 0). Over CAN
explains how the pieces are used; this page lists them.
    

    Three kinds of message:
    

      

    • Send_… — the host sends data to the driver. A few are answered (the motion commands with
  Respond_data_pack_1, the gripper command with Respond_Gripper_data_pack); most are not.
      • 

    • Send_Respond_… — the host asks for data with a remote frame (RTR bit set, no data) under the command ID;
  the driver answers with a data frame under the same command ID.
      • 

    • Respond_… — frames only the driver sends.
      • 

    

    Multi-byte values are big-endian (data[0] first); floats are IEEE 754 single precision; bits inside a byte are
numbered from the most significant bit (bit 7 first).
